Decorated Local Systems and Character Varieties
Benedetta Facciotti, Marta Mazzocco, Nikita Nikolaev

Abstract
The focus of this paper is the study of the moduli space of representations of fundamental groupoids of surfaces Σ \Sigma with boundaries with values in G := G ​ L n ​ ( ℂ ) G:=GL_{n}(\mathbb{C}) . In absence of marked points on the boundary, this moduli space is realized in many equivalent ways: as the moduli space of linear local systems on Σ \Sigma , as the moduli space of representations of the fundamental groupoid Π 1 ​ ( Σ ) \Pi_{1}(\Sigma) , as the space of monodromy data and as character variety. By adding marked points to the boundary of Σ \Sigma in order to capture irregular singularities, the Betti moduli space has been generalized in several ways by many authors. Although it is clear that these different approaches describe essentially the same spaces of mathematical objects, exactly how they fit together has not yet been established. Motivated by the broader programme of establishing an explicit and conceptually coherent relationship between the existing approaches to the study of the decorated Betti moduli space, in this paper, we develop a categorical framework that allows for a systematic definition of the decorated Betti moduli spaces space, in the presence of higher order poles, designed to specialize to the different points of view encountered in the literature.



Introduction

The focus of this paper is the study of moduli spaces of representations of fundamental groupoids of
surfaces
\Sigma
with boundaries with values in
G:=GL_{n}(\mathbb{C})
. In absence of marked points on the boundary, this moduli space is realized in many equivalent ways: as the moduli space
\mathfrak{Loc}_{G}(\Sigma)
of linear local systems on
\Sigma
, as the moduli space
\mathfrak{Rep}_{G}\big(\Pi_{1}(\Sigma)\big)
of representations of the fundamental groupoid
\Pi_{1}(\Sigma)
, as the moduli space
\mathfrak{Rep}_{G}\big(\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p)\big)
of monodromy data and as character variety
\mathfrak{X}_{G}(\Sigma)
. We call all these moduli spaces
1
1

1

When we speak of a
moduli space
, we think of an appropriate moduli stack, but in almost every instance it is sufficient to restrict our attention to the underlying set of isomorphism classes.
collectively as
Betti moduli space
. Their equivalence can be stated as follows:

\text{$\mathfrak{Loc}_{G}(\Sigma)$}\cong\text{$\mathfrak{Rep}_{G}\big(\Pi_{1}(\Sigma)\big)$}\cong\text{$\mathfrak{Rep}_{G}\big(\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p)\big)$}\cong\text{$\mathfrak{X}_{G}(\Sigma)$}.

For convenience of the reader, we recall these definitions and the proof of these equivalences in Sections
1.2
,
1.3
and
1.4
.

By adding marked points to the boundary of
\Sigma
in order to capture irregular singularities, the Betti moduli space has been generalized in several ways by many authors as summarized below. Although it is clear that these different approaches describe essentially the same spaces of mathematical objects,
exactly how they fit together has not yet been established.
In this paper, we develop a categorical framework that allows for a clear and systematic definition of the
decorated Betti moduli spaces
designed to specialize to the different points of view encountered in the literature.

Let us briefly recall the generalizations considered in this paper.

The moduli space of linear local systems was enriched by adding filtrations at marked points implicitly in Deligne’s work on regular singular connections
[
13
]
. The systematic construction of moduli spaces of such filtered local systems was later carried out by Simpson in the context of parabolic structures
[
32
]
, and further extended to irregular singularities by Boalch. Building on this framework, Fock and Goncharov study special positive and decorated loci inside these moduli spaces, endowing them with cluster structures and giving rise to higher Teichmüller spaces
[
15
]
. The notion of moduli space of filtered
2
2

2

We note that in
[
15
]
the authors use the term framed instead of filtered.
was further generalized by Goncharov and Shen
[
20
]
who introduced the moduli space of pinnings, namely an enrichment of the moduli space of filtered
G
–local systems on
\Sigma
by adding decorations on some of the flags.
Note that the concept of
pinning
was essentially introduced by Chevalley and later formalised by Tits, Grothendieck, and Demazure in the study of reductive algebraic groups.
In the context of filtered local systems, pinnings were used by Fock-Goncharov
[
15
, §2.2]
and Goncharov-Shen who gave an alternative definition of
pinning

[
20
, Def 3.7, fn. 12]
.
If the group is adjoint (e.g.,
\operatorname{\mathsf{PGL}}(\mathbb{C})
but not
\operatorname{\mathsf{GL}}_{n}(\mathbb{C})
), then their definition of
pinning
is equivalent to the classical notion
[
20
, Lemma 3.30]
.

The moduli space of representations of the fundamental groupoid was generalized by Gual-tieri, Li and Pym in
[
21
]
exploting the theory of Lie groupoids and Lie algebroids which is naturally involved in the holomorphic case, in which the Betti moduli space is naturally homeomorphic to the de Rham moduli space of
holomorphic connections on a Riemann surface.
Indeed, any holomorphic connection on a Riemann surface
\Sigma
defines a representation of the space of vector fields
\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}
, which has a structure of a Lie algebroid.
A distinguished integration of such a Lie algebroid is the fundamental Lie groupoid
\Pi_{1}(\Sigma)
, whose representation corresponds to the parallel transport determined by the connection.
By Lie’s second theorem, representations of
\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}
and
\Pi_{1}(\Sigma)
are equivalent.
What they showed is that this construction can be naturally extended to the case of higher order poles by incorporating the effective divisor
D
encoding the poles and their multiplicities.
Hence, as meromorphic connections are representations of the Lie algebroid
\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}(-D)
of vector fields vanishing along
D
, Lie’s second theorem implies they are equivalent to representations of the integrating Lie groupoid
\Pi_{1}(\Sigma,D)
.

The moduli space of monodromy data was generalized by Boalch in
[
6
]
, where he introduced a moduli space that was later called
wild character variety
in
[
7
]
.
Topologically, instead of the original Riemann surface, one considers its real oriented blow-up at the poles of the connection.
Additional points are then removed at the intersections of circles in tubular neighborhoods around the boundaries created by higher-order poles with the so-called Stokes directions.
Algebraically, rather than using the usual notion of representation, the construction employs Stokes representations, defined in
[
6
]
.
The moduli space is then defined taking the quotient by a specific action of the centralizer of the irregular type defined around each pole.

Finally, in
[
11
]
, Chekhov, Mazzocco and Rubtsov defined the so called
decorated character variety
, now renamed
bordered cusped character variety
.
The construction of this moduli space involves some topological modifications to the starting surface, based on
[
18
]
.
More precisely, while one still needs to consider the real oriented blow-up of the surface at the poles, the approach here differs in that, instead of introducing additional punctures, one designates marked points on the boundaries arising from higher order poles only.
Treating the marked points as cusps, the authors assign a Borel subgroup to each of them.
The bordered cusped character variety is then the quotient of the representation space of the groupoid of arcs with base points at the cusps by the action by mixed conjugation (see
subsection 6.1
) of the unipotent radical of the Borel subgorups associated to the cusps.
This approach was then generalized to the
n
dimensional case in
[
9
]
.

This paper is motivated by the broader programme of establishing an explicit and conceptually coherent relationship between all the above existing approaches to the study of the decorated Betti moduli space. As a concrete contribution to this programme, we develop a categorical framework that allows for a systematic definition of the Betti moduli space in the presence of higher order poles.
This viewpoint is also compatible with the Riemann–Hilbert correspondence, whose emphasis on the classification of geometric and algebraic objects makes a categorical perspective natural.
The first key step of our approach is therefore the introduction of suitable groupoids that accurately describe these structures.

In order to state the main result of this paper, we briefly introduce the notation for the relevant groupoids by providing a rough description of their objects, referring the reader to the appropriate sections for the precise definitions. A common feature of all these groupoids is that their objects carry certain decorations on the fibers/stalks over the marked points.

In this paper, we consider surfaces with marked boundary
(\Sigma,P)
, where
\Sigma
is a compact connected, oriented, smooth real two-dimensional
manifold with boundary components each of which is diffeomorphic to a circle, and
P\subset\partial\Sigma
is a finite set of points. These points carry some extra information as described in Definition
2.2
, but we neglect it in this introduction.

Following
[
15
]
,
a
filtered local system
on a surface with marked boundary
(\Sigma,P)
is a pair
({\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{F}})
consisting of a local system
{\mathcal{E}}
on
\Sigma
endowed with a collection
{\mathcal{F}}=\left\{{\mathcal{F}}_{p}:p\in P\right\}
where
{\mathcal{F}}_{p}
is a filtration near
p
(see Definition
3.1
). We denote the groupoid of
filtered local systems, and the corresponding moduli space, respectively by

\textup{Loc}_{G}^{\mathrm{Fi}}(\Sigma,P)\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\mathfrak{Loc}_{G}^{\mathrm{Fi}}(\Sigma,P)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\textup{Loc}_{G}^{\mathrm{Fi}}(\Sigma,P)\big/{\sim}.

Next, a
filtered representation
of the fundamental groupoid
\Pi_{1}(\Sigma)
of a surface with marked boundary
(\Sigma,P)
is a triple
(\mathnormal{E},\mathnormal{F},\Phi)
consisting of a representation
(\mathnormal{E},\Phi)
of the fundamental groupoid
\Pi_{1}(\Sigma)
endowed with a collection
\mathnormal{F}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\left\{\mathnormal{F}_{p}:p\in P\right\}
of filtrations on the fibres over the marked points which are flat in the sense specified in Definition
4.1
. We denote the groupoid of filtered representations of rank
n
on
(\Sigma,P)
with filtered isomorphisms, and the corresponding moduli space (see Definition
4.2
), by

\textup{Rep}_{G}^{\mathrm{Fi}}\big(\Pi_{1}(\Sigma),P\big)\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\mathfrak{Rep}_{G}^{\mathrm{Fi}}\big(\Pi_{1}(\Sigma),P\big)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\textup{Rep}_{G}^{\mathrm{Fi}}\big(\Pi_{1}(\Sigma),P\big)\big/{\sim}.

Thanks to the fact that the decorations arise only on marked points, the introduction of a discrete model for this groupoid is natural. Therefore, we introduce the groupoid of filtered representations of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)
of rank
n
, and the corresponding moduli space (see Definitions
4.12
and
4.13
) by

\textup{Rep}_{G}^{\mathrm{Fi}}\big(\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)\big)\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\mathfrak{Rep}_{G}^{\mathrm{Fi}}\big(\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)\big)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\textup{Rep}_{G}^{\mathrm{Fi}}\big(\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)\big)\big/{\sim}

respectively.

Finally we consider (see Definition

6.3
)
the
decorated representation variety
of
(\Sigma,P)
, namely the subset
R_{G}(\Sigma,P)
of
\operatorname{\mathsf{Hom}}\big(\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P),G\big)
consisting of all groupoid homomorphisms
\rho:\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)\to G
which are upper-triangular on loops based at marked points that lie on boundaries corresponding to simple poles. On this, we act by mixed conjugation by the affine algebraic subgroup of
G_{P}:=G^{|P|}
:

B_{P}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\prod_{p\in P}B=\operatorname{\mathsf{Aut}}(\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{n},\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{\bullet})

where
\mathop{\mathsf{Aut}}\nolimits(\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{n},\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{\bullet})
is the set of automorphisms of
\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
that preserve the standard flag
\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{\bullet}
.
In this way, we get an action groupoid on an affine algebraic variety:

B_{P}\ltimes R_{G}(\Sigma,P)\hskip 10.00002pt\text{with}\hskip 10.00002ptg.\rho\mathrel{\mathop{:}}={\mathrm{t}}^{\ast}g\cdot\rho\cdot{\mathrm{s}}^{\ast}g^{-1}.

Its moduli space (see Definition
6.8
)
is called
filtered character stack
and is defined as the following affine algebraic quotient stacks:

\mathfrak{X}^{\mathrm{Fi}}_{G}(\Sigma,P)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=R_{G}(\Sigma,P)/B_{P}.

Similarly, we can introduce framed (and projectively framed) analogues of all the above categories by framing every flag with a basis (or the equivalence class of a basis) and requiring all filtered isomorphisms to be unipotent (or projectively unipotent). We denote the corresponding groupoids and moduli spaces in the same way as above replacing the index
{\mathrm{Fi}}
by
{\mathrm{Fr}}
(or
{\mathrm{PFr}}
). Therefore we will speak of
decorated
groupoids when referring to both filtered and framed (or projectively framed) groupoids.

Establishing the equivalence of these groupoids yields, at the level of objects, a bijection between the underlying sets of the corresponding moduli spaces.

Theorem
.

For any surface with marked boundary
(\Sigma,P)
, there are canonical equivalences of categories between the groupoids of decorated local systems, decorated representations of the fundamental groupoid
\Pi_{1}(\Sigma)
, decorated representations of the discrete fundamental groupoid
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)
, and suitable mixed conjugation action groupoids over the decorated representation variety
R_{G}(\Sigma,P)
:

\textup{Loc}_{G}^{\square}(\Sigma,P)\cong\textup{Rep}_{G}^{\square}\big(\Pi_{1}(\Sigma),P\big)\cong\textup{Rep}_{G}^{\square}\big(\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)\big)\cong K_{P}^{\square}\ltimes R_{G}(\Sigma,P),

for any
\square\in\left\{{\mathrm{Fi}},{\mathrm{Fr}},{\mathrm{PFr}}\right\}
where
K^{\mathrm{Fi}}_{P}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=B_{P}
,
K^{\mathrm{Fr}}_{P}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=U_{P}
and
K^{\mathrm{Fr}}_{P}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=U_{P}^{\times}
(see (
6.11
)).
As a consequence, there are canonical bijections between the corresponding moduli sets::

\mathfrak{Loc}_{G}^{\square}(\Sigma,P)\cong\mathfrak{Rep}_{G}^{\square}\big(\Pi_{1}(\Sigma),P\big)\cong\mathfrak{Rep}_{G}^{\square}\big(\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)\big)\cong\mathfrak{X}_{G}^{\square}(\Sigma,P).

Since
\mathfrak{Loc}_{G}^{\mathrm{Fi}}(\Sigma,P)
carries a natural cluster variety structure
[
15
]
, this theorem shows that this cluster structure is intrinsic to the filtered Betti moduli space.

In the case when all the boundaries coming from simple poles are unmarked, i.e. when
P=P_{{\mathrm{I}}}
(see Definition
2.2
), and the group is
SL_{n}(\mathbb{C})
, the framed character variety is of the form
\mathfrak{X}^{\mathrm{Fr}}_{SL_{n}(\mathbb{C})}(\Sigma,P_{{\mathrm{I}}})=\operatorname{\mathsf{Hom}}\big(\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P_{{\mathrm{I}}}),SL_{n}(\mathbb{C})\big)/U_{P_{{\mathrm{I}}}}
. This was introduced by Chekhov, Mazzocco and Rubtsov in
[
11
, §4]
(who called it the
decorated character variety
) based on the work of Li-Bland and Ševera in
[
25
, §6]
.
These papers have served as one of the main inspirations for this project. In particular, we wanted to prove that
the Fock-Goncharov-Shen variables can be used to coordinatize any of the above moduli sets even when some decorations are forgotten - see Remark
6.10
. Motivated by this,
in Section
5
, we show that the map induced by the forgetful functor

\textup{Loc}_{G}^{{\mathrm{Fi}}}(\Sigma,P)\longrightarrow\textup{Loc}_{G}^{{\mathrm{Fi}}}(\Sigma,P_{\mathrm{I}})

is a ramified covering.

This paper is organized as follows. In Section
1
,
we give a careful treatment of the
classical setting of linear local systems on surfaces with boundary, representations of the fundamental groupoid, and the relationship between the corresponding character variety and the moduli space of monodromy data. In Section

2
we motivate and define surfaces with marked boundaries, and give a finite presentation for the fundamental groupoid
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)
. In Section
3
, we introduce various notions of local systems, which we collectively call
decorated local systems
, and we discuss the relationships between them. In Section
4
, we reformulate decorated local systems as
decorated
representations of the fundamental groupoid and the discrete fundamental groupoid. In Section
5
, we study the forgetful functors that drop the secondary marked points. In Section
6
, we give an explicit description of the moduli space of decorated local systems.

1
Review: Local Systems and Character Varieties

In this section, we set the stage by reviewing the classical setting of linear local systems on surfaces with boundary, representations of the fundamental groupoid, and the relationship between the corresponding character variety and the moduli space of monodromy data.
We give a rather careful treatment of the subject for the purpose of completeness as well as to introduce notation and recall basic concepts needed in the rest of the paper.

In what follows, we put
G\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\operatorname{\mathsf{GL}}_{n}(\mathbb{C})
for arbitrary
n
, and let
B
and
U
denote respectively the subgroups consisting of upper-triangular and unipotent upper-triangular matrices in
G
.
We also let
\Sigma
be any surface by which we mean a connected, oriented, topological real two-dimensional manifold with or without boundary.

1.1
Groupoids and Moduli Spaces

1.1.1
Groupoids

First, recall that a
groupoid
is a category in which every morphism is invertible.
To spell this out a little, a groupoid consists of a set of objects
X
and a set of morphisms
{\mathcal{G}}
together with several structure maps including a pair of maps
{\mathrm{s}},{\mathrm{t}}:{\mathcal{G}}\to X
called the
source
and
target maps
which respectively extract the source and target objects of a morphism.
We usually say that
{\mathcal{G}}
is a groupoid over
X
and write
{\mathcal{G}}\rightrightarrows X
.
There is also an embedding of
X
into
{\mathcal{G}}
as the subset of identity morphisms and we typically identify
X
with its embedded image inside
{\mathcal{G}}
.

A
Lie groupoid
is a groupoid
{\mathcal{G}}\rightrightarrows X
such that
{\mathcal{G}}
and
X
are topological manifolds (although
{\mathcal{G}}
may be non-Hausdorff), all the structure maps are continuous, the embedding
X\lhook\joinrel\rightarrow{\mathcal{G}}
is closed, and the source and target maps
{\mathrm{s}},{\mathrm{t}}
are submersions.
Similarly, we can define
holomorphic
and
algebraic
Lie groupoids.
A
groupoid homomorphism

\mathnormal{F}:{\mathcal{G}}\to{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime}
is simply a functor and a
Lie groupoid homomorphism
is a functor which is also a continuous (resp. holomorphic or algebraic) map.

1.1.2
Moduli spaces

Any groupoid
{\mathcal{G}}\rightrightarrows X
has a corresponding
moduli set
: it is the set of isomorphism classes of objects in
X
that we shall denote in any of the following ways:

\mathfrak{G}=X/{\mathcal{G}}=X/{\sim}.

If
x
is an object of
X
, we usually denote its isomorphism class by
[x]
.
If the groupoid
{\mathcal{G}}\rightrightarrows X
carries additional structure — such as continuous, smooth, analytic, or algebraic — then the moduli set
\mathfrak{G}
may be enriched to a
moduli stack
in the respective category.
However, these details will not play any appreciable role in our story.
So, from now on, when we speak of a
moduli space
, we think of an appropriate moduli stack, but in almost every instance it is sufficient to restrict our attention to the underlying moduli set.

1.1.3
Three examples of Lie groupoids

The first example of a Lie groupoid is as follows.
If
K\times X\to X
is a group action on a set
X
, then we can form the
action groupoid

{\mathcal{G}}=K\ltimes X\rightrightarrows X
.
Its set of morphisms is given by
K\times X
with source and target maps to
X
given by the natural projection and the group action, respectively:
{\mathrm{s}}(k,x)=x
and
{\mathrm{t}}(k,x)=k.x
.
The composition is given by
(k_{2},x_{2})\circ(k_{1},x_{1})=(k_{2}k_{1},x_{1})
whenever
x_{2}=k_{1}.x_{1}
.
The corresponding moduli set
X/K
is the set of all
K
-orbits in
X
.
Moreover, if
X
is an affine algebraic variety,
K
is an affine algebraic group, and the action is algebraic, then
{\mathcal{G}}=K\ltimes X\rightrightarrows X
is an algebraic Lie groupoid, and the corresponding moduli space
\mathfrak{G}
is an algebraic stack equal to the stack-theoretic quotient
X/K
.

Second, the
fundamental groupoid

\Pi_{1}(\Sigma)\rightrightarrows\Sigma
of the surface
\Sigma
is the space of all continuous paths
\gamma:[0,1]\to X
considered up to homotopy with fixed endpoints.
It is a real four-dimensional topological manifold.
The
restriction
of the groupoid
\Pi_{1}(\Sigma)
to a subset
S\subset\Sigma
is by definition the full subgroupoid
\Pi_{1}(\Sigma)|_{S}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}={\mathrm{s}}^{-1}(S)\cap{\mathrm{t}}^{-1}(S)\subset\Pi_{1}(\Sigma)
consisting of paths on
\Sigma
that begin and end in
S
.
In particular, for any point
p\in\Sigma
, the restriction of the fundamental groupoid
\Pi_{1}(\Sigma)
to
p
is nothing but the fundamental group:

\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p)=\Pi_{1}(\Sigma)\big|_{p}={\mathrm{s}}^{-1}(p)\cap{\mathrm{t}}^{-1}(p)\subset\Pi_{1}(\Sigma).

Thus, the fundamental group
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p)
is an example of a groupoid over a single point.

Third, to any topological complex vector bundle
\mathnormal{E}\to\Sigma
, we can naturally associate a Lie groupoid
\operatorname{\mathsf{GL}}(\mathnormal{E})\rightrightarrows\Sigma
called the
transport groupoid
.
Its objects are the points of
\Sigma
and its morphisms are the linear isomorphisms between the fibres of
\mathnormal{E}
; that is, for any pair of points
p,q\in\Sigma
, the set of morphisms in
\operatorname{\mathsf{GL}}(\mathnormal{E})
from
p
to
q
is the set
GL(\mathnormal{E}|_{p},\mathnormal{E}|_{q})
of all linear isomorphisms
\mathnormal{E}|_{p}\overset{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.35623pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}{\longrightarrow}\mathnormal{E}|_{q}
.
The restriction of
\operatorname{\mathsf{GL}}(\mathnormal{E})
to a point
p\in\Sigma
is nothing but the automorphism group of the fibre:
\operatorname{\mathsf{GL}}(\mathnormal{E})|_{p}=\operatorname{\mathsf{GL}}(\mathnormal{E}|_{p})\simeq G
.

1.2
Linear Local Systems

In this paper, by a
local system
on
\Sigma
we shall mean either a linear local system or equivalently a
G
-local system.
Let us quickly remind the reader of what these are and how to pass back and forth between the two notions.

Linear local systems
(more precisely,
complex-linear local systems of finite rank
) are locally constant sheaves
{\mathcal{E}}
on
\Sigma
of finite-dimensional complex vector spaces.
In particular, all stalks
{\mathcal{E}}_{p}
of
{\mathcal{E}}
are complex vector spaces of the same dimension called the
rank
of
{\mathcal{E}}
.
Likewise, a
G
-
local system
is a locally constant sheaf
\hat{{\mathcal{E}}}
of sets on
\Sigma
equipped with a right
G
-action such that the stalk
\hat{{\mathcal{E}}}_{p}
of
\hat{{\mathcal{E}}}
at every point
p\in\Sigma
is a
G
-torsor; i.e., a set equipped with a free and transitive right
G
-action.

Linear local systems on
\Sigma
together with linear sheaf isomorphisms (i.e., sheaf morphisms which are linear isomorphisms on stalks) form a groupoid which, along with the corresponding
moduli space of local systems
, will be respectively denoted by

\textup{Loc}_{G}(\Sigma)\hskip 20.00003pt\text{and}\hskip 20.00003pt\mathfrak{Loc}_{G}(\Sigma)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\textup{Loc}_{G}(\Sigma)\big/{\sim}.

We usually draw no distinction between
G
-local systems and linear local systems because their categories are naturally equivalent thanks to the following lemma.

Lemma 1.1
.

There is an equivalence of categories between the groupoid of linear local systems and the groupoid of
G
-local systems (where morphisms are sheaf morphisms which are
G
-equivariant maps on stalks) given by the following pair of inverse functors:

{\mathcal{E}}\longmapsto\hat{{\mathcal{E}}}=GL(\mathbb{C}^{n},{\mathcal{E}})\hskip 20.00003pt\text{and}\hskip 20.00003pt\hat{{\mathcal{E}}}\longmapsto{\mathcal{E}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\hat{{\mathcal{E}}}\times_{G}\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{n}_{\Sigma}.

The notation for this pair of functors will be explained in the course of the proof.

Proof.

If
{\mathcal{E}}
is a linear local system of rank
n
, then for any
p\in\Sigma
the set
\hat{{\mathcal{E}}}_{p}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=GL(\mathbb{C}^{n},{\mathcal{E}}_{p})
of all linear isomorphisms
\mathbb{C}^{n}\overset{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.35623pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}{\longrightarrow}{\mathcal{E}}_{p}
is a
G
-torsor: it has a free and transitive right
G
-action by pre-composition; i.e.,
f.g=f\circ g
for any
f\in GL(\mathbb{C}^{n},{\mathcal{E}}_{p})
and any
g\in G=GL(\mathbb{C}^{n})
.
Similarly, the set
GL\big(\mathbb{C}^{n},{\mathcal{E}}(U))
for any open set
U\subset\Sigma
has a right
G
-action though not necessarily free and transitive.
Therefore, the correspondence

\hat{{\mathcal{E}}}:U\mapsto GL\big(\mathbb{C}^{n},\mathcal{E}(U))

is a locally constant sheaf of sets with a right
G
-action whose stalk at any
p\in\Sigma
is the
G
-torsor
\hat{{\mathcal{E}}}_{p}=\operatorname{\mathsf{GL}}(\mathbb{C}^{n},{\mathcal{E}}_{p})
.
In other words,
\hat{{\mathcal{E}}}
is a
G
-local system, which we call
\hat{{\mathcal{E}}}=\mathrel{\mathop{:}}GL(\mathbb{C}^{n},{\mathcal{E}})
by a slight abuse of notation.

Conversely, if
\hat{{\mathcal{E}}}
is a
G
-local system, then
{\mathcal{E}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\hat{{\mathcal{E}}}\times_{G}\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{n}_{\Sigma}
is a linear local system on
\Sigma
, where
\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{n}_{\Sigma}
is the constant sheaf with value
\mathbb{C}^{n}
with its natural right
G
-action.
Recall that in general, if
X
and
Y
are two sets equipped with a right
G
-action, then
X\times_{G}Y
(sometimes called the
tensor product of
G
-sets
) is defined as the quotient set
X\times Y/{\sim}
by the equivalence relation
(x.g,y)\sim(x,y.g)
for all
x\in X
,
y\in Y
, and
g\in G
.

Let us now explain how these constructions extend to morphisms, thus defining a pair of functors.
Recall that any morphism of sheaves is completely determined on stalks.
So a morphism of linear local systems
\varphi:{\mathcal{E}}\to{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime}
induces a linear map on stalks
\varphi_{p}:{\mathcal{E}}_{p}\to{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime}_{p}
which in turn induces a
G
-equivariant map
\hat{\varphi}_{p}:GL(\mathbb{C}^{n},{\mathcal{E}}_{p})\to GL(\mathbb{C}^{n},{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime}_{p})
given by post-composition with
\varphi_{p}
(i.e.,
f\mapsto\varphi_{p}\circ f
), which in turn induces a sheaf morphism
\hat{\varphi}:GL(\mathbb{C}^{n},{\mathcal{E}})\to GL(\mathbb{C}^{n},{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime})
; i.e.,
\hat{\varphi}:\hat{{\mathcal{E}}}\to\hat{{\mathcal{E}}}^{\prime}
.
Conversely, any morphism of
G
-local systems
\hat{\varphi}:\hat{{\mathcal{E}}}\to\hat{{\mathcal{E}}}^{\prime}
induces a
G
-equivariant map on stalks
\hat{\varphi}_{p}:\hat{{\mathcal{E}}}_{p}\to\hat{{\mathcal{E}}}^{\prime}_{p}
which in turn induces a linear map
\varphi_{p}:\hat{{\mathcal{E}}}_{p}\times_{G}\mathbb{C}^{n}\to\hat{{\mathcal{E}}}^{\prime}_{p}\times_{G}\mathbb{C}^{n}
given by
(f,v)\mapsto\big(\hat{\varphi}_{p}(f),v\big)
and therefore a linear morphism
\varphi:{\mathcal{E}}\to{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime}
.
To see that
\varphi_{p}
is indeed a linear map, recall that
\hat{{\mathcal{E}}}^{\prime}_{p}
is a
G
-torsor, so for any
f\in\hat{{\mathcal{E}}}_{p}
there is a unique element
g\in G
such that
\hat{\varphi}_{p}(f)=f.g=f\circ g
, and so
\big(\hat{\varphi}_{p}(f),v\big)=(f.g,v)\sim(f,vg)
.

Finally, we explain why these two functors determine an equivalence of categories. For the sake of lighter notations, let us denote by
\mathsf{C}
and
D
the categories of linear local systems on
\Sigma
and the one of
G
-local systems on
\Sigma
respectively, by
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
the functors

{\mathcal{F}}:\textup{C}\longmapsto\textup{D}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{and}\hskip 20.00003pt{\mathcal{G}}:\textup{D}\longmapsto\textup{C}.

If
{\mathcal{E}}
is a linear local system and
\hat{{\mathcal{E}}}=GL(\mathbb{C}^{n},{\mathcal{E}})
is the associated
G
-local system, then the associated linear local system
{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\hat{{\mathcal{E}}}\times_{G}\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{n}_{\Sigma}
is canonically isomorphic to
{\mathcal{E}}
.
The reason is that, at any
p\in\Sigma
, the stalk
{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime}_{p}=\hat{{\mathcal{E}}}_{p}\times_{G}\mathbb{C}^{n}=GL(\mathbb{C}^{n},{\mathcal{E}}_{p})\times_{G}\mathbb{C}^{n}
is canonically isomorphic to
{\mathcal{E}}_{p}
because, for any
n
-dimensional vector space
E
, there is a canonical isomorphism
GL(\mathbb{C}^{n},E)\times_{G}\mathbb{C}^{n}\overset{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.35623pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}{\longrightarrow}E
given by
(f,v)\mapsto f(v)
.

This isomorphism provides natural transformations
\eta:\operatorname{Id}_{\mathnormal{C}}\Rightarrow\mathcal{G}\circ\mathcal{F}
and
\zeta:\operatorname{Id}_{D}\Rightarrow\mathcal{F}\circ\mathcal{G}
.
∎

Finally, we also mention that any diffeomorphism
f:\Sigma^{\prime}\to\Sigma
— or, more generally, any homotopy equivalence — induces an equivalence
f^{\ast}:\textup{Loc}_{G}(\Sigma)\overset{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.35623pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}{\longrightarrow}\textup{Loc}_{G}(\Sigma^{\prime})
by pullback.
In particular, any local system on
\Sigma
restricts to a local system on the interior
\Sigma^{\circ}\subset\Sigma
, and this restriction functor is actually an equivalence of categories
\textup{Loc}_{G}(\Sigma)\overset{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.35623pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}{\longrightarrow}\textup{Loc}_{G}(\Sigma^{\circ})
which has a canonical inverse.

Lemma 1.2
.

For any surface
\Sigma
, any local system
{\mathcal{E}}^{\circ}
on the interior
\Sigma^{\circ}
has a canonical extension to a local system
{\mathcal{E}}
on
\Sigma
, thus yielding an inverse pair of restriction-extension functors:

\textup{Loc}_{G}(\Sigma)\overset{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.65764pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}{\rightleftharpoons}\textup{Loc}_{G}(\Sigma^{\circ}).

Proof.

The canonical extension
{\mathcal{E}}
to
\Sigma
of a local system
{\mathcal{E}}^{\circ}
on
\Sigma^{\circ}
is defined as follows.
For every boundary point
p\in\partial\Sigma
, the stalk
{\mathcal{E}}_{p}
of
{\mathcal{E}}
at
p
is defined as the restriction of
{\mathcal{E}}^{\circ}
to the germ of a sectorial neighbourhood of
p
.
Recall that, since
\Sigma
is a manifold with boundary, a
sectorial neighbourhood
of a boundary point
p
is the intersection with
\Sigma^{\circ}
of a simply connected open boundary chart containing
p
.
More precisely,
{\mathcal{E}}_{p}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\varinjlim{\mathcal{E}}^{\circ}(U)
is the direct limit of vector spaces
{\mathcal{E}}^{\circ}(U)
indexed over all sectorial neighbourhoods
U\subset\Sigma
of
p
.
Notice that since
{\mathcal{E}}^{\circ}
is locally constant, the vector spaces
{\mathcal{E}}^{\circ}(U)
stabilise in this limit, and so there is a canonical linear isomorphism
{\mathcal{E}}_{p}\overset{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.35623pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}{\longrightarrow}{\mathcal{E}}^{\circ}(U)
for any sectorial neighbourhood
U
of
p
.
∎

1.3
Groupoid Representations and Holonomy

A
representation
of a groupoid
{\mathcal{G}}\rightrightarrows X
is by definition a pair
(\mathnormal{E},\Phi)
consisting of a complex vector bundle
\mathnormal{E}\to X
and a groupoid morphism
\Phi:{\mathcal{G}}\to\operatorname{\mathsf{GL}}(\mathnormal{E})
.
We usually write
\Phi_{\gamma}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\Phi(\gamma)\in\operatorname{\mathsf{GL}}(\mathnormal{E})
for any
\gamma\in{\mathcal{G}}
.
A morphism
\varphi:(\mathnormal{E},\Phi)\to(\mathnormal{E}^{\prime},\Phi^{\prime})
is a bundle map
\varphi:\mathnormal{E}\to\mathnormal{E}^{\prime}
which intertwines the homomorphisms
\Phi,\Phi^{\prime}
in the following sense.
For any
\gamma\in{\mathcal{G}}
with source and target
p,q\in X
, we have the following commutative diagram of maps between the fibres of
\mathnormal{E}
and
\mathnormal{E}^{\prime}
:

\hbox to86.18pt{\vbox to59.23pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 43.0887pt\lower-29.60864pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{i.e.}\hskip 20.00003pt\varphi_{q}\circ\Phi_{\gamma}=\Phi^{\prime}_{\gamma}\circ\varphi_{p}.

(1.1)

Moreover, if
\mathcal{G}
is a topological (resp. smooth, holomorphic, algebraic) Lie groupoid, then the vector bundles
\mathnormal{E}
and the bundle maps
\varphi
are required to be continuous (resp. smooth, holomorphic, algebraic).
We denote the groupoid of representations of
{\mathcal{G}}\rightrightarrows X
of rank
n
and the corresponding moduli space by

\textup{Rep}_{G}({\mathcal{G}})\hskip 20.00003pt\text{and}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{$\mathfrak{Rep}_{G}$}({\mathcal{G}})\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\textup{Rep}_{G}({\mathcal{G}})\big/{\sim}.

In the case of the fundamental groupoid, we give the following

Definition 1.3
.

A
representation
(or, more precisely, a
linear representation
of rank
n
, or a
G
-representation
) of the fundamental groupoid
\Pi_{1}(\Sigma)\rightrightarrows\Sigma
is a pair
(\mathnormal{E},\Phi)
consisting of a topological complex vector bundle
\mathnormal{E}\to\Sigma
of rank
n
and a (continuous) groupoid homomorphism
\Phi:\Pi_{1}(\Sigma)\to\operatorname{\mathsf{GL}}(\mathnormal{E})
.

Likewise, since the fundamental group is a groupoid over a single point and a vector bundle over a single point is a vector space, we recover representations of the fundamental group as groupoid representations.
Recall that, given a point
p\in\Sigma
, a
representation
of the fundamental group
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p)
is a pair
(E,\phi)
consisting of an
n
-dimensional complex vector space
E
and a group homomorphism
\phi:\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p)\to\operatorname{\mathsf{GL}}(E)
.

Since the fundamental group
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p)
is the restriction of the fundamental groupoid
\Pi_{1}(\Sigma)
to the point
p
, any representation
(\mathnormal{E},\Phi)
of
\Pi_{1}(\Sigma)
restricts to a representation of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p)
by taking
E
to be the fibre
\mathnormal{E}_{p}
of
\mathnormal{E}
at
p
and
\phi
to be the restriction of
\Phi
.
This yields a
restriction functor
on representations:

\mathnormal{R}:\textup{Rep}_{G}\big(\Pi_{1}(\Sigma)\big)\to\textup{Rep}_{G}\big(\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p)\big)

sending

(\mathnormal{E},\Phi)\mapsto(E,\phi)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\Big(\mathnormal{E}_{p},\Phi\big|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p)}\Big).

This functor turns out to be an equivalence of categories with a preferred explicit inverse equivalence that gives a convenient way to extend representations from the fundamental group
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p)
to the fundamental groupoid
\Pi_{1}(\Sigma)
.
We will state and prove this equivalence as part of a more general result (
Proposition 1.4
) after discussing the holonomy representation.

1.3.1
Holonomy representation

Any local system
{\mathcal{E}}
on
\Sigma
induces a representation
(\mathnormal{E},\Phi)
of the fundamental groupoid
\Pi_{1}(\Sigma)
by taking the holonomy along paths, and vice versa by taking the deck-transformation-equivariant sections of a constant sheaf on the universal cover of
\Sigma
.
Let us briefly recall how this works.

The
holonomy
of a local system
{\mathcal{E}}
along a path
\gamma:[0,1]\to\Sigma
from
p
to
q\in\Sigma
is by definition the linear isomorphism between on stalks,

\operatorname{hol}_{\gamma}:{\mathcal{E}}_{p}\overset{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.35623pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}{\longrightarrow}{\mathcal{E}}_{q},

(1.2)

defined as the composition of canonical isomorphisms

{{\mathcal{E}}_{p}\overset{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.35623pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}{\longrightarrow}\gamma^{-1}{\mathcal{E}}\big([0,1]\big)\overset{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.35623pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}{\longrightarrow}{\mathcal{E}}_{q}}.

When
p=q
, the isomorphism
\operatorname{hol}_{\gamma}
is usually called the
monodromy
of
{\mathcal{E}}
along a loop
\gamma
based at
p
and denoted by
\operatorname{mon}_{\gamma}
.
The isomorphisms defining
\operatorname{hol}_{\gamma}
depend only on the homotopy class of
\gamma
relative its endpoint and they exist by virtue of the fact that the inverse-image sheaf
\gamma^{-1}{\mathcal{E}}
on the interval
[0,1]
is constant because the interval is contractible.
Furthermore, observe that any sheaf morphism
\varphi:{\mathcal{E}}\to{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime}
defines a linear map
\gamma^{-1}{\mathcal{E}}\big([0,1]\big)\to\gamma^{-1}{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime}\big([0,1]\big)
, and therefore the holonomies of
{\mathcal{E}}
and
{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime}
along any path
\gamma
from
p
to
q
are related by the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}}_{p}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}}_{q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime}_{p}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime}_{q}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{hol}_{\gamma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{p}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{hol}^{\prime}_{\gamma}}

(1.3)

Given a local system
{\mathcal{E}}
, let
{\mathcal{O}}_{\Sigma}
be the sheaf of continuous functions on
\Sigma
and consider the vector bundle
\mathnormal{E}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}={\mathcal{E}}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{\Sigma}
(where, in line with common practice, we treat vector bundles interchangeably with their sheaves of sections).
The fibre
\mathnormal{E}_{p}
of this bundle at any point
p\in\Sigma
is canonically isomorphic to the stalk
{\mathcal{E}}_{p}
.
Then the holonomy of
{\mathcal{E}}
defines a groupoid homomorphism

\operatorname{hol}:\Pi_{1}(\Sigma)\to\operatorname{\mathsf{GL}}(\mathnormal{E})\hskip 10.00002pt\text{sending}\hskip 10.00002pt\gamma\mapsto\operatorname{hol}_{\gamma}.

The result is a representation
(\mathnormal{E},\Phi)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=({\mathcal{E}}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{\Sigma},\operatorname{hol})
of the fundamental groupoid
\Pi_{1}(\Sigma)
called the
holonomy representation
of
{\mathcal{E}}
.
If, in addition, we fix a basepoint
p\in\Sigma
, then the restriction of the holonomy representation of
{\mathcal{E}}
to the fundamental group
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p)
determines a representation
(E,\Phi)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=({\mathcal{E}}_{p},\operatorname{mon}_{p})
with

\operatorname{mon}_{p}:\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p)\to GL({\mathcal{E}}_{p})\hskip 10.00002pt\text{sending}\hskip 10.00002pt\gamma\mapsto\operatorname{mon}_{\gamma}

called the
monodromy representation
of
{\mathcal{E}}
at
p
.

Proposition 1.4
.

There is an equivalence of categories

\operatorname{Hol}:\textup{Loc}_{G}(\Sigma)\overset{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.35623pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}{\longrightarrow}\textup{Rep}_{G}\big(\Pi_{1}(\Sigma)\big)

sending

{\mathcal{E}}\longmapsto(\mathnormal{E},\Phi)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=({\mathcal{E}}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{\Sigma},\operatorname{hol})

called the
holonomy representation functor
which associates a local system to its holonomy representation.
Furthermore, for any
p\in\Sigma
, the composition of the restriction functor with the holonomy representation functor is an equivalence of categories

\operatorname{Mon}_{p}:\textup{Loc}_{G}(\Sigma)\overset{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.35623pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}{\longrightarrow}\textup{Rep}_{G}\big(\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p)\big)

sending

{\mathcal{E}}\longmapsto(E,\phi)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=({\mathcal{E}}_{p},\operatorname{mon}_{p})

called the
monodromy representation functor
.
Consequently, the restriction of representations from the fundamental groupoid
\Pi_{1}(\Sigma)
to the fundamental group
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p)
is also an equivalence of categories:

\mathnormal{R}:\textup{Rep}_{G}\big(\Pi_{1}(\Sigma)\big)\overset{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.35623pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}{\longrightarrow}\textup{Rep}_{G}\big(\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p)\big)

sending

\hskip 56.9055pt(\mathnormal{E},\Phi)\mapsto(E,\phi)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\Big(\mathnormal{E}_{p},\Phi\big|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p)}\Big).

Explicitly, an inverse to the monodromy representation functor
\operatorname{Mon}_{p}
is given by

\operatorname{Mon}_{p}^{-1}:(E,\phi)\mapsto{\mathcal{E}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\big(\tilde{\Sigma}\times E\big)/\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p)

where
\tilde{\Sigma}\to\Sigma
is the universal cover based at
p
and the quotient is by the diagonal action of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p)
by deck transformations on
\tilde{\Sigma}
and by the representation
\phi
on
E
.
Similarly, an inverse functor to the restriction functor
\mathnormal{R}
lifts a representation
(E,\phi)
of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p)
to the holonomy representation of the local system
{\mathcal{E}}=\big(\tilde{\Sigma}\times E\big)/\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p)
; i.e.,

\operatorname{\mathnormal{R}}^{-1}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\operatorname{Hol}\circ\operatorname{Mon}_{p}^{-1}.

These notations will be fully explained in the course of the proof (specifically, see (
A.1
)) of this classical result.
For clarity, we remark that the holonomy and monodromy representation functors fit into the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{Loc}_{G}(\Sigma)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{Rep}_{G}\big(\Pi_{1}(\Sigma)\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{Rep}_{G}\big(\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p)\big).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hol}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Mon}_{p}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathnormal{R}}

We also state the following immediate corollary of
Proposition 1.4
.

Corollary 1.5
.

There are canonical bijections between the moduli spaces of local systems, of representations of the fundamental groupoid
\Pi_{1}(\Sigma)
, and of representations of the fundamental group
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p)
for any
p\in\Sigma
:

\mathfrak{Loc}_{G}(\Sigma)\cong\mathfrak{Rep}_{G}\big(\Pi_{1}(\Sigma)\big)\cong\mathfrak{Rep}_{G}\big(\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p)\big),

given by
[{\mathcal{E}}]\mapsto[\operatorname{Hol}({\mathcal{E}})]\mapsto[\operatorname{Mon}_{p}({\mathcal{E}})]
.

Although
Proposition 1.4
is classical, we have found no single reference that cleanly addresses all of its aspects.
For this reason, and because we shall use parts of this proof in the next section, we supply a detailed proof in Appendix
A
.

Remark 1.6
.

Let us explain in more detail the definition (
1.2
) of the holonomy of a local system
{\mathcal{E}}
along a path
\gamma
.
First, the inverse image
\gamma^{-1}{\mathcal{E}}
is by definition the sheaf on the interval
[0,1]
associated with the presheaf that sends open subsets
I\subset[0,1]
to the direct limit of
{\mathcal{E}}(U)
over open subsets
U\subset\Sigma
containing the image
\gamma(I)
; in symbols,
I\mapsto\varinjlim{\mathcal{E}}(U)
.
Concretely, this implies that for any open subset
I\subset[0,1]
, there is an open neighbourhood
U\subset\Sigma
of
\gamma(I)
together with a linear map
{\mathcal{E}}(U)\to\gamma^{-1}{\mathcal{E}}(I)
.

Since
{\mathcal{E}}
is locally constant, it follows that the inverse image sheaf
\gamma^{-1}{\mathcal{E}}
is locally constant, too: for any sufficiently small open subset
I\subset[0,1]
, there is an open neighbourhood
U
of
\gamma(I)
such that the restriction
{\mathcal{E}}|_{U}
is a constant sheaf on
U
, which implies that the restriction
\gamma^{-1}{\mathcal{E}}\big|_{I}
is a constant sheaf on
I
with a canonical isomorphism
{\mathcal{E}}(U)\overset{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.35623pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}{\longrightarrow}\gamma^{-1}{\mathcal{E}}(I)
.
But since
[0,1]
is contractible, the locally constant sheaf
\gamma^{-1}{\mathcal{E}}
is in fact a constant sheaf on
[0,1]
, which means means there is a canonical isomorphism
\gamma^{-1}{\mathcal{E}}(I)\overset{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.35623pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}{\longrightarrow}\gamma^{-1}{\mathcal{E}}\big([0,1]\big)
for any open subset
I\subset[0,1]
.

Next, any half-open interval
[0,\epsilon)
is an open subset of
[0,1]
, so any open neighbourhood
U
of the image
\gamma\big([0,\epsilon)\big)
is in particular an open neighbourhood of
p=\gamma(0)
.
But since
{\mathcal{E}}_{p}
is the stalk of the locally constant sheaf
{\mathcal{E}}
at
p
, there is by definition an isomorphism
{\mathcal{E}}_{p}\overset{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.35623pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}{\longrightarrow}{\mathcal{E}}(U)
for a sufficiently small
U
.
Consequently, we find the canonical isomorphism
{\mathcal{E}}_{p}\overset{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.35623pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}{\longrightarrow}\gamma^{-1}{\mathcal{E}}\big([0,1]\big)
used in the definition of the holonomy
\operatorname{hol}_{\gamma}
.

1.4
Character Variety

The advantage of fixing a point
p\in\Sigma
and reducing our attention to the representations of the fundamental group
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p)
is that it can be used to give an explicit, finite-dimensional model of the moduli space
\mathfrak{Loc}_{G}(\Sigma)
.

A
frame
(or
framing
) on an
n
-dimensional vector space
E
is an isomorphism
\beta:E\overset{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.35623pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{C}^{n}
.
Equivalently, a frame is a choice of an ordered basis of
E
or of a group isomorphism
\operatorname{\mathsf{GL}}(E)\overset{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.35623pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}{\longrightarrow}G
.
Therefore, given any representation
(E,\Phi)
of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p)
, a frame on
E
induces a group homomorphism
\rho:\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p)\to G
defined by
\rho:\gamma\mapsto\rho_{\gamma}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\beta\circ\Phi_{\gamma}\circ\beta^{-1}
.
Conversely, any
\rho:\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p)\to G
gives rise to the representation
(\mathbb{C}^{n},\rho)
.

If
(E,\Phi)
is a representation with frame
\beta
, then changing the frame on
E
has the effect of conjugating the homomorphism
\rho
by an element of
G
.
Namely,
\rho^{\prime}:\gamma\mapsto\rho^{\prime}_{\gamma}=g\rho_{\gamma}g^{-1}
whenever
\beta^{\prime}\circ\beta^{-1}=g\in G
.
This formula defines a conjugation action of
G
on the set of group homomorphisms, called the
representation variety
,

R_{G}(\Sigma,p)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\operatorname{\mathsf{Hom}}\big(\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p),G\big).

Likewise, if
\varphi:(E,\Phi)\overset{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.35623pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}{\longrightarrow}(E^{\prime},\Phi^{\prime})
is an isomorphism and
\beta,\beta^{\prime}
are frames on
E,E^{\prime}
that determine
\rho,\rho^{\prime}\in R_{G}(\Sigma,p)
, then
\varphi
gives a group element
g\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\beta^{\prime}\circ\varphi\circ\beta^{-1}\in G
which relates
\rho
and
\rho
by the same conjugation action:
\rho^{\prime}_{\gamma}=g\rho_{\gamma}g^{-1}
for all
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p)
.

This defines an inclusion functor from the action groupoid
G\ltimes R_{G}(\Sigma,p)
to the groupoid of representations of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p)
, and this functor turns out to be an equivalence.

Proposition 1.7
.

There is a canonical equivalence of categories

\textup{Rep}_{G}\big(\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p)\big)\cong G\ltimes R_{G}(\Sigma,p).

Furthermore, for any
p,p^{\prime}\in\Sigma
, there is an equivalence of categories
G\ltimes R_{G}(\Sigma,p)\cong G\ltimes R_{G}(\Sigma,p^{\prime})
.

This equivalence is given by the inclusion functor

G\ltimes R_{G}(\Sigma,p)\overset{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.35623pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}{\longrightarrow}\textup{Rep}_{G}\big(\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p)\big)\hskip 10.00002pt\text{sending}\hskip 10.00002pt\rho\longmapsto(E,\Phi)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=(\mathbb{C}^{n},\rho),

and viewing any
g\in G
as an isomorphism
g:\mathbb{C}^{n}\to\mathbb{C}^{n}
.
An inverse functor requires a choice of frame
\beta:E\overset{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.35623pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{C}^{n}
for every vector space
E\in\textup{Vec}_{G}
.
Once chosen, it is given on objects by
(E,\Phi)\mapsto\rho
where
\rho_{\gamma}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\beta\circ\Phi_{\gamma}\circ\beta^{-1}
for all
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p)
, and on morphisms by
\varphi\mapsto g\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\beta^{\prime}\circ\varphi\circ\beta^{-1}
.

Proof of
Proposition 1.7
.

We just need to check that any inverse functor defined as suggested above is indeed inverse to the inclusion functor.
That it is a left inverse is obvious especially if we choose the identity frame on
\mathbb{C}^{n}
.
For a right inverse, if
(E,\Phi){\mapsto}\rho
via a frame
\beta
, then
\beta
in fact defines the desired isomorphism from
(E,\Phi)
to
(\mathbb{C}^{n},\rho)
.
∎

If
\Sigma
is compact, the representation variety
R_{G}(\Sigma,p)
is a smooth affine algebraic variety
[
28
,
31
]
.
If
\Sigma
has genus
g
and no boundary, then the representation variety has dimension
n^{2}(2g-1)+1
when
g\geq 2
and
(n^{2}+n)g
when
g=0,1
.
If
\Sigma
has
s\geq 1
holes then the representation variety has dimension
n^{2}(2g+s-1)
.

The affine algebraic group
G
is acting algebraically on the affine algebraic variety
R_{G}(\Sigma,p)
, so the action groupoid
G\ltimes R_{G}(\Sigma,p)
is algebraic.
Its moduli space is the well-known algebraic quotient stack called the
character variety
:

\mathfrak{X}_{G}(\Sigma)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=R_{G}(\Sigma,p)\big/G.

Corollary 1.8
.

There are canonical bijections between the moduli spaces of local systems, representations of the fundamental groupoid
\Pi_{1}(\Sigma)
, representations of the fundamental group
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p)
for any basepoint
p\in\Sigma
, and the character stack of
\Sigma
:

\text{$\mathfrak{Loc}_{G}(\Sigma)$}\cong\text{$\mathfrak{Rep}_{G}\big(\Pi_{1}(\Sigma)\big)$}\cong\text{$\mathfrak{Rep}_{G}\big(\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p)\big)$}\cong\text{$\mathfrak{X}_{G}(\Sigma)$}.

Consequently, all these moduli spaces have a natural structure of an affine algebraic quotient stack.

Explicitly, the above bijection is defined as the composition

\displaystyle\mathfrak{Loc}_{G}(\Sigma)

\displaystyle\to\mathfrak{Rep}_{G}\big(\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p)\big)\to\text{$\operatorname{\mathsf{Hom}}\big(\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p),G)/G$}=\mathfrak{X}_{G}(\Sigma)

\displaystyle[{\mathcal{E}}]

\displaystyle\mapsto[\operatorname{Mon}_{p}({\mathcal{E}})]=[({\mathcal{E}}_{p},\operatorname{mon}_{p})]\mapsto[\rho]

where the last arrow involves choosing a frame on the vector space
{\mathcal{E}}_{p}
.

1.5
Monodromy Data

Finally, if
\Sigma
is compact of genus
g
, then it has at most finitely many boundary components.
Then the fundamental group
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p)
is finitely generated by
2g+s
generators with one relation, where
s\geq 0
is the total number of punctures and boundary components of
\Sigma
, collectively referred to as
holes
.
In fact, if there is at least one hole (i.e.,
s\geq 1
), then
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p)
is a free group on
2g+s-1
generators.
A generating set can be obtained by selecting
s
loops going round the holes of
\Sigma
as well as
2g
loops representing the
{\mathrm{A}}
and
{\mathrm{B}}
-cycles.
More precisely:

1.

Choose any collection of
2g
oriented loops
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{g},\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{g}
based at
p
that determine a basis of
{\mathrm{A}}
and
{\mathrm{B}}
-cycles.
Namely, if
\Sigma^{\prime}
denotes the closed surface obtained from
\Sigma
by capping off all boundary components, then the fundamental group
\pi_{1}(\Sigma^{\prime},p)
is generated by the
\alpha_{i},\beta_{i}
such that
\prod_{i=1}^{g}[\alpha_{i},\beta_{i}]=1
where
[\alpha_{i},\beta_{i}]\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\alpha_{i}\beta_{i}\alpha_{i}^{-1}\beta_{i}^{-1}
is the commutator and
1=1_{p}
is the constant path at
p
.

2.

If
s\geq 1
, enumerate all holes of
\Sigma
as
1,\ldots,s
.
For each
i=1,\ldots,s
, let
\gamma_{i}
be a positively-oriented loop based at
p
going round only the
i
-th hole; i.e.,
\gamma_{i}
belongs to the conjugacy class in
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p)
determined by the
i
-th hole.

These generators can be chosen in such a way as to satisfy the relation

\prod_{i=1}^{\begin{subarray}{c}g\\
\longleftarrow\end{subarray}}[\alpha_{i},\beta_{i}]\prod_{i=1}^{\begin{subarray}{c}s\\
\longleftarrow\end{subarray}}\gamma_{i}=1.

(1.4)

Here, the left arrow over the product sign indicates that the product is taken from right to left.
Therefore, the fundamental group has an explicit presentation

\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p)\cong\left\langle\alpha_{i},\beta_{i},\gamma_{i}~\big|~\text{ relation \eqref{250922123329}}\right\rangle.

(1.5)

The relation (
1.4
) can be solved for any
\gamma_{i}
, but not for any of the
\alpha_{i}
or
\beta_{i}
.
Consequently, if
s\geq 1
, then
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p)
is isomorphic to the free group on
2g+s-1
generators.

Next, we introduce the following definition.

Definition 1.9
.

For any integers
g,s\geq 0
, a
monodromy data
in
G
of type
(g,s)
is any collection of matrices
(\bm{\mathnormal{A}},\bm{\mathnormal{B}},\bm{\mathnormal{M}})\subset G
consisting of:

•

\bm{\mathnormal{A}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\left\{\mathnormal{A}_{1},\ldots,\mathnormal{A}_{g}\right\},\bm{\mathnormal{B}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\left\{\mathnormal{B}_{1},\ldots,\mathnormal{B}_{g}\right\}
called
period monodromies
;

•

\bm{\mathnormal{M}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\left\{\mathnormal{M}_{1},\ldots,\mathnormal{M}_{s}\right\}
called
standard monodromies
;

and satisfying the following relation:

\prod_{i=1}^{\begin{subarray}{c}g\\
\longleftarrow\end{subarray}}[\mathnormal{A}_{i},\mathnormal{B}_{i}]\prod_{i=1}^{\begin{subarray}{c}s\\
\longleftarrow\end{subarray}}\mathnormal{M}_{i}=\mathds{1}.

(1.6)

We denote the
space of monodromy data
in
G
of type
(g,s)
by

\sf{Mon}_{G}(g,s)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\left\{(\bm{\mathnormal{A}},\bm{\mathnormal{B}},\bm{\mathnormal{M}})\in G^{2g+s}~\Big|~\textup{ relation \eqref{251005161035} }\big.\right\}.

Definition 1.10
.

To equip a surface
\Sigma
of genus
g
with
s
holes with monodromy data is to choose a point
p\in\Sigma
and an isomorphism

R_{G}(\Sigma,p)\overset{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.35623pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}{\longrightarrow}\sf{Mon}_{G}(g,s).

A choice of finite presentation of the fundamental group
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p)
of the form (
1.5
) determines such an isomorphism by associating monodromy data
(\bm{\mathnormal{A}},\bm{\mathnormal{B}},\bm{\mathnormal{M}})\in\sf{Mon}_{G}(g,s)
to the generators
(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\beta},\bm{\gamma})
of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p)
like so:

\alpha_{i}\mapsto\mathnormal{A}_{i}~,\hskip 20.00003pt\beta_{i}\mapsto\mathnormal{B}_{i}~,\hskip 20.00003pt\gamma_{i}\mapsto\mathnormal{M}_{i}.

Note that if
s\geq 1
, then by eliminating one of the matrices
\mathnormal{M}_{i}
from the list
(\bm{\mathnormal{A}},\bm{\mathnormal{B}},\bm{\mathnormal{M}})
of generators destroys the relation (
1.6
).
This yields a bijection
\sf{Mon}_{G}(g,s)\cong G^{2g+s-1}
which shows that the set of monodromy data
\sf{Mon}_{G}(g,s)
is a smooth affine algebraic variety of dimension
(2g+s-1)n^{2}
provided that
s\geq 1
.

The space of monodromy data
\sf{Mon}_{G}(g,s)
is equipped with a natural left
G
-action given by simultaneous conjugation:

g.(\bm{\mathnormal{A}},\bm{\mathnormal{B}},\bm{\mathnormal{M}})=(g\bm{\mathnormal{A}}g^{-1},g\bm{\mathnormal{B}}g^{-1},g\bm{\mathnormal{M}}g^{-1}).

Then we arrive at the following classical result.

Proposition 1.11
.

Let
\Sigma
be a surface of genus
g
with
s
holes.
Then there is a non-canonical equivalence of categories between the groupoid of
G
-local systems on
\Sigma
and an action groupoid over the space of monodromy data of type
(g,s)
:

\textup{Loc}_{G}(\Sigma)\simeq G\ltimes\sf{Mon}_{G}(g,s).

To obtain such an equivalence, fix a point
p\in\Sigma
and choose any presentation of the fundamental group
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p)
of the form (
1.5
).
Consequently, there is a bijection between the character variety and the moduli space of monodromy data:

\text{$\mathfrak{X}_{G}(\Sigma)$}\cong\sf{Mon}_{G}(g,s)/G.

In particular, if
s\geq 1
, then
\mathfrak{X}_{G}(\Sigma)\cong G^{2g+s-1}/G
.
Consequently, the moduli space
\mathfrak{Loc}_{G}(\Sigma)
of
G
-local systems on
\Sigma
is an algebraic stack of dimension
(2g-2)n^{2}+2
if
s=0
and
(2g+s-2)n^{2}+1
if
s\geq 1
.

We stress that the above equivalence and bijection depend nontrivially on the chosen presentation (
1.5
) of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p)
.

2
Surfaces with Marked Boundary

Recall that a
bordered surface
is a connected, oriented, smooth real two-dimensional manifold-with-boundary
\Sigma
which we also assume to be compact.
It has finitely many boundary components each of which is diffeomorphic to a circle.
A
marked bordered surface
is a pair consisting of a bordered surface equipped with a finite nonempty subset of
marked points
such that each boundary component contains at least one marked point.
An isomorphism of marked bordered surfaces is an orientation-preserving diffeomorphism that sends marked points to marked points.

Remark 2.1
.

The terminology for
marked bordered surfaces
matches what seems to be a broadly accepted meaning in the literature, including Fomin-Shapiro-Thurston
[
17
, §2]
, Smith
[
33
]
, Bridgeland-Smith
[
8
, §8]
, Allegretti-Bridgeland
[
1
, §3.6]
, Allegretti
[
3
, §2]
, to name a few.
However, it corresponds to what Goncharov
[
19
, §1]
and Goncharov-Shen
[
20
, §2]
call a “decorated surface”.

Marked points in the interior are called
punctures
.
For our purposes, it will be more natural to work not with marked bordered surfaces but with their real-oriented blowups along the punctures.
Concretely, the real-oriented blowup (see, e.g.,
[
27
, §1.1]
) of any marked bordered surface is a bordered surface
\Sigma
obtained by replacing each puncture with a boundary circle containing no marked points whilst keeping the rest of the surface, including the boundary marked points, unchanged.
Thus,
\Sigma
has marked points on some, but not all, boundary components.
Furthermore, sometimes it will be important for us to place an extra marked point on each unmarked boundary of
\Sigma
yet still be able to distinguish the original boundary circles with marked points from the ones that arose from punctures.
This motivates the following definition.

Definition 2.2
.

A
surface with marked boundary

is a pair
(\Sigma,P)
where
\Sigma
is a compact bordered surface whose boundary components are partitioned as

I=I_{\mathrm{sim}}\sqcup I_{\mathrm{reg}}\sqcup I_{\mathrm{irr}}

into
simple
,
regular
, and
irregular
boundary circles, and where
P\subset\partial\Sigma
is a finite set of boundary
marked points
, itself partitioned as

P=P_{\mathrm{I}}\sqcup P_{\mathrm{II}}

into respectively
primary marked points
and
secondary marked points
.
Furthermore, every irregular boundary circle contains at least one primary marked point and no regular ones; every regular boundary circle contains exactly one secondary marked point and no primary ones; and every simple boundary circle contains no marked points at all.
An isomorphism
f:(\Sigma,P)\to(\Sigma^{\prime},P^{\prime})
of decorated surfaces is an orientation-preserving homeomorphism
f:\Sigma\to\Sigma^{\prime}
with the property that
f(P_{\mathrm{I}})=P^{\prime}_{\mathrm{I}}
and
f(P_{\mathrm{II}})=P^{\prime}_{\mathrm{II}}
.

Often only the relative position of the marked points plays any role.
So for almost everything we say, we may allow the subset
P\subset\partial\Sigma
to be defined only up to isotopy, which concretely means that marked points can slide continuously along the boundary without crossing each other.
Hence, secondary marked points, being the sole occupiers of a regular boundary circle, are free to move around unconstrained.

Remark 2.3
.

Fock-Goncharov
[
15
, Def 1.1]
use the term “marked surface” which in our language is a surface with marked boundary in which all boundaries are marked.

Fock-Rosly
[
14
, §2]
and Fock-Goncharov
[
16
, §2]
also use the terms “ciliated surface”, “cilia”, and “holes” which in our language mean respectively a
surface with marked boundary with no secondary points,
P_{\mathrm{II}}=\emptyset
,
primary marked points
, and
simple boundary circles
.

Chekhov-Mazzocco
[
10
]
, Chekhov-Mazzocco-Rubtsov
[
9
,
11
]
, and Dal Martello-Mazzocco
[
12
]
use the terms “bordered cusped Riemann surface” or “Riemann surface with bordered” or “boundary cusps” for a manifold
\Sigma_{g,s,n}
whose underlying topological surface is in our language a surface with marked boundary with at least one primary marked point,
P_{\mathrm{I}}\neq\emptyset
and no secondary ones
P_{\mathrm{II}}=\emptyset
.

Boalch
[
4
]
considers a smooth compact complex curve
\Sigma
with a finite subset
\bm{a}\subset\Sigma
(using his notation).
He then considers its real-oriented blowup
\hat{\Sigma}\to\Sigma
and decorates its boundary
\partial\hat{\Sigma}
with two types of marked points called “singular directions”
\mathbb{A}\subset\partial\hat{\Sigma}
and “Stokes directions”
\mathbb{S}\subset\partial\hat{\Sigma}
.
If we drop the complex structure on
\hat{\Sigma}
and consider just the underlying topological surface, then the pair
(\hat{\Sigma},\mathbb{A})
is an example of our surface with marked boundary with no secondary marked points.

Jordan-Le-Schrader-Shapiro
[
24
, Def 1.1]
use the term “decorated surface” in a quite different way but which can be directly related to ours.
Namely, their notion involves regions in
\Sigma
separated by simple curves, called “walls”, that are closed or have endpoints on the boundary.
Their attention is largely restricted to a subclass called
simple decorated surfaces

[
24
, Rem 1.5]
which are equivalent to our our surface with marked boundary where all boundaries are marked.

The
boundary loop
based at a marked point
p\in P
is the loop determined by the boundary circle containing
p
, oriented according to the natural orientation on the boundary
\partial\Sigma
.
We also consider the punctured boundary
\hat{\partial}\Sigma\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\partial\Sigma\smallsetminus P_{\mathrm{I}}
, punctured along the primary marked points only.
It is a union of circles and open intervals.
A
boundary segment

\delta\subset\partial\Sigma
is the closure of a simply connected component of the punctured boundary
\hat{\partial}\Sigma
.
It has a natural orientation matching the orientation of the boundary
\partial\Sigma
.
Thus, it is a path
\delta:[0,1]\to\partial\Sigma
completely determined by its endpoints which are two (not necessarily distinct) primary marked points
p_{1}=\delta(0)
and
p_{2}=\delta(1)\in P_{\mathrm{I}}
.
For this reason, we may sometimes denote this boundary segment using the interval notation
[p_{1},p_{2}]
.
We denote the set of all boundary segments of
(\Sigma,P)
by

\mathfrak{A}=\mathfrak{A}(\Sigma,P)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\left\{\text{$\delta$ boundary segments}\right\}.

Remark 2.4
.

Note that Goncharov-Shen introduce the term “coloured decorated surface” which in our language corresponds to a triple
(\Sigma,P,\mathfrak{A}_{0})
consisting of a surface with marked boundary
(\Sigma,P)
and a sub-collection
\mathfrak{A}_{0}\subset\mathfrak{A}
of
boundary segments
(which they call “coloured boundary intervals”).

A surface with marked boundary
(\Sigma,P)
is completely determined up to isomorphism by the genus
g
of
\Sigma
; an ordered collection
\textbf{{s}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=(l,r,d)
of non-negative integers equal respectively to the number of unmarked, marked by secondary points, and marked by primary points boundaries; and an unordered collection
\bm{m}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\left\{m_{1},\ldots,m_{d}\right\}
of strictly positive integers equal to the number of primary marked points per boundary circle marked by primary points.
In total,
(\Sigma,P)
has
s=|\bm{s}|\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=l+r+d
holes,
r
secondary marked points,
m=|\bm{m}|\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=m_{1}+\ldots+m_{d}
primary marked points.
Also,
|P|=m+r
. These considerations justify the following

Definition 2.5
.

A surface with marked boundary
(\Sigma,P)
is called of
type

(g,\bm{s},\bm{m})
, where
\textbf{{s}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=(l,r,d)
and
\bm{m}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=(m_{1},\ldots,m_{d})
, if it has
l
simple boundaries,
r
regular boundaries,
d
irregular boundaries and
m_{i}
primary marked points on the
i
-th irregular boundary.

2.1
Discrete Fundamental Groupoid

Now, let
(\Sigma,P)
be a surface with marked boundary such that
P\neq\emptyset
.

Definition 2.6
.

The
discrete fundamental groupoid
of a surface with marked boundary
(\Sigma,P)
is the restriction of the fundamental groupoid
\Pi_{1}(\Sigma)
to
P
:

\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\Pi_{1}(\Sigma)\big|_{P}={\mathrm{s}}^{-1}(P)\cap{\mathrm{t}}^{-1}(P)\subset\Pi_{1}(\Sigma).

Note that the restricted fundamental groupoid
\Pi_{1}(\Sigma)\big|_{P}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}={\mathrm{s}}^{-1}(P)\cap{\mathrm{t}}^{-1}(P)
makes sense as a groupoid over any subset
P\subset\Sigma
, not necessarily finite.
However, our interest lies with surfaces with marked boundary: the upshot is that, just as every surface has a naturally associated
continuous
fundamental groupoid, every surface with marked boundary has a naturally associated
discrete
fundamental groupoid.

We call
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)

discrete
because, in contrast to the
continuous
fundamental groupoid
\Pi_{1}(\Sigma)
, it has finitely many objects
P
and any two objects are related by a countable collection of arrows.
More accurately, the subspace topology on
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)
inherited from
\Pi_{1}(\Sigma)
is the discrete topology.
Even more importantly, as we argue next, the discrete fundamental groupoid
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)
is a
finitely-generated groupoid
, which means we can find a finite collection of paths
\gamma_{i}\in\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)
such that any path
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)
can be written as a finite composition of the
\gamma_{i}
.

Remark 2.7
.

The discrete fundamental groupoid
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)
has been considered by Li–Bland-Ševera
[
25
, §4]
who denoted it by
\Pi_{1}(\Sigma,\mathnormal{V})
, and by Chekhov-Mazzocco-Rubtsov
[
11
, §4]
who called it the “fundamental groupoid of arcs” and denoted it by
\mathfrak{U}
.
It also appears in Dal Martello-Mazzocco
[
12
, §3]
who denote it by
\pi_{\mathfrak{a}}(\Sigma_{g,s,m})
.

Lemma 2.8
.

For surface with marked boundary
(\Sigma,P)
, where
\Sigma
has genus
g
and
s
holes, the discrete fundamental groupoid
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)
is finitely generated by
2g+s+|P|-1
elements and one relation.
Moreover, if
s\geq 1
, then
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)
is freely generated by
{2g+s+|P|-2}
elements.
Any choice of such free generators determines (up to isotopy) a graph
\Gamma
embedded into
\Sigma
with vertices
P
such that the discrete fundamental groupoid
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)
is isomorphic to the free groupoid
\Pi\Gamma\rightrightarrows P
generated by
\Gamma
.

Proof.

Select any point
p_{0}\in P
.
Recall that the fundamental group
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p_{0})
is finitely generated by
2g+s
elements satisfying one relation, and that it is in fact isomorphic to the free group on
2g+s-1
generators whenever
s\geq 1
.
Let us take any such generating set.
Next, for every point
p\in P\smallsetminus p_{0}
, choose a path from
p_{0}
to
p_{1}
.
There are a total of
|P|-1
such paths.
Then the resulting collection generates
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)
and satisfies one relation.
∎

2.1.1
Finite Presentation

We now give a step-by-step prescription of choosing a convenient finite generating set for the discrete fundamental groupoid
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)
of any surface with marked boundary
(\Sigma,P)
.
This procedure specialises directly to the classical setting without marked points described in
subsection 1.5
by simply choosing the base point of the fundamental group
p_{0}
to lie on a boundary of
\Sigma
.

Informally speaking, we first select the
main
basepoint
p_{0}\in P
.
Then we select one distinguished marked point from each marked boundary circle and choose paths to connect them to the main basepoint
p_{0}
.
Then the basis of generators is made up of these paths as well as all the boundary segments connecting the primary marked points, all the loops based at the main basepoint that go round the un-marked boundaries, and finally the loops that represent the
A
- and
B
-cycles.
More precisely, our recipe is as follows; see
Figure 1
.

Let
(\Sigma,P)
be a surface with marked boundary of type
({\bf s},{\bf m})
, then we make the following choices.

Figure 1
:
In the top picture the main point is a primary point. In the bottom one, the main point is a secondary point.

1.

Select any main basepoint
p_{0}\in P
and choose any collection of
2g
oriented loops
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{g},\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{g}
based at
p_{0}
that determine a basis of
A
- and
B
-cycles of
\Sigma
.

2.

Enumerate all simple boundary circles as
1,\ldots,l
.
For each
i=1,\ldots,l
, let
\gamma_{i}
be a positively-oriented loop based at
p_{0}
going round only the
i
-th unmarked boundary circle; i.e.,
\gamma_{i}
belongs to the conjugacy class in
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p_{0})
determined by the
i
-th unmarked boundary component.

3.

Enumerate all regular boundary circles as
1,\ldots,r
, and label the corresponding secondary marked points as
p_{1},\ldots,p_{r}
.
Let
\delta_{i}
be the positively-oriented boundary loop based at
p_{i}
, for each
i=1,\ldots,r
.

4.

For each
i=1,\ldots,r
, choose a path
d_{i}
with source
p_{0}
and target
p_{i}
.
In addition, if
p_{0}\in P_{\mathrm{II}}
, ensure that
p_{1}=p_{0}
and that
d_{1}=1
is the constant path at
p_{0}
.

5.

Enumerate all irregular boundary circles as
1,\ldots,d
.
For each
i=1,\ldots,d
, select one distinguished marked point on the
i
-th irregular boundary circle and call it
p_{i,1}
.
Let
m_{i}\geq 1
be the number of irregular marked points on the
i
-th irregular boundary circle, so that
m_{1}+\ldots+m_{d}=m
.
Enumerate all the
m_{i}
marked points belonging to this boundary circle as
p_{i,j}
for
j=1,\ldots,m_{i}
in a consecutive fashion starting with
p_{i,1}
and following the positive orientation of the boundary circle.
Let
\delta_{i,j}
be the positively-oriented boundary segment with source
p_{i,j}
and target
p_{i,j+1}
, where
j
is understood mod
m_{i}+1
.

6.

For each
i=1,\ldots,d
, choose a path
c_{i}
with source
p_{0}
and target
p_{i,1}
.
In addition, if
p_{0}\in P_{\mathrm{I}}
, ensure that
p_{1,1}=p_{0}
and that
c_{1}=1
is the constant path at
p_{0}
.

In total, this procedure selects
2g+s+r+m-1
paths
\alpha_{i},\beta_{i},\gamma_{i},\delta_{i},\delta_{i,j},c_{i},d_{i}
which we claim generate the discrete fundamental groupoid
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)
.
To see this, note if we (right) conjugate each boundary loop
\delta_{i}
by the connecting path
d_{i}
, we get a positively-oriented loop based at
p_{0}
going round the
i
-th regular boundary circle.
If we concatenate the boundary segments
\delta_{i,j}
into the boundary circle for each
i
, and then (right) conjugate by the connecting path
c_{i}
, we likewise obtain a positively-oriented loop based at
p_{0}
going round the
i
-th irregular boundary circle.
More precisely, let us introduce the following notation:

\displaystyle\gamma_{i}^{\mathrm{II}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=d_{i}^{-1}\delta_{i}^{\mathrm{II}}d_{i}~,\hskip 20.00003pt

\displaystyle\delta^{\mathrm{II}}_{i}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\delta_{i}~,\hskip 20.00003pt

\displaystyle i

\displaystyle=1,\ldots,r;

\displaystyle\gamma_{i}^{\mathrm{I}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=c_{i}^{-1}\delta_{i}^{\mathrm{I}}c_{i}~,\hskip 20.00003pt

\displaystyle\delta_{i}^{\mathrm{I}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\delta_{i,m_{i}}\cdots\delta_{i,1}~,\hskip 20.00003pt

\displaystyle i

\displaystyle=1,\ldots,d.

We find that the above procedure in particular yields
2g+s
loops
\alpha_{i},\beta_{i},\gamma_{i},\gamma^{\mathrm{II}}_{i}
,
\gamma^{\mathrm{I}}_{i}
that generate the fundamental group
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p_{0})
and satisfy the relation

\prod_{i=1}^{\begin{subarray}{c}g\\
\longleftarrow\end{subarray}}[\alpha_{i},\beta_{i}]\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{\begin{subarray}{c}l\\
\longleftarrow\end{subarray}}\gamma_{i}\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{\begin{subarray}{c}r\\
\longleftarrow\end{subarray}}\gamma_{i}^{\mathrm{II}}\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{\begin{subarray}{c}d\\
\longleftarrow\end{subarray}}\gamma_{i}^{\mathrm{I}}=1.

(2.1)

More explicitly in terms of only the generators, this relation reads

\prod_{i=1}^{\begin{subarray}{c}g\\
\longleftarrow\end{subarray}}[\alpha_{i},\beta_{i}]\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{\begin{subarray}{c}l\\
\longleftarrow\end{subarray}}\gamma_{i}\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{\begin{subarray}{c}r\\
\longleftarrow\end{subarray}}d_{i}^{-1}\delta_{i}d_{i}\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{\begin{subarray}{c}d\\
\longleftarrow\end{subarray}}c_{i}^{-1}\left(\prod_{j=1}^{\begin{subarray}{c}m_{i}\\
\longleftarrow\end{subarray}}\delta_{i,j}\right)c_{i}=1.

(2.2)

Observe that this relation can be solved uniquely for any of
\gamma_{i}
,
\delta_{i}
,
\delta_{i,j}
, but not for any of
\alpha_{i}
,
\beta_{i}
,
c_{i}
,
d_{i}
.
Therefore, we can destroy this relation by excluding any one of the paths
\gamma_{i}
,
\delta_{i}
,
\delta_{i,j}
from the list of generators.
Moreover, we have one extra relation
because one of the paths
c_{i}
or
d_{i}
is a loop based at
p_{0}
which, without loss of generality, we may take from the outset to be the constant path at
p_{0}
as suggested in the above procedure.
Indeed, suppose for definiteness that
c_{1}
is the loop at
p_{0}
.
Then we can change the chosen generators to be such that
c_{1}=1
: we (left) conjugate every path
\alpha_{i},\beta_{i},\gamma_{i}
by
c_{1}
, right multiply every path
c_{i},d_{i}
by
c_{1}^{-1}
, and leave all
\delta_{i},\delta_{i,j}
alone.
Notice that all the loops
\gamma_{i},\gamma_{i}^{\mathrm{II}},\gamma_{i}^{\mathrm{I}}
and each commutator
[\alpha_{i},\beta_{i}]
get (left) conjugated by
c_{1}
.
This means the lefthand side in (
2.1
) gets conjugated by
c_{1}
, leaving the relation unchanged.
We summarise this discussion as follows.

Lemma 2.9
.

Let
(\Sigma,P)
be any surface with marked boundary
of genus
g
with
s\geq 1
boundary circles, of which
l\geq 0
are unmarked,
r\geq 0
are regular and
d\geq 0
are irregular. Let
m=|P_{{\mathrm{I}}}|
.
Select any
p_{0}\in P
.
Then the above procedure determines a finite presentation of the discrete fundamental groupoid
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)
with
2g+s+r+m-1
generators and one relation:

\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)\cong\left\langle\alpha_{i},\beta_{i},\gamma_{i},\delta_{i},\delta_{i,j},c_{i},d_{i},\ell_{i}~\Big|~\text{ relation \eqref{251109164128}}\right\rangle.

(2.3)

Mind that
c_{1}=1
if
p_{0}\in P_{\mathrm{I}}
and
d_{1}=1
if
p_{0}\in P_{\mathrm{II}}
.
In particular, by excluding any one of the paths
\gamma_{i},\delta_{i},\delta_{i,j}
from the list of generators in either case gives a finite presentation with
2g+s+r+m-2
and no relations.

3
Decorated Local Systems

In this section, we introduce various notions of local systems, which we collectively call
decorated local systems
, and we discuss the relationships between them.

We will sometimes write
({\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{F}},\star)
for a decorated local system with
\star=\left\{\star_{p}:p\in P\right\}
where
\star_{p}
stands for the intended decoration on the local filtration
{\mathcal{F}}_{p}
, including blank (or empty) decoration.
Correspondingly, we use a uniform notation
\textup{Loc}_{G}^{\square}
for the groupoids and
\mathfrak{Loc}_{G}^{\square}
for the corresponding moduli spaces of decorated local systems where
\square
indicates the type of decoration; e.g.,
{\mathrm{Fi}}
for filtered local systems,
{\mathrm{Fr}}
for framed local systems and
{\mathrm{PFr}}
for projectively framed local systems.

3.1
Filtered Local Systems

The purpose of decorating a surface with marked points is to keep track of allowing to decorate local systems with additional local linear-algebraic data, the most fundamental of which are flags.

Recall that a
flag

F
, by which we shall always mean a
complete flag
, on an
n
-dimensional complex vector space
E
is a nested collection of
n
distinct subspaces

F=\big(0=F_{0}\subset F_{1}\subset\cdots\subset F_{n}=E\big)\hskip 10.00002pt\text{where}\hskip 10.00002pt\dim F_{i}=i.

We refer to a pair
(E,F)
as a
filtered vector space
.
A
filtered map

\varphi:(E,F)\to(E^{\prime},F^{\prime})
is a linear map
\varphi:E\to E^{\prime}
that respects the flags in the sense that
\varphi(F_{i})\subset F^{\prime}_{i}
.

Let
(\Sigma,P)
be a decorated surface.
For every primary marked point
p\in P_{\mathrm{I}}
, let
U_{p}\subset\Sigma
be any sectorial or arc neighbourhood of
p
; i.e., a contractible open subset of respectively
\Sigma
or
\partial\Sigma
containing
p
.
For every secondary marked point
p\in P_{\mathrm{II}}
, let
U_{p}\subset\Sigma
be either the boundary circle
C_{p}\subset\partial\Sigma
containing
p
or any annular neighbourhood of
C_{p}
; i.e., an open subset of
\Sigma
that contracts onto
C_{p}
.

If
{\mathcal{E}}
is a local system on
\Sigma
, let us denote its restriction to a local system on the subset
U_{p}
for each
p\in P
by

{\mathcal{E}}_{p}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}={\mathcal{E}}|_{U_{p}}.

This notation is not to be confused with the stalk of
{\mathcal{E}}
at
p
which we denote by
E_{p}
.
If
p\in P_{\mathrm{I}}
, then
U_{p}
is contractible onto
p
and so there is a canonical isomorphism
\Gamma(U_{p},{\mathcal{E}}_{p})\cong E_{p}
.
However, if
p\in P_{\mathrm{II}}
, then
U_{p}
is a non-contractible loop and so there is no such isomorphism in general; in fact,
\Gamma(U_{p},{\mathcal{E}}_{p})
is typically the zero vector space.

Definition 3.1
.

A
filtered local system
on a surface with marked boundary
(\Sigma,P)
is a pair
({\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{F}})
consisting of a local system
{\mathcal{E}}
on
\Sigma
endowed with a collection
{\mathcal{F}}=\left\{{\mathcal{F}}_{p}:p\in P\right\}
where
{\mathcal{F}}_{p}
is a
filtration
near
p
.
That is, for any sectorial neighbourhood
U_{p}
of
p
,

{\mathcal{F}}_{p}
is a nested collection of
n
distinct local subsystems of the restricted local system
{\mathcal{E}}_{p}
over
U_{p}
, called a
local filtration
near
p
:

{\mathcal{F}}_{p}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\big(0={\mathcal{F}}_{p}^{0}\subset{\mathcal{F}}_{p}^{1}\subset\cdots\subset{\mathcal{F}}_{p}^{n}={\mathcal{E}}_{p}\big)\hskip 10.00002pt\text{where}\hskip 10.00002pt\operatorname{rank}({\mathcal{F}}_{p}^{i})=i.

A
filtered morphism

\varphi:({\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{F}})\to({\mathcal{E}}^{\prime},{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime})
between filtered local systems on
(\Sigma,P)
is a morphism of sheaves
\varphi:{\mathcal{E}}\to{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime}
whose restriction
\varphi_{p}:{\mathcal{E}}_{p}\to{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime}_{p}
to each subset
U_{p}
respects the local filtrations in the sense that
\varphi_{p}({\mathcal{F}}_{p}^{i})\subset{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime i}_{p}
for every
i
.
It is an isomorphism whenever
\varphi:{\mathcal{E}}\to{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime}
is.
We denote the groupoid and the corresponding moduli space of filtered local systems of rank
n
on
(\Sigma,P)
with filtered isomorphisms by

\textup{Loc}_{G}^{\mathrm{Fi}}(\Sigma,P)\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\mathfrak{Loc}_{G}^{\mathrm{Fi}}(\Sigma,P)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\textup{Loc}_{G}^{\mathrm{Fi}}(\Sigma,P)\big/{\sim}.

Notice that this definition specialises to the classical situation in the absence of marked points:
\textup{Loc}^{\mathrm{Fi}}_{G}(\Sigma,\emptyset)=\textup{Loc}_{G}(\Sigma)
.
Also, notice that any morphism of filtered local systems is in particular a morphism of local systems, so for any
P^{\prime}\subsetneq P
there is a forgetful functor that drops all local filtrations near the forgotten marked points
P\smallsetminus P^{\prime}
:

\textup{Loc}_{G}^{\mathrm{Fi}}(\Sigma,P)\to\textup{Loc}_{G}^{\mathrm{Fi}}(\Sigma,P^{\prime}).

This functor is surjective on objects and faithful on morphisms but never full.

We remark that in the literature, it is common to encounter local systems decorated by local filtrations only near the primary marked points.
Although the
