\textup{Loc}_{G}^{\mathrm{Fi}}(\Sigma,P)\to\textup{Loc}_{G}^{\mathrm{Fi}}(\Sigma,P_{\mathrm{I}})
fails to be an equivalence, the nature of this failure turns out to be finite up to isomorphism; cf.
subsection 5.2
.

Next, we give a useful alternative characterisation of the groupoid of filtered local systems which concentrates all the filtration information to the stalks at marked points.

Lemma 3.2
.

A filtered local system
({\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{F}})
on a surface with marked boundary
(\Sigma,P)
is equivalently a pair
({\mathcal{E}},F)
consisting of a local system
{\mathcal{E}}
on
\Sigma
endowed with a collection
F=\left\{F_{p}:p\in P\right\}
where each
F_{p}
is a flag in the stalk
E_{p}
of
{\mathcal{E}}
at
p
with the following property.
For every secondary marked point
p\in P_{\mathrm{II}}
, the flag
F_{p}
is invariant under the local monodromy
\operatorname{mon}_{\gamma}
of
{\mathcal{E}}
along the boundary loop
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p)
based at
p
; i.e,
\operatorname{mon}_{\gamma}\in\operatorname{\mathsf{Aut}}(E_{p},F_{p})
.

Similarly, a morphism
\varphi:({\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{F}})\to({\mathcal{E}}^{\prime},{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime})
of filtered local systems on
(\Sigma,P)
is equivalently a morphism
\varphi:({\mathcal{E}},F)\to({\mathcal{E}}^{\prime},F^{\prime})
which is defined as a morphism of sheaves
\varphi:{\mathcal{E}}\to{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime}
whose restriction to the stalks at every marked point
p\in P
is a filtered linear map
\varphi_{p}:(E_{p},F_{p})\to(E^{\prime}_{p},F^{\prime}_{p})
.

In other words, the groupoid
\textup{Loc}_{G}^{\mathrm{Fi}}(\Sigma,P)
of filtered local systems on a surface with marked boundary
(\Sigma,P)
is isomorphic to the groupoid parameterising the pairs
({\mathcal{E}},F)
as above.

Namely,
F_{p}
is the stalk of
{\mathcal{F}}
at
p
; conversely,
{\mathcal{F}}
is the parallel transport of
F_{p}
to every point of
U_{p}
.
Consequently,
Definition 3.1
is independent of the size of the neighbourhoods
U_{p}

From now on, we use these characterisations of
\textup{Loc}_{G}^{\mathrm{Fi}}(\Sigma,P)
interchangeably.

Proof.

For any
p\in P
, the local filtration
{\mathcal{F}}_{p}
of a filtered local system
({\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{F}})
determines a flag in the stalk of
{\mathcal{E}}
at every point of
U_{p}
; in particular, it determines a flag
F_{p}
in the stalk
E_{p}
of
{\mathcal{E}}
at
p\in U_{p}
.
Moreover, since
{\mathcal{F}}_{p}
is a filtration by local subsystems of
{\mathcal{E}}_{p}
, the holonomy of
{\mathcal{E}}
along paths contained in
U_{p}
defines a filtered isomorphism between any two stalks and in particular a filtered isomorphism with the filtered stalk
(E_{p},F_{p})
.
Notably, this means the flag
F_{p}
at any secondary marked point
p\in P_{\mathrm{II}}
is invariant under the local monodromy of
{\mathcal{E}}
around the boundary loop based at
p
.
We stress, however, that the flags
F_{p}
at any primary marked point
p\in P_{\mathrm{I}}
may not be invariant under the local monodromy: their purpose is only to constrain the possible morphisms of decorated local systems.

Conversely, a flag
F_{p}
in
E_{p}
determines the local filtration
{\mathcal{F}}_{p}
over
U_{p}
by using the holonomy of
{\mathcal{E}}
to transport
F_{p}
to every stalk of
{\mathcal{E}}
along
U_{p}
.
When
p\in P_{\mathrm{II}}
is a secondary marked point, this local filtration
{\mathcal{F}}_{p}
is well-defined if and only if the flag
F_{p}
is invariant under the local monodromy.
∎

Remark 3.3
.

Our notion of
filtered local systems
(
Definition 3.1
) corresponds to what several authors call “framed local systems”.
This includes Fock-Goncharov
[
15
, Def 1.2]
, Goncharov
[
19
]
, Allegretti
[
2
, Def 3.4]
, Goncharov-Shen
[
20
, §3]
, Allegretti-Bridgeland
[
1
, §4]
, as well as Dal Martello-Mazzocco
[
12
, §3]
amongst others.
It also corresponds to what Jordan-Le-Schrader-Shapiro
[
24
, Def 1.6]
call “decorated local systems”.

In the case of a surface with marked boundary
(\Sigma,P)
with at least one marked point and no unmarked boundaries,
our moduli space
\mathfrak{Loc}_{G}^{\mathrm{Fi}}(\Sigma,P)
corresponds to the moduli space
{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathnormal{G},\hat{\mathnormal{S}}}
in the notation of
[
15
, Def 1.2]
, to
{\mathcal{X}}_{{\mathrm{G}},\mathbb{S}}
in the notation of
[
20
]
, to
{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbb{S},\mathbb{M})
in the notation of
[
1
, §4]
, and to
{\mathrm{Ch}}(\mathbb{S})
in the notation of
[
24
, Def 1.8]
.
In contrast, we reserve the symbol “
\mathfrak{X}
” for the decorated character stack/variety introduced below in
subsection 6.3
, which we will show is isomorphic to
\mathfrak{Loc}_{G}^{\mathrm{Fi}}(\Sigma,P)
but which is defined independently.

3.2
Framed Local Systems

The local filtrations of filtered local systems are often further decorated by linear-algebraic data, the most important of which is a basis adapted to the local filtration.
First, let us recall some important definitions.

A
frame
on a filtered vector space
(E,F)
of dimension
n
is an isomorphism
\beta:E\overset{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.35623pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{C}^{n}
which takes the flag
F
to the standard flag
\mathbb{C}^{\bullet}
meaning
\beta(F_{i})=\mathbb{C}^{i}
for every
i
.
In short, it is a filtered isomorphism
\beta:(E,F)\overset{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.35623pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}{\longrightarrow}(\mathbb{C}^{n},\mathbb{C}^{\bullet})
.
Equivalently, it is a choice of basis
\beta=(e_{1},\ldots,e_{n})
of
E
adapted to the flag
F
in the sense that
F_{i}=\left\langle e_{1},\ldots,e_{i}\right\rangle
.
We call
(E,F,\beta)
a
framed vector space
.

For example, the standard flag
\mathbb{C}^{\bullet}
on
\mathbb{C}^{n}
carries the
standard frame
, denoted by
\mathds{1}
, given by the identity map
\mathrm{id}:\mathbb{C}^{n}\to\mathbb{C}^{n}
or equivalently by the standard basis of
\mathbb{C}^{n}
.

Actually, the information of the flag
F
in a framed vector space is superfluous as it is fully determined by the frame
\beta
.
So we may equally well say that a framed vector space is a pair
(E,\beta)
consisting of a vector space equipped with an isomorphism
\beta:E\overset{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.35623pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{C}^{n}
.

A
unipotent isomorphism

\varphi:(E,F,\beta)\to(E^{\prime},F^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})
between two framed vector spaces is a linear isomorphism
\varphi:E\overset{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.35623pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}{\longrightarrow}E^{\prime}
whose matrix representation in the two frames is a unipotent upper-triangular matrix
\beta^{\prime}\circ\varphi\circ\beta^{-1}\in U
.
In contrast, a
strict isomorphism

\varphi:(E,F,\beta)\to(E^{\prime},F^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})
between two framed vector spaces is a linear isomorphism such that
\beta=\beta^{\prime}\circ\varphi
; i.e., the matrix representation of
\varphi
is the identity matrix.
A strict isomorphism is necessarily unipotent, and a unipotent isomorphism is necessarily filtered.

Definition 3.4
.

Let
({\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{F}})
be any filtered local system on a surface with marked boundary
(\Sigma,P)
.
A
local frame
on
({\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{F}})
at
p\in P
is a filtered trivialisation in the stalk at
p
; i.e., a filtered isomorphism
\beta_{p}:({\mathcal{E}}_{p},{\mathcal{F}}_{p})\overset{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.35623pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}{\longrightarrow}(\mathbb{C}^{n},\mathbb{C}^{\bullet})
.
A
frame
on
({\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{F}})
over
P
is a collection
\beta=\left\{\beta_{p}:p\in P\right\}
of local frames, one at each marked point.
Any such triple
({\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{F}},\beta)
is called a
framed local system
on
(\Sigma,P)
.

Each local frame
\beta_{p}
is equivalently a basis
\beta_{p}=(e_{1},\ldots,e_{n})
of the vector space
E_{p}
adapted to the flag
F_{p}
; i.e.,
F_{p}^{i}=\left\langle e_{1},\ldots,e_{i}\right\rangle
.
It also extends by parallel transport to a trivialisation of the restricted filtered local system
\beta_{p}:({\mathcal{E}}_{p},{\mathcal{F}}_{p})\overset{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.35623pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}{\longrightarrow}(\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{n},\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{\bullet})
over any sectorial or arc neighbourhood
U_{p}\subset\Sigma
of
p
.
Namely, the basis
\beta_{p}
of
E_{p}
extends to a basis
\beta_{p}=(e_{1},\ldots,e_{n})
of sections of the local system
{\mathcal{E}}
over
U_{p}
; i.e.,
e_{i}\in\Gamma(U_{p},{\mathcal{E}})
.
This basis of sections is adapted to the local filtration
{\mathcal{F}}_{p}
in the sense that
\Gamma(U_{p},{\mathcal{F}}_{p})=\left\langle e_{1},\ldots,e_{i}\right\rangle
.
But beware that, if
p
is a secondary marked point, the local frame
\beta_{p}:(E_{p},F_{p})\overset{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.35623pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}{\longrightarrow}(\mathbb{C}^{n},\mathbb{C}^{\bullet})
does
not
in general extend to a trivialisation of the filtered local system
({\mathcal{E}}_{p},{\mathcal{F}}_{p})
over the boundary circle based at
p
or any annular neighbourhood of it.

Definition 3.5
.

A
unipotent isomorphism

\varphi:({\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{F}},\beta)\to({\mathcal{E}}^{\prime},{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})
between two framed local systems is an isomorphism of the underlying filtered local systems such that each restriction
\varphi_{p}:(E_{p},F_{p})\to(E^{\prime}_{p},F^{\prime}_{p})
is unipotent in the sense that its matrix representation with respect to the given frames is a unipotent upper-triangular matrix:
\beta^{\prime}_{p}\circ\varphi_{p}\circ\beta^{-1}_{p}\in U\subset G
.
We denote the groupoid and the corresponding moduli space of framed local systems of rank
n
on
(\Sigma,P)
with unipotent isomorphisms by

\textup{Loc}_{G}^{\mathrm{Fr}}(\Sigma,P)\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\mathfrak{Loc}_{G}^{\mathrm{Fr}}(\Sigma,P)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\textup{Loc}_{G}^{\mathrm{Fr}}(\Sigma,P)\big/{\sim}.

In other words, a unipotent isomorphism
\varphi
takes each local frame
\beta_{p}
to the local frame
\beta^{\prime}_{p}
up to a unipotent upper-triangular matrix.
Sometimes, the isomorphisms between framed local systems are restricted further to preserve the frames on the nose as follows.

Definition 3.6
.

A
strict isomorphism

\varphi:({\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{F}},\beta)\to({\mathcal{E}}^{\prime},{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})
between two framed local systems over a surface with marked boundary
(\Sigma,P)
is an isomorphism
\varphi:{\mathcal{E}}\overset{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.35623pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}{\longrightarrow}{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime}
of the underlying local systems which relates the frame in the sense that
\beta=\beta^{\prime}\circ\varphi
.
We denote the groupoid and the corresponding moduli space of framed local systems of rank
n
on
(\Sigma,P)
with strict isomorphisms by

\textup{Loc}_{G}^{\mathrm{sFr}}(\Sigma,P)\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\mathfrak{Loc}_{G}^{\mathrm{sFr}}(\Sigma,P)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\textup{Loc}_{G}^{\mathrm{sFr}}(\Sigma,P)\big/{\sim}.

When referring to a framed local system
({\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{F}},\beta)
as an object in this groupoid, we will call it a
strictly framed local system
on
(\Sigma,P)
.

Any framed local system has an underlying filtered local system, and any isomorphism of framed local systems is in particular an isomorphism of filtered local systems.
Similarly, any strictly framed local system is in particular a framed local system, and any strict isomorphism is unipotent.
Thus, there is a chain of forgetful functors

\textup{Loc}_{G}^{\mathrm{sFr}}(\Sigma,P)\to\textup{Loc}_{G}^{\mathrm{Fr}}(\Sigma,P)\to\textup{Loc}_{G}^{\mathrm{Fi}}(\Sigma,P).

Any filtered local system can always be promoted to a framed local system by choosing local frames arbitrarily, whilst the objects of
\textup{Loc}_{G}^{\mathrm{sFr}}
are literally the same as those of
\textup{Loc}_{G}^{\mathrm{Fr}}
.
Consequently, these functors are surjective on objects and faithful on morphisms but never full unless
P
is empty: the groupoid
\textup{Loc}_{G}^{\mathrm{sFr}}
has far fewer isomorphisms than
\textup{Loc}_{G}^{\mathrm{Fr}}
which itself has fewer isomorphisms than
\textup{Loc}_{G}^{\mathrm{Fi}}
.
This is analogous to how the identity matrix is in particular a unipotent upper-triangular matrix which is in particular an upper-triangular matrix.
As a result, the first forgetful functor is actually an embedding.
On the other hand, although there are many possible ways to embed the objects of
\textup{Loc}_{G}^{\mathrm{Fi}}
into the objects of
\textup{Loc}_{G}^{\mathrm{Fr}}
, it is not possible to embed the groupoid
\textup{Loc}_{G}^{\mathrm{Fi}}
into
\textup{Loc}_{G}^{\mathrm{Fr}}
.

Remark 3.7
.

Our terminology
framed local systems
(
Definition 3.4
) is more in keeping with the well-established terminology from differential geometry where traditionally a “frame” at a point is an ordered basis in the fibre of a given vector bundle.
This also closely matches the use of this word by other authors including Boalch
[
5
,
6
,
4
]
, Hiroe-Yamakawa
[
22
]
, and Jordan-Le-Schrader-Shapiro
[
24
, Def 1.8]
.
Likewise, our terminology
filtered local systems
closely matches the similar terminology used by Deligne
[
13
]
, Malgrange
[
26
, §4]
, Simpson
[
32
, §3]
, Sabbah
[
29
,
30
]
, Boalch
[
4
]
, Huang-Sun
[
23
, Def 3.3]
, and many others.

Flags endowed with a basis for the associated graded are sometimes called
decorated flags

[
20
, §3.1.2]
or
affine flags

[
15
, p.41]
, although the latter term is also commonly used for flags in infinite-dimensional vector spaces.
On the other hand, we use the word “decorated” as a collective adjective to describe flags with extra structure instead.

3.3
Projectively Framed Local Systems

A
projective frame

\bar{\beta}
on a filtered vector space
(E,F)
is a frame defined up to a nonzero scalar multiple.
In other words, it is a basis
\beta=(e_{1},\ldots,e_{n})
of
E
adapted to the flag
F
but which is defined only up to overall rescaling; i.e.,
\beta=(e_{1},\ldots,e_{n})\sim(te_{1},\ldots,te_{n})=t\beta
for any
t\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
.
A
projectively unipotent map

\varphi:(E,F,\bar{\beta})\to(E^{\prime},F^{\prime},\bar{\beta}^{\prime})
is a unipotent map up to a scalar factor: i.e., the matrix representation of
\varphi
with respect to any two representative frames
\beta,\beta^{\prime}
is unipotent up to a nonzero scalar factor:
\beta^{\prime}\circ\varphi\circ\beta^{-1}\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}U
.

Definition 3.8
.

Let
({\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{F}})
be a filtered local system on a surface with marked boundary
(\Sigma,P)
.
A
local projective frame

\hat{\beta}_{p}
on
({\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{F}})
at
p\in P
is a local frame considered up to scale; i.e.,
\bar{\beta}_{p}
is an equivalence class of local frames
\beta_{p}:(E_{p},F_{p})\overset{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.35623pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}{\longrightarrow}(\mathbb{C}^{n},\mathbb{C}^{\bullet})
where
\beta_{p}\sim t\beta_{p}
for any
t\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
.
A
projective frame
on
({\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{F}})
is a collection
\bar{\beta}=\left\{\bar{\beta}_{p}:p\in P\right\}
of local projective frames.
Any such triple
({\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{F}},\bar{\beta})
is called a
projectively framed local system
.

A morphism
\varphi:({\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{F}},\bar{\beta})\to({\mathcal{E}}^{\prime},{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime},\bar{\beta}^{\prime})
of two projectively framed local systems, called a
projectively unipotent morphism
, is a filtered morphism
\varphi:({\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{F}})\to({\mathcal{E}}^{\prime},{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime})
whose matrix representation with respect to any representative frames
\beta,\beta^{\prime}
is projectively unipotent:
\beta^{\prime}\circ\varphi\circ\beta^{-1}\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}U
.
We denote the groupoid and the corresponding moduli space of projectively framed local systems of rank
n
on
(\Sigma,P)
with projectively unipotent morphisms by

\textup{Loc}_{G}^{\mathrm{PFr}}(\Sigma,P)\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\mathfrak{Loc}_{G}^{\mathrm{PFr}}(\Sigma,P)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\textup{Loc}_{G}^{\mathrm{PFr}}(\Sigma,P)\big/{\sim}.

4
Decorated Groupoid Representations

In this section we reformulate decorated local systems as
decorated
representations of the fundamental groupoid and the discrete fundamental groupoid.

4.1
Filtered Groupoid Representations

Let
(\Sigma,P)
be a decorated surface.
As we did at the start of
subsection 3.1
, for every primary marked point
p\in P_{\mathrm{I}}
, let
U_{p}\subset\Sigma
be any sectorial or arc neighbourhood of
p
.
For every secondary marked point
p\in P_{\mathrm{II}}
, let
U_{p}\subset\Sigma
be either the boundary circle
C_{p}\subset\partial\Sigma
containing
p
or any annular neighbourhood of
C_{p}
.

If
\mathnormal{E}
is a vector bundle on
\Sigma
, let us denote its restriction to a vector bundle over the subset
U_{p}
for each
p\in P
by

\mathnormal{E}_{p}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\mathnormal{E}|_{U_{p}}.

This notation is not to be confused with the fibre of
\mathnormal{E}
at
p
which we denote by
E_{p}
.

Back in
subsection 1.3
(specifically,
Definition 1.3
), we recalled the notion of representations of the fundamental groupoid
\Pi_{1}(\Sigma)\rightrightarrows\Sigma
.
We now enhance this notion by introducing local decorations near the marked points in the form of filtrations which are locally preserved by the representation.

Definition 4.1
.

A
filtered representation
of the fundamental groupoid
\Pi_{1}(\Sigma)
of a surface with marked boundary
(\Sigma,P)
is a triple
(\mathnormal{E},\mathnormal{F},\Phi)
consisting of a representation
(\mathnormal{E},\Phi)
of the fundamental groupoid
\Pi_{1}(\Sigma)
endowed with a collection
\mathnormal{F}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\left\{\mathnormal{F}_{p}:p\in P\right\}
of
flat local filtrations
.
That is, for any sectorial neighbourhood
U_{p}
of
p
,
\mathnormal{F}_{p}
is a nested collection of
n
distinct vector subbundles of the restricted vector bundle
\mathnormal{E}_{p}
over
U_{p}
,

\mathnormal{F}_{p}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\big(0=\mathnormal{F}_{p}^{0}\subset\mathnormal{F}_{p}^{1}\subset\cdots\subset\mathnormal{F}_{p}^{n}=\mathnormal{E}_{p}\big)\hskip 10.00002pt\text{where}\hskip 10.00002pt\operatorname{rank}(\mathnormal{F}_{p}^{i})=i,

which is
flat
with respect to
\Phi
in the following sense.
For every path
\gamma:[0,1]\to U_{p}
, the groupoid homomorphism
\Phi
induces a filtered isomorphism between the corresponding fibres of the vector bundle
\mathnormal{E}_{p}
:

\Phi_{\gamma}:(\mathnormal{E}_{p},\mathnormal{F}_{p})\big|_{\gamma(0)}\overset{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.35623pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}{\longrightarrow}(\mathnormal{E}_{p},\mathnormal{F}_{p})\big|_{\gamma(1)}.

We stress that in this definition, the homomorphism
\Phi:\Pi_{1}(\Sigma)\to\operatorname{\mathsf{GL}}(\mathnormal{E})
is
not
assumed to relate in any way the local filtrations near different marked points.
These filtrations are merely local decorations on a groupoid representation in the ordinary sense.
In light of this, perhaps a more appropriate name would be “locally filtered representations” or “filtration-decorated representations”, but we prefer the shorter one.
The primary purpose of these filtrations is instead to constrain the set of possible morphisms between the given representations as defined next.

Definition 4.2
.

A
filtered morphism

\varphi:(\mathnormal{E},\mathnormal{F},\Phi)\to(\mathnormal{E}^{\prime},\mathnormal{F}^{\prime},\Phi^{\prime})
of two filtered representations of
\Pi_{1}(\Sigma)
on
(\Sigma,P)
is a morphism
\varphi:(\mathnormal{E},\Phi)\to(\mathnormal{E}^{\prime},\Phi^{\prime})
of the underlying representations which restricts over every
U_{p}
to a map of filtered vector bundles
\varphi_{p}:(\mathnormal{E}_{p},\mathnormal{F}_{p})\to(\mathnormal{E}^{\prime}_{p},\mathnormal{F}^{\prime}_{p})
.
We denote the groupoid and the corresponding moduli space of filtered representations of rank
n
on
(\Sigma,P)
with filtered isomorphisms by

\textup{Rep}_{G}^{\mathrm{Fi}}\big(\Pi_{1}(\Sigma),P\big)\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\mathfrak{Rep}_{G}^{\mathrm{Fi}}\big(\Pi_{1}(\Sigma),P\big)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\textup{Rep}_{G}^{\mathrm{Fi}}\big(\Pi_{1}(\Sigma),P\big)\big/{\sim}.

Next, we give an alternative characterisation of decorated groupoid representations, analogous to
Lemma 3.2
for filtered local systems.

Lemma 4.3
.

A filtered representation
(\mathnormal{E},\mathnormal{F},\Phi)
of the fundamental groupoid
\Pi_{1}(\Sigma)
of a surface with marked boundary
(\Sigma,P)
is equivalently a triple
(\mathnormal{E},F,\Phi)
consisting of a representation
(\mathnormal{E},\Phi)
of
\Pi_{1}(\Sigma)
decorated by a collection
F\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\left\{F_{p}:p\in P\right\}
where
F_{p}
is a flag in the fibre
E_{p}
of
\mathnormal{E}
at
p
with the following addition property.
For every secondary marked point
p\in P_{\mathrm{II}}
, the flag
F_{p}
is invariant under the isomorphism
\Phi_{\gamma}:E_{p}\to E_{p}
where
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p)
is the boundary loop.

Again, it is worth stressing that the homomorphism
\Phi:\Pi_{1}(\Sigma)\to\operatorname{\mathsf{GL}}(\mathnormal{E})
is
not
assumed to relate in any way the local flags at different marked points.
The homomorphism
\Phi
is, so to speak, ‘ignorant’ of the flags
F_{p}
: if
\gamma
is any path between
p,p^{\prime}\in P
, we do
not
require the corresponding isomorphism
\Phi_{\gamma}:E_{p}\overset{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.35623pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}{\longrightarrow}E_{p^{\prime}}
to respect the flags
F_{p},F_{p^{\prime}}
.
The only exception to this are the flags at secondary marked points which must be invariant under the local monodromy.

Lemma 4.4
.

A morphism of two filtered representations of
\Pi_{1}(\Sigma)
on
(\Sigma,P)
is equivalently a morphism
\varphi:(\mathnormal{E},\Phi,F)\to(\mathnormal{E}^{\prime},\Phi^{\prime},F^{\prime})
consisting of a morphism of representations
\varphi:(\mathnormal{E},\Phi)\to(\mathnormal{E}^{\prime},\Phi^{\prime})
which is filtered at every marked point; i.e., the restriction of
\varphi
to any
p\in P
is a filtered map
\varphi_{p}:(E_{p},F_{p})\to(E^{\prime}_{p},F^{\prime}_{p})
.

The key fact about filtered representations of the fundamental groupoid is the following proposition regarding their close relationship with filtered local systems.

Proposition 4.5
.

The holonomy representation functor extends to an equivalence of categories between filtered local systems and filtered fundamental groupoid representations:

\operatorname{Hol}:\textup{Loc}_{G}^{\mathrm{Fi}}(\Sigma,P)\overset{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.35623pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}{\longrightarrow}\textup{Rep}_{G}^{\mathrm{Fi}}\big(\Pi_{1}(\Sigma),P\big).

It sends
({\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{F}})\mapsto(\mathnormal{E},\mathnormal{F},\Phi)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=({\mathcal{E}}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{\Sigma},{\mathcal{F}}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{\Sigma},\operatorname{hol})
.

Proof.

The holonomy representation functor
\operatorname{Hol}:\textup{Loc}_{G}(\Sigma)\overset{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.35623pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}{\longrightarrow}\textup{Rep}_{G}(\Sigma)
was explicitly constructed in
Proposition 1.4
.
Let
({\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{F}})
be a filtered local system.
The holonomy functor yields a representation
(\mathnormal{E},\Phi)=({\mathcal{E}}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{\Sigma},\operatorname{hol})
.
For every
p\in P
, the local filtration
{\mathcal{F}}_{p}
induces a local filtration
\mathnormal{F}_{p}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}={\mathcal{F}}_{p}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{U_{p}}
on the bundle
\mathnormal{E}_{p}
over
U_{p}
.
This filtration is flat because
\Phi
is the holonomy of
{\mathcal{E}}
and
{\mathcal{F}}_{p}
is invariant under the holonomy along paths in
U_{p}
.
If we regard
{\mathcal{F}}_{p}
as a sheaf on
\Sigma
with support on
U_{p}
, then we can simply write
\mathnormal{F}_{p}={\mathcal{F}}_{p}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{\Sigma}
as in the statement.
∎

As for filtered local systems, the above definitions specialise to the classical situation without marked points:
\textup{Rep}_{G}^{\mathrm{Fi}}\big(\Pi_{1}(\Sigma),\emptyset\big)=\textup{Rep}_{G}\big(\Pi_{1}(\Sigma)\big)
.

4.2
Framed and Projectively Framed Groupoid Representations

Definition 4.6
.

Let
(\mathnormal{E},\mathnormal{F},\Phi)
be a filtered representation of the fundamental groupoid
\Pi_{1}(\Sigma)
on a surface with marked boundary
(\Sigma,P)
.
A
local frame

\beta_{p}
on
(\mathnormal{E},\mathnormal{F},\Phi)
at
p\in P
is a filtered flat trivialisation at
p
; i.e., a filtered isomorphism
\beta_{p}:(E_{p},F_{p})\overset{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.35623pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}{\longrightarrow}(\mathbb{C}^{n},\mathbb{C}^{\bullet})
of vector bundles over
U_{p}
with the following property called
flatness
with respect to
\Phi
.
For every path
\gamma:[0,1]\to U_{p}
, the groupoid homomorphism
\Phi
satisfies

\beta_{p}\big|_{\gamma(1)}\circ\Phi_{\gamma}=\beta_{p}\big|_{\gamma(0)}.

A
frame
on
(\mathnormal{E},\mathnormal{F},\Phi)
is a collection
\beta=\left\{\beta_{p}:P\in P\right\}
of local frames, one at each marked point.
Any such triple
(\mathnormal{E},\mathnormal{F},\Phi)
is a called a
framed

representation
of the fundamental groupoid
\Pi_{1}(\Sigma)
on the surface with marked boundary
(\Sigma,P)
.

Definition 4.7
.

A
unipotent isomorphism

\varphi:(\mathnormal{E},\mathnormal{F},\Phi,\beta)\to(\mathnormal{E}^{\prime},\mathnormal{F}^{\prime},\Phi^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})
of two framed representations of the fundamental groupoid
\Pi_{1}(\Sigma)
on the surface with marked boundary
(\Sigma,P)
is a filtered isomorphism such that each restriction
\varphi_{p}:(E_{p},F_{p})\to(E^{\prime}_{p},F^{\prime}_{p})
is a unipotent map with respect to the given frames at
p
.
We denote the groupoid and the corresponding moduli space of framed representations of rank
n
on
(\Sigma,P)
by

\textup{Rep}_{G}^{\mathrm{Fr}}\big(\Pi_{1}(\Sigma),P\big)\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\mathfrak{Rep}_{G}^{\mathrm{Fr}}\big(\Pi_{1}(\Sigma),P\big)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\textup{Rep}_{G}^{\mathrm{Fr}}\big(\Pi_{1}(\Sigma),P\big)\big/{\sim}.

Analogously, we give the following:

Definition 4.8
.

Let
(\mathnormal{E},\mathnormal{F},\Phi)
be a filtered representation of the fundamental groupoid
\Pi_{1}(\Sigma)
on a surface with marked boundary
(\Sigma,P)
.
A
local projective frame

\overline{\beta}_{p}
on
(\mathnormal{E},\mathnormal{F},\Phi)
at
p\in P
is an equivalence class of filtered isomorphisms
\beta_{p}:(E_{p},F_{p})\overset{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.35623pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}{\longrightarrow}(\mathbb{C}^{n},\mathbb{P}\mathbb{C}^{\bullet})
of vector bundles over
U_{p}
which are flat with respect to
\Phi
, where
\beta_{p}\sim t\beta_{p}
for any
t\in C^{\times}
.
A
projective frame
on
(\mathnormal{E},\mathnormal{F},\Phi)
is a collection
\overline{\beta}=\left\{\beta_{p}:P\in P\right\}
of local projective frames, one at each marked point.
Any such triple
(\mathnormal{E},\mathnormal{F},\Phi,\overline{\beta})
is a called a
filtered projectively framed representation
of the fundamental groupoid
\Pi_{1}(\Sigma)
on the surface with marked boundary
(\Sigma,P)
.

Definition 4.9
.

A
projectively unipotent isomorphism

\varphi:(\mathnormal{E},\mathnormal{F},\Phi,\overline{\beta})\to(\mathnormal{E}^{\prime},\mathnormal{F}^{\prime},\Phi^{\prime},\overline{\beta}^{\prime})
of two projectively framed representations of the fundamental groupoid
\Pi_{1}(\Sigma)
on the surface with marked boundary
(\Sigma,P)
is a filtered morphism such that each restriction
\varphi_{p}:(E_{p},F_{p})\to(E^{\prime}_{p},F^{\prime}_{p})
is a projectively unipotent map with respect to the given projective frames at
p
.
We denote the groupoid and the corresponding moduli space of projectively framed representations of rank
n
on
(\Sigma,P)
by

\textup{Rep}_{G}^{\mathrm{PFr}}\big(\Pi_{1}(\Sigma),P\big)\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\mathfrak{Rep}_{G}^{\mathrm{PFr}}\big(\Pi_{1}(\Sigma),P\big)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\textup{Rep}_{G}^{\mathrm{PFr}}\big(\Pi_{1}(\Sigma),P\big)\big/{\sim}.

The proof of
Proposition 4.5
can be easily adapted to the framed case by adding the frame information on the fibers, leading to the following:

Proposition 4.10
.

The holonomy representation functor extends to an equivalence of categories between decorated local systems and decorated representations of the fundamental groupoid:

\operatorname{Hol}:\textup{Loc}_{G}^{\square}(\Sigma,P)\overset{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.35623pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}{\longrightarrow}\textup{Rep}_{G}^{\square}\big(\Pi_{1}(\Sigma),P\big),

for any
\square\in\left\{{\mathrm{Fi}},{\mathrm{Fr}},{\mathrm{PFr}}\right\}
.
It sends
({\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{F}},\star)\mapsto(\mathnormal{E},\mathnormal{F},\Phi,\star)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=({\mathcal{E}}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{\Sigma},{\mathcal{F}}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{\Sigma},\operatorname{hol},\star)
, where
\star=\emptyset,\beta,\overline{\beta}
for
\square={\mathrm{Fi}},{\mathrm{Fr}},{\mathrm{PFr}}
respectively.

4.3
Discrete and Decorated Groupoid Representations

Now, let us recall the discrete fundamental groupoid
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)\rightrightarrows P
introduced in
subsection 2.1
, defined over any surface with marked boundary
(\Sigma,P)
.
The manifestation of its discrete nature from the point of view of representation theory is as follows.
Any representation of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)
involves in particular a complex vector bundle
E\to P
which becomes nothing but a finite collection of vector spaces
E=\left\{E_{p}:p\in P\right\}
.
Correspondingly, a groupoid homomorphism
\phi:\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)\to\operatorname{\mathsf{GL}}(E)
becomes a finitely-generated countable collection of isomorphisms between these vector spaces which is completely determined by its values on the finite set of generators of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)
.
Furthermore, since the discrete fundamental groupoid
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)
is a full subgroupoid of
\Pi_{1}(\Sigma)
, any representation of
\Pi_{1}(\Sigma)
restricts to a representation of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)
.
The advantage of this point of view is that it provides a ‘discrete’ model for the representation category of
\Pi_{1}(\Sigma)
in the sense of the following lemma that generalises the final assertion of
Proposition 1.4
.

Lemma 4.11
.

For any surface with marked boundary
(\Sigma,P)
, the restriction functor

\textup{Rep}_{G}\big(\Pi_{1}(\Sigma)\big)\overset{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.35623pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}{\longrightarrow}\textup{Rep}_{G}\big(\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)\big)

(4.1)

is an equivalence of categories.

Explicitly, this restriction functor sends

(\mathnormal{E},\Phi)\mapsto(E,\phi)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\Big(\mathnormal{E}|_{P},\Phi\big|_{\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)}\Big).

An inverse functor is obtained choosing any
p\in P
and restricting a representation
(E,\phi)
of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)
to a representation of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p)
, then lifting it to the holonomy representation of the local system
{\mathcal{E}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\big(\tilde{\Sigma}\times E_{p}\big)/\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p)
where
\tilde{\Sigma}\to\Sigma
is the universal cover based at
p
.
In particular,
Lemma 4.11
implies that, for any
p\in P
, the restriction functor

\textup{Rep}_{G}\big(\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)\big)\overset{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.35623pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}{\longrightarrow}\textup{Rep}_{G}\big(\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p)\big)

is also an equivalence of categories.

Proof of
Lemma 4.11
.

All the necessary ingredients for the proof of this lemma were developed in the course of the proof of
Proposition 1.4
(see Appendix
A
).
In particular, select any point
p\in P
and consider the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{Rep}_{G}\big(\Pi_{1}(\Sigma)\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{Rep}_{G}\big(\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{Loc}_{G}(\Sigma)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{Rep}_{G}\big(\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p)\big),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathnormal{R}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathnormal{R}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathnormal{R}^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Hol}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Mon}_{p}}

(4.2)

where
\mathnormal{R},\mathnormal{R}^{\prime},\mathnormal{R}^{\prime\prime}
are the restriction functors.
By
Proposition 1.4
,
\mathnormal{R}^{\prime}
is an equivalence, and we need to show that so is
\mathnormal{R}
.
This will also imply that
\mathnormal{R}^{\prime\prime}
is an equivalence.

It is clear that
\mathnormal{R}
is full, but to see that it is faithful we appeal to the diagram (
4.2
).
Suppose
(\mathnormal{E},\Phi)
and
(\mathnormal{E}^{\prime},\Phi^{\prime})
is a pair of representations of
\Pi_{1}(\Sigma)
, and
(E,\phi)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\mathnormal{R}(\mathnormal{E},\Phi)
and
(E^{\prime},\phi^{\prime})\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\mathnormal{R}(\mathnormal{E}^{\prime},\Phi^{\prime})
are their restrictions to representations of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)
.
Restrict them further to representations
(E_{p},\phi_{p})\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\mathnormal{R}^{\prime\prime}(E,\phi)
and
(E^{\prime}_{p},\phi^{\prime}_{p})\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\mathnormal{R}^{\prime\prime}(E^{\prime},\phi^{\prime})
of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p)
.
The map
\mathnormal{R}^{\prime}=\mathnormal{R}^{\prime\prime}\circ\mathnormal{R}:\mathop{\mathsf{Iso}}\nolimits\big((\mathnormal{E},\Phi),(\mathnormal{E}^{\prime},\Phi^{\prime})\big)\to\mathop{\mathsf{Iso}}\nolimits\big((E_{p},\phi_{p}),(E^{\prime}_{p},\phi^{\prime}_{p})\big)
is bijective and in particular injective by
Proposition 1.4
, so it follows that the map
\mathnormal{R}:\mathop{\mathsf{Iso}}\nolimits\big((\mathnormal{E},\Phi),(\mathnormal{E}^{\prime},\Phi^{\prime})\big)\to\mathop{\mathsf{Iso}}\nolimits\big((E,\phi),(E^{\prime},\phi^{\prime})\big)
must be injective, too.

Next, we argue that
\mathnormal{R}
is essentially surjective.
Suppose
(E,\phi)
is a representation of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)
.
Restrict it to a representation
(E_{p},\phi_{p})\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\mathnormal{R}^{\prime\prime}(E,\phi)
of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p)
, and lift that to the holonomy representation
(\mathnormal{E},\Phi)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\operatorname{Hol}({\mathcal{E}})
of
\Pi_{1}(\Sigma)
of the local system
{\mathcal{E}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\big(\tilde{\Sigma}\times E_{p}\big)/\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p)
.
Thus,
\mathnormal{R}^{\prime}(\mathnormal{E},\Phi)=\mathrel{\mathop{:}}(E^{\prime}_{p},\phi^{\prime}_{p})\cong(E_{p},\phi_{p})
.
We claim that the restricted representation
(\mathnormal{E}|_{P},\Phi|_{P})\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\mathnormal{R}(\mathnormal{E},\Phi)
of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)
is isomorphic to the original representation
(E,\phi)
.

The first thing to observe is that the fibre
E_{q}
of the bundle
E\to P
over any
q\in P
is isomorphic to the fibre
E_{p}
via the representation
\phi_{\gamma}:E_{q}\overset{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.35623pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}{\longrightarrow}E_{p}
in as many ways as there are homotopy classes of paths from
q
to
p
.
As a result, a vector
e
in
E_{q}
determines an infinite collection
\underline{e}=\left\{e_{\gamma}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\varphi_{\gamma}(e)\right\}_{\gamma}
of vectors in
E_{p}
, one for each homotopy class
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)
with source
q
and target
p
.
Equivalently, the collection
\underline{e}
is enumerated by points
\tilde{q}
in the fibre
\pi^{-1}(q)
of the universal cover
\pi:\tilde{\Sigma}\to\Sigma
based at
p
.
Any two homotopy classes of paths from
q
to
p
form a loop at
p
, and the components of the infinite collection
\underline{e}
are related by the action of the fundamental group
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p)
through the representation
\phi_{p}:\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p)\to GL(E)
.
In other words, upon examining (
A.2
), we have a canonical isomorphism between
E_{q}
and the local system’s stalk
{\mathcal{E}}_{q}
:

\nu_{q}:E_{q}\overset{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.35623pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}{\longrightarrow}{\mathcal{E}}_{q}=\big(\pi^{-1}(q)\times E_{p}\big)\big/\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p),

(4.3)

and this isomorphism intertwines the representation
\phi
and the holonomy of
{\mathcal{E}}
.
At the same time, recall from (
A.4
) that the fibre
\mathnormal{E}|_{q}
of the vector bundle
\mathnormal{E}
is also canonically isomorphic to the stalk
{\mathcal{E}}_{q}
:

\eta_{q}:\mathnormal{E}|_{q}\overset{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.35623pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}{\longrightarrow}{\mathcal{E}}_{q}=\big(\pi^{-1}(q)\times E_{p}\big)\big/\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p),

(4.4)

and this isomorphism also intertwines the holonomy of
{\mathcal{E}}
with the representation
\Phi
and hence its restriction
\phi
of
\Phi
.
We get an isomorphism
\varphi_{q}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\nu_{q}^{-1}\circ\eta_{q}:\mathnormal{E}|_{q}\overset{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.35623pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}{\longrightarrow}E_{q}
for every
q\in P
, and therefore a bundle isomorphism
\varphi:\mathnormal{E}|_{P}\overset{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.35623pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}{\longrightarrow}E
which intertwines the representations
\phi^{\prime}
and
\phi
.
∎

We can conclude from
Lemma 4.11
that just the mere addition of marked points to the surface
\Sigma
without any decorations does not yield any new phenomena from the point of view of representation theory.
However, the important observation is that the restriction functor of
Lemma 4.11
retains any decoration that a representation of
\Pi_{1}(\Sigma)
may have along
P
, so it acts like a ‘discretisation’ of decorated representations of
\Pi_{1}(\Sigma)
.
To phrase this precisely, we introduce the following definitions.

Definition 4.12
.

Let
(\Sigma,P)
be any surface with marked boundary.
A
filtered representation
of the discrete fundamental groupoid
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)
is a triple
(E,F,\phi)
consisting of a complex vector bundle
E=\left\{E_{p}:p\in P\right\}\to P
, a filtration
F=\left\{F_{p}:p\in P\right\}
, and a groupoid homomorphism
\phi:\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)\to\operatorname{\mathsf{GL}}(E)
satisfying the following additional property.
For every secondary marked point
p\in P_{\mathrm{II}}
, the flag
F_{p}
is invariant under the isomorphism
\phi_{\gamma}:E_{p}\overset{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.35623pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}{\longrightarrow}E_{p}
where
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p)
is the boundary loop based at
p
.

Since
E
and
E^{\prime}
are just finite collections of vector spaces, a bundle map
\varphi
is nothing but a finite collection of linear maps

\varphi=\left\{\varphi_{p}:(E_{p},F_{p})\to(E^{\prime}_{p},F^{\prime}_{p})~\big|~p\in P\right\}.

Definition 4.13
.

A morphism
\varphi:(E,F,\phi)\to(E^{\prime},F^{\prime},\phi^{\prime})
of two filtered representations of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)
is a filtered bundle map
\varphi:(E,F)\to(E^{\prime},F^{\prime})
that intertwines the representations
\phi,\phi^{\prime}
.
We denote the groupoids and the corresponding moduli spaces of filtered representations of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)
of rank
n
by

\textup{Rep}_{G}^{\mathrm{Fi}}\big(\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)\big)\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\mathfrak{Rep}_{G}^{\mathrm{Fi}}\big(\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)\big)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\textup{Rep}_{G}^{\mathrm{Fi}}\big(\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)\big)\big/{\sim}.

Analogously, in the framed and projectively framed case we give the following definitions.

Definition 4.14
.

Let
(\Sigma,P)
be any with marked boundary.
A
framed representation
of the discrete fundamental groupoid
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)
is a quadruple
(E,F,\phi,\beta)
consisting of a filtered representation
(E,F,\phi)
together with a collection
\beta=\left\{\beta_{p}:p\in P\right\}
of local frames at each marked point.

Definition 4.15
.

A unipotent morphism
\varphi:(E,F,\phi,\beta)\to(E^{\prime},F^{\prime},\phi^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})
of two framed representations of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)
is a
filtered isomorphism such that each restriction
\varphi_{p}:(E_{p},F_{p})\to(E^{\prime}_{p},F^{\prime}_{p})
is a unipotent map with respect to the given frames at
p
.
We denote the groupoids and the corresponding moduli spaces of framed representations of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)
of rank
n
by

\textup{Rep}_{G}^{\mathrm{Fr}}\big(\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)\big)\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\mathfrak{Rep}_{G}^{\mathrm{Fr}}\big(\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)\big)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\textup{Rep}_{G}^{\mathrm{Fr}}\big(\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)\big)\big/{\sim}.

Definition 4.16
.

Let
(\Sigma,P)
be any surface with marked boundary.
A
projectively framed representation
of the discrete fundamental groupoid
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)
is a quadruple
(E,F,\phi,\overline{\beta})
consisting of a filtered representation
(E,F,\phi)
together with a collection
\overline{\beta}=\left\{\overline{\beta}_{p}:p\in P\right\}
of projective local frames at each marked point.

Definition 4.17
.

A projectively unipotent morphism
\varphi:(E,F,\phi,\overline{\beta})\to(E^{\prime},F^{\prime},\phi^{\prime},\overline{\beta}^{\prime})
of two framed representations of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)
is a
filtered isomorphism such that each restriction
\varphi_{p}:(E_{p},F_{p})\to(E^{\prime}_{p},F^{\prime}_{p})
is a projectively unipotent map with respect to the given projective frames at
p
.
We denote the groupoids and the corresponding moduli spaces of framed representations of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)
of rank
n
by

\textup{Rep}_{G}^{\mathrm{PFr}}\big(\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)\big)\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\mathfrak{Rep}_{G}^{\mathrm{PFr}}\big(\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)\big)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\textup{Rep}_{G}^{\mathrm{PFr}}\big(\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)\big)\big/{\sim}.

In respect of these definitions, the restriction functor of
Lemma 4.11
extends to an equivalence of categories for the corresponding filtered representations.
We summarise this as follows.

Lemma 4.18
.

Let
(\Sigma,P)
be any surface with marked boundary with nonempty
P
.
Then the restriction functor

\textup{Rep}_{G}^{\square}\big(\Pi_{1}(\Sigma),P\big)\overset{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.35623pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}{\longrightarrow}\textup{Rep}_{G}^{\square}\big(\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)\big)

is an equivalence of categories for any
\square\in\left\{{\mathrm{Fi}},{\mathrm{Fr}},{\mathrm{PFr}}\right\}
.

Together with
Proposition 4.10
this lemma immediately implies the following result.

Proposition 4.19
.

For any surface with marked boundary
(\Sigma,P)
with nonempty
P
, the restricted holonomy representation functor gives an equivalence of

\operatorname{Hol}:\textup{Loc}_{G}^{\square}(\Sigma,P)\overset{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.35623pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}{\longrightarrow}\textup{Rep}_{G}^{\square}\big(\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)\big),

for any
\square\in\left\{{\mathrm{Fi}},{\mathrm{Fr}},{\mathrm{PFr}}\right\}
.

Remark 4.20
.

By dropping the decorations, and restricting
P
to a single point, this result reduces to the second part of
Proposition 1.4
.

We can regard these equivalences as providing in a sense a
discrete model
for the groupoid and moduli space of decorated local systems.

5
Forgetting secondary points

In this section, we study the forgetful functors that allow to drop all secondary marked points. In particular, we prove that all vertical arrows in the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{Loc}_{G}^{\mathrm{Fi}}(\Sigma,P)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{Rep}_{G}^{\mathrm{Fi}}\big(\Pi_{1}(\Sigma),P\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{Rep}_{G}^{\mathrm{Fi}}\big(\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{Loc}_{G}^{\mathrm{Fi}}(\Sigma,P_{{\mathrm{I}}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{Rep}_{G}^{\mathrm{Fi}}\big(\Pi_{1}(\Sigma),P_{{\mathrm{I}}}\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{Rep}_{G}^{\mathrm{Fi}}\big(\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P_{{\mathrm{I}}})\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

(5.1)

are ramified coverings of degree
(n!)^{r}
where
r
is the number of secondary marked points; i.e.,
r=|P_{\mathrm{II}}|
. We also describe the fibres of such coverings. In order to do so, we need to study the stratification of flags and dive into Jordan types.

5.1
Stratification by Relative Position of Adjacent Flags

Filtered local systems on a surface with marked boundary have a well-defined collection of invariants obtained by calculating the relative positions of all adjacent pairs of filtrations.
The meaning of ‘adjacent’ here is unambiguously provided by the boundary segments of the irregular boundary circles.

Recall that, given a pair of (complete) flags
F,F^{\prime}
on an
n
-dimensional vector space, their
relative position
is defined as the permutation matrix
\sigma\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\operatorname{pos}(F,F^{\prime})\in W
whose entries are given by

\operatorname{pos}(F,F^{\prime})_{ij}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\dim\left(\frac{F_{i}\cap F^{\prime}_{j}}{F_{i}\cap F^{\prime}_{j-1}+F_{i-1}\cap F^{\prime}_{j}}\right).

Before proceeding, we mention some basic properties of the relative position matrix.
If
F
and
F^{\prime}
are two flags of lengths
m
and
m^{\prime}
in an
n
-dimensional vector space, then
\sigma=\operatorname{pos}(F,F^{\prime})
is an
m{\times}m^{\prime}
-matrix whose entries sum up to
n
.
If
F
and
F^{\prime}
are complete flags, then
\sigma
is a permutation matrix.
If
\sigma
is the skew-identity matrix, then we say that
F
and
F^{\prime}
are
transverse
(or in
general position
) and write
F\pitchfork F^{\prime}
.
Isomorphisms preserve the relative position of two flags:
\operatorname{pos}\big(\varphi(F),\varphi(F^{\prime})\big)=\operatorname{pos}(F,F^{\prime})
for any linear isomorphism
\varphi:E\overset{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.35623pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}{\longrightarrow}E^{\prime}
.
Switching the order of the flags transposes the relative position matrix:
\operatorname{pos}(F,F^{\prime})=\operatorname{pos}(F^{\prime},F)^{\top}
.

Let
(\Sigma,P)
be any surface with marked boundary with a least one primary marked point.
Pick a positively-oriented boundary segment
\delta\in\mathfrak{A}=\mathfrak{A}(\Sigma,P)
with source
\delta(0)=p_{1}
and target
\delta(1)=p_{2}\in P_{\mathrm{I}}
.
If
({\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{F}})
is a filtered local system on
(\Sigma,P)
, let
(E_{p_{i}},F_{p_{i}})
be the stalk of
({\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{F}})
at
p_{i}
.
Then we can compare the two (not necessarily distinct) flags
F_{p_{1}}
and
F_{p_{2}}
by parallel transporting them to any common point
p
in
\delta
.
Concretely, let
E_{p}
be the stalk of
{\mathcal{E}}
at
p\in\delta
, and let
\operatorname{hol}_{i}:E_{p_{i}}\overset{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.35623pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}{\longrightarrow}E_{p}
be the holonomy of
{\mathcal{E}}
along a path from
p_{i}
to
p
contained in
\delta
.

Definition 5.1
.

The
relative position
of the filtrations
{\mathcal{F}}_{p_{1}},{\mathcal{F}}_{p_{2}}
on
{\mathcal{E}}
over
\delta
, or equivalently of the flags
F_{p_{1}},F_{p_{2}}
over
\delta
, is defined as the relative position of their parallel transport to any common point
p\in\delta
:

\operatorname{pos}({\mathcal{F}}_{p_{1}},{\mathcal{F}}_{p_{2}})=\operatorname{pos}(F_{p_{1}},F_{p_{2}})\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\operatorname{pos}\Big(\operatorname{hol}_{1}F_{p_{1}},\operatorname{hol}_{2}F_{p_{2}}\Big)\in W.

The matrix
\operatorname{pos}({\mathcal{F}}_{p_{1}},{\mathcal{F}}_{p_{2}})
is independent of
p
and hence well-defined because the relative position of two flags in a vector space is invariant under isomorphisms.
For exactly the same reason, it is also preserved by isomorphisms of filtered local systems and therefore defines an invariant.
Namely, it is a collection of permutation matrices
\sigma_{\delta}
labelled by the boundary segments
\delta\in\mathfrak{A}=\mathfrak{A}(\Sigma,P)
; i.e., an element

\bm{\sigma}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=(\sigma_{\delta})\in W_{\mathfrak{A}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\prod_{\delta\in\mathfrak{A}}W.

Definition 5.2
.

We say that a filtered local system
({\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{F}})
on
(\Sigma,P)
has
adjacent flags in relative position

\bm{\sigma}\in W_{\mathfrak{A}}
if
\operatorname{pos}\big({\mathcal{F}}_{\delta(0)},{\mathcal{F}}_{\delta(1)})=\sigma_{\delta}
for every
\delta\in\mathfrak{A}
.
In this case, we write
\operatorname{pos}({\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{F}})\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\bm{\sigma}
.
Such decorated local systems form a full subgroupoid of
\textup{Loc}_{G}^{{\mathrm{Fi}}}(\Sigma,P)
.
We denote it and its moduli space by

\textup{Loc}_{G}^{{\mathrm{Fi}}}(\Sigma,P;\bm{\sigma})\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\mathfrak{Loc}_{G}^{{\mathrm{Fi}}}(\Sigma,P;\bm{\sigma})\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\textup{Loc}_{G}^{{\mathrm{Fi}}}(\Sigma,P;\bm{\sigma})/{\sim}.

The fact that these are full subgroupoids implies immediately that the groupoid of filtered local systems decomposes into the disjoint union of all these subgroupoids given by fixing relative positions of adjacent flags.

Proposition 5.3
.

The groupoid of filtered local systems on
(\Sigma,P)
decomposes into a disjoint union of full subgroupoids enumerated by the finite set
W_{\mathfrak{A}}
of all possible relative positions of pairs of flags at adjacent primary marked points:

\textup{Loc}_{G}^{{\mathrm{Fi}}}(\Sigma,P)=\coprod_{\bm{\sigma}\in W_{\mathfrak{A}}}\textup{Loc}_{G}^{{\mathrm{Fi}}}(\Sigma,P;\bm{\sigma}).

Consequently, the moduli space of filtered local systems admits a similar decomposition:

\mathfrak{Loc}_{G}^{\mathrm{Fi}}(\Sigma,P)=\coprod_{\bm{\sigma}\in W_{\mathfrak{A}}}\mathfrak{Loc}_{G}^{{\mathrm{Fi}}}(\Sigma,P;\bm{\sigma}).

5.2
Invariant Flags and Shuffled Jordan Type

Let
J\subset G
denote the subset of all invertible Jordan matrices.
If
\phi\in\operatorname{\mathsf{Aut}}(E)
is an automorphism of an
n
-dimensional vector space
E
, let
J(\phi)\subset J
denote the subset of all Jordan matrices that have the same Jordan type as the Jordan type
\operatorname{Jt}(\phi)
of
\phi
:

J(\phi)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\left\{\mathnormal{J}\in J~\big|~\operatorname{Jt}(\mathnormal{J})=\operatorname{Jt}(\phi)\right\}.

Recall that
J(\phi)
is a finite set whose cardinality equals the number of all possible permutations of the distinct eigenvalues of
\phi
and of the Jordan blocks for the same eigenvalue.
In particular, if
\phi
has all-distinct eigenvalues, then
|J(\phi)|=n!
.

Here, we denote by
W\subset G
the subgroup of permutations.

Definition 5.4
.

An invertible
shuffled Jordan matrix
is any upper-triangular matrix of the form
\mathnormal{Z}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\tau\mathnormal{J}\tau^{-1}
where
\mathnormal{J}\in J
is an invertible Jordan matrix and
\tau\in W
is a permutation matrix.
Let
J_{W}\subset G
denote the subset of all shuffled Jordan matrices, and let
J_{W}(\phi)\subset J_{W}
be the finite subset of all shuffled Jordan matrices that have the same Jordan type as a given automorphism
\phi\in\operatorname{\mathsf{Aut}}(E)
:

\displaystyle J_{W}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\left\{\mathnormal{Z}=\tau\mathnormal{J}\tau^{-1}\in B~\Big|~\mathnormal{J}\in J\text{ and }\tau\in W\right\},

\displaystyle J_{W}(\phi)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\left\{\mathnormal{Z}=\tau\mathnormal{J}\tau^{-1}\in B~\Big|~\mathnormal{J}\in J(\phi)\text{ and }\tau\in W\right\}.

Equivalently,
J_{W}
is the set of all pairs
(\mathnormal{J},\tau)\in J\times W
such that
\mathnormal{Z}=\tau\mathnormal{J}\tau^{-1}
is upper-triangular, considered up to equivalence:
(\mathnormal{J},\tau)\sim(\mathnormal{J}^{\prime},\tau^{\prime})
if and only if
\mathnormal{Z}=\mathnormal{Z}^{\prime}
which means
\mathnormal{J}=(\tau^{-1}\tau^{\prime})\mathnormal{J}^{\prime}(\tau^{-1}\tau^{\prime})^{-1}
.

Note that, if a Jordan matrix
\mathnormal{J}\in J
is diagonal, then of course the conjugate
\mathnormal{Z}=\tau\mathnormal{J}\tau^{-1}
by any permutation
\tau\in W
remains diagonal.
In general, however,
\mathnormal{Z}=\tau\mathnormal{J}\tau^{-1}
remains upper-triangular for only a subset
W(\mathnormal{J})\subset W
of permutations which we call
shuffles
of
\mathnormal{J}
.
Also, note that a shuffled Jordan matrix
\mathnormal{Z}\in J_{W}
may not be a Jordan matrix.
See
subsection B.5
for more details and examples.

5.3
Forgetful functors

We start by proving the right vertical arrow in diagram (
5.1
).

Let
P_{\mathrm{II}}=\left\{p_{1},\ldots,p_{r}\right\}
.
For each
i=1,\ldots,r
, denote by
\delta_{i}\in\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p_{i})
the boundary loop based at
p_{i}
.

Proposition 5.5
.

For any surface with marked boundary
(\Sigma,P)
, the surjective map

\mathfrak{Rep}_{G}^{\mathrm{Fi}}\big(\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)\big)\to\mathfrak{Rep}_{G}^{\mathrm{Fi}}\big(\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P_{\mathrm{I}})\big)

induced by the forgetful restriction functor is a ramified covering of degree
(n!)^{r}
where
r
is the number of secondary marked points; i.e.,
r=|P_{\mathrm{II}}|
.
The cardinality of the fibre can be read off from the Jordan type of the local monodromy at each secondary marked point.
Namely, the fibre above an isomorphism class
[(E,F,\phi)]\in\mathfrak{Rep}_{G}^{\mathrm{Fi}}\big(\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P_{\mathrm{I}})\big)
is canonically in bijection with the set
J_{W}(\phi_{\delta_{1}})\times\cdots\times J_{W}(\phi_{\delta_{r}})
where each
\delta_{i}
is the boundary loop at the secondary marked point
p_{i}\in P_{\mathrm{II}}
.
In particular, the complement of the branch locus is the open dense subset consisting of all filtered representations
(E,F,\phi)
such that each automorphism
\phi_{\delta_{i}}\in\operatorname{\mathsf{Aut}}(E_{p_{i}})
has all-distinct eigenvalues.

Proof.

We consider the case
P_{\mathrm{I}}\neq\emptyset
; the proof in the other case is almost completely identical.
We will explain that any filtered representation of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P_{\mathrm{I}})
can be lifted to a filtered representation of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)
in finitely many ways up to isomorphism.
Given a filtered representation
(E,\phi,F)
of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P_{\mathrm{I}})
, the underlying undecorated representation
(E,\phi)
lifts to an undecorated representation
(\tilde{\mathnormal{E}},\tilde{\Phi})
of
\Pi_{1}(\Sigma)
by
Lemma 4.11
.
Then we restrict
(\tilde{\mathnormal{E}},\tilde{\Phi})
to an undecorated representation
(\tilde{E},\tilde{\phi})
of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)
.
By (
4.3
) and (
4.4
), the fibre
\tilde{E}_{p}
of
\tilde{E}
at any
p\in P_{\mathrm{I}}
is canonically isomorphic to the fibre
E_{p}
of
E
at
p
.
Using these canonical isomorphisms, we transport the flags
F
in all fibres of
E\to P_{\mathrm{I}}
to the corresponding fibres of
\tilde{E}|_{P_{\mathrm{I}}}\to P_{\mathrm{I}}
, and we denote these flags by the same letter
F
.
Thus, we have constructed a
partially
filtered representation
(\tilde{E},\tilde{\phi},F)
of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)
, namely a representation that is filtered only on the fibers above
P_{{\mathrm{I}}}
.

Next, for every
p\in P\smallsetminus P_{\mathrm{I}}
, let
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p)
be the boundary loop based at
p
.
Consider the vector space
\tilde{E}_{p}
with the automorphism
\tilde{\phi}_{p}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\tilde{\phi}_{\gamma}
.
Endow
\tilde{E}_{p}
with any
\tilde{\phi}_{p}
-invariant flag
\tilde{F}_{p}
.
The result is a filtered representation
(\tilde{E},\tilde{\phi},\tilde{F})
of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)
whose forgetful restriction to a filtered representation of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P_{\mathrm{I}})
is canonically isomorphic to
(E,\phi,F)
.

If for any
p\in P\smallsetminus P_{\mathrm{I}}
we make another such choice
\tilde{F}^{\prime}_{p}
in
\tilde{E}_{p}
, then we obtain a different filtered representation
(\tilde{E},\tilde{\phi},\tilde{F}^{\prime})
of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)
whose forgetful restriction is canonically isomorphic to
(E,\phi,F)
.
Two such filtered representations are equivalent if and only if there exists, for every
p\in P\smallsetminus P_{\mathrm{I}}
, an automorphism
\tilde{\psi}_{p}\in\operatorname{\mathsf{Aut}}(\tilde{E}_{p})
which moves
\tilde{F}_{p}
to
\tilde{F}^{\prime}_{p}
and commutes with
\tilde{\phi}_{p}
in the sense that
\tilde{\phi}_{p}\tilde{\psi}_{p}=\tilde{\psi}_{p}\tilde{\phi}_{p}
.
By
Proposition B.1
, for every
p\in P\smallsetminus P_{\mathrm{I}}
, there are only finitely many such equivalences classes of
\tilde{F}_{p}
.

Finally, it is a Zariski open condition that each
\phi_{\gamma_{i}}
has all-distinct eigenvalues.
∎

Thanks to
Proposition 4.19
, the following result does not require proof:

Proposition 5.6
.

The surjective map

\mathfrak{Rep}_{G}^{\mathrm{Fi}}\big(\Pi_{1}(\Sigma),P\big)\longrightarrow\mathfrak{Rep}_{G}^{\mathrm{Fi}}\big(\Pi_{1}(\Sigma),P_{\mathrm{I}}\big)

induced by the forgetful functor is a ramified covering of degree
(n!)^{r}
where
r
is the number of secondary marked points; i.e.,
r=|P_{\mathrm{II}}|
.
The cardinality of the fibre can be read off from the Jordan type of the local monodromy at each secondary marked point.
Namely, the fibre above an isomorphism class
[(\mathnormal{E},\mathnormal{F},\Phi)]\in\mathfrak{Rep}_{G}^{\mathrm{Fi}}\big(\Pi_{1}(\Sigma),P_{\mathrm{I}}\big)
is canonically in bijection with the set
J_{W}(\Phi_{\delta_{1}})\times\cdots\times J_{W}(\Phi_{\delta_{r}})
where each
\delta_{i}
is the boundary loop at the secondary marked point
p_{i}\in P_{\mathrm{II}}

In particular, the complement of the branch locus is the open dense subset consisting of all filtered representations
(\mathnormal{E},\mathnormal{F},\Phi)
such that each automorphism
\Phi_{\delta_{i}}\in\operatorname{\mathsf{Aut}}(E_{p_{i}})
has all-distinct eigenvalues.

Finally, thanks to
Proposition 4.5
, the following result follows:

Proposition 5.7
.

The surjective map between the moduli spaces of filtered local systems

\mathfrak{Loc}_{G}^{\mathrm{Fi}}(\Sigma,P)\longrightarrow\mathfrak{Loc}_{G}^{\mathrm{Fi}}(\Sigma,P_{\mathrm{I}})

(5.2)

is a ramified covering of degree
(n!)^{r}
where
r
is the number of secondary marked points; i.e.,
r=|P_{\mathrm{II}}|
.
The cardinality of the fibre can be read off from the Jordan type of the local system’s local monodromy at each secondary marked point.
Namely, the fibre above an isomorphism class
[({\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{F}})]\in\mathfrak{Loc}_{G}^{\mathrm{Fi}}(\Sigma,P_{\mathrm{I}})
is canonically in bijection with the set of
r
-tuples of shuffled Jordan matrices of the same Jordan type as the local monodromies of
{\mathcal{E}}
around the boundary loops
\delta_{1},\ldots,\delta_{r}
; i.e., the set

J_{W}(\phi_{1})\times\cdots\times J_{W}(\phi_{r})\hskip 10.00002pt\text{where}\hskip 10.00002pt\phi_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\operatorname{mon}_{\delta_{i}}({\mathcal{E}})\in\operatorname{\mathsf{Aut}}(E_{p_{i}}).

In particular, the complement of the branch locus is the open dense subset consisting of all filtered local systems whose local monodromy at each secondary marked point has all-distinct eigenvalues.

6
Decorated Character Varieties

The advantage of considering representations of the discrete fundamental groupoid instead of the continuous fundamental groupoid is that this discrete model allows us to give an explicit description of the moduli space of decorated local systems.
This discrete model is the direct analogue of the character variety in the classical situation without marked points.

6.1
Mixed Conjugation Action Groupoid

Let
(\Sigma,P)
be any surface with marked boundary and consider the groupoid
\textup{Rep}_{G}\big(\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)\big)
of representations of the discrete fundamental groupoid.

Suppose
(E,\phi)
is such a representation.
Since the vector bundle
E\to P
is merely a finite collection of vector spaces
E=\left\{E_{p}:p\in P\right\}
, it is always possible to choose a global trivialisation
\chi:E\overset{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.35623pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}{\longrightarrow}\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
where
\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{n}\to P
is the trivial vector bundle over
P
.
It is enough to simply fix an isomorphism
\chi_{p}:E_{p}\overset{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.35623pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{C}^{n}
for every
p\in P
.
Then the groupoid homomorphism
\phi:\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)\to\operatorname{\mathsf{GL}}(E)
determines a groupoid homomorphism
\rho:\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)\to G
, its
matrix representation
with respect to
\chi
:

\rho\mathrel{\mathop{:}}={\mathrm{t}}^{\ast}\chi\circ\phi\circ{\mathrm{s}}^{\ast}\chi^{-1}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{i.e.}\hskip 10.00002pt\rho_{\gamma}={\mathrm{t}}^{\ast}\chi_{\gamma}\circ\phi_{\gamma}\circ{\mathrm{s}}^{\ast}\chi^{-1}_{\gamma}=\chi_{{\mathrm{t}}(\gamma)}\circ\phi_{\gamma}\circ\chi^{-1}_{{\mathrm{s}}(\gamma)},

(6.1)

for any
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)
.

Similarly, suppose
\varphi:(E,\phi)\to(E^{\prime},\phi^{\prime})
is an isomorphism of representations and we choose trivialisations
\chi,\chi^{\prime}
of
E,E^{\prime}
.
Then the linear isomorphism
\varphi:E\overset{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.35623pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}{\longrightarrow}E^{\prime}
determines an automorphism
g:\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{n}\to\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
defined by the commutative diagram

\hbox to82.43pt{\vbox to53.38pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 41.21466pt\lower-28.99753pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {}{

{}{}{}}
{{{{{}}{
