{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.69044pt}{-25.48367pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{i.e.}\hskip 20.00003ptg\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\chi^{\prime}\circ\varphi\circ\chi^{-1}.

(6.2)

Since
\varphi
intertwines the representations
\phi,\phi^{\prime}
in the sense that
\phi^{\prime}_{\gamma}=\varphi_{{\mathrm{t}}(\gamma)}\circ\phi_{\gamma}\circ\varphi^{-1}_{{\mathrm{s}}(\gamma)}
for every
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)
, it follows that this automorphism
g=\left\{g_{p}:p\in P\right\}\in\operatorname{\mathsf{Aut}}(\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{n})
intertwines their matrix representations
\rho,\rho^{\prime}\in\operatorname{\mathsf{Hom}}\big(\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P),G\big)
in the sense that
\rho^{\prime}_{\gamma}=g_{{\mathrm{t}}(\gamma)}\rho_{\gamma}g_{{\mathrm{s}}(\gamma)}^{-1}
.
Of course, an automorphism
g
of the trivial vector bundle
\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{n}\to P
is nothing but an element of the affine algebraic group

G_{P}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\prod_{p\in P}G=\operatorname{\mathsf{Aut}}(\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{n}).

(6.3)

We are therefore naturally led to the
mixed conjugation
action groupoid

G_{P}\ltimes\operatorname{\mathsf{Hom}}\big(\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P),G\big)\hskip 20.00003pt\text{with}\hskip 20.00003ptg.\rho\mathrel{\mathop{:}}={\mathrm{t}}^{\ast}g\cdot\rho\cdot{\mathrm{s}}^{\ast}g^{-1}.

(6.4)

More explicitly, for any
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)
, this group action is

(g.\rho)_{\gamma}={\mathrm{t}}^{\ast}g_{\gamma}\cdot\rho_{\gamma}\cdot{\mathrm{s}}^{\ast}g^{-1}_{\gamma}=g_{{\mathrm{t}}(\gamma)}\rho_{\gamma}g_{{\mathrm{s}}(\gamma)}^{-1}.

The discussion above shows that a choice of trivialisation
\chi:E\overset{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.35623pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}{\longrightarrow}\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
for every rank-
n
vector bundle
E\to P
determines a functor

\textup{Rep}_{G}\big(\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)\big)\to G_{P}\ltimes\operatorname{\mathsf{Hom}}\big(\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P),G\big)

(6.5)

sending representations
(E,\phi)
and morphisms
\varphi
to their matrix representations
\rho
and
g
given by (
6.1
) and (
6.2
).

Making a different choice of trivialisations yields different matrix representations and therefore a different functor.
However, any two trivialisations
\chi,\tilde{\chi}:E\overset{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.35623pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}{\longrightarrow}\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
of the same bundle differ by an automorphism
g\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\tilde{\chi}\circ\chi^{-1}\in\operatorname{\mathsf{Aut}}(\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{n})=G_{P}
so that
\tilde{\chi}=g\chi
.
As a result, the two matrix representations
\rho,\tilde{\rho}
of
\phi:\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)\to\operatorname{\mathsf{GL}}(E)
are related by mixed conjugation by
g
because
\tilde{\rho}={\mathrm{t}}^{\ast}g\cdot\rho\cdot{\mathrm{s}}^{\ast}g^{-1}
.
Consequently, all such functors (
6.5
) are naturally isomorphic, and in fact they form an equivalence of categories as stated in the following:

Lemma 6.1
.

For any surface with marked boundary
(\Sigma,P)
, there is a canonical equivalence of categories

\textup{Rep}_{G}\big(\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)\big)\cong G_{P}\ltimes\operatorname{\mathsf{Hom}}\big(\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P),G\big).

Consequently, for any
p\in P
, there is a canonical bijection

\text{$\operatorname{\mathsf{Hom}}\big(\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P),G\big)/G_{P}$}\cong\text{$\operatorname{\mathsf{Hom}}\big(\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p),G\big)/G$}=\text{$\mathfrak{X}_{G}(\Sigma,p)$}.

Proof.

This equivalence of categories is given by the inclusion functor from right to left.
Namely, it sends any homomorphism
\rho\in\operatorname{\mathsf{Hom}}\big(\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P),G)
to the representation
(E,\phi)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=(\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{n},\rho)
on the trivial vector bundle and regards any group element
g\in G_{P}=\operatorname{\mathsf{Aut}}(\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{n})
as a bundle automorphism.
Any inverse functor is given by matrix representation (
6.5
) and requires a choice of trivialisation for every vector bundle
E\to P
.
We just have to check that these functors are indeed inverse to one another.

Going from right to left to right is obvious because the trivial vector bundle
\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{n}\to P
has a canonical trivialisation, so the functors compose to send
\rho\mapsto(\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{n},\rho)\mapsto\rho
and similarly on morphisms.
So the right-left-right composition of these functors is equal to the identity functor.

For going from left to right to left, suppose
(E,\phi)
is a representation of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)
and let
\rho
be its matrix representation with respect to the chosen trivialisation
\chi
.
Then the functors compose to send
(E,\phi)\mapsto\rho\mapsto(\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{n},\rho)
.
But then
\chi
itself defines the desired isomorphism
(E,\phi)\overset{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.35623pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}{\longrightarrow}(\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{n},\rho)
.
Similarly, given any morphism
\varphi:(E,\phi)\to(E^{\prime},\phi^{\prime})
, the functors compose to send
\varphi\mapsto g=\chi^{\prime}\circ\varphi\circ\chi^{-1}\mapsto g
.
This means
g\in\operatorname{\mathsf{Aut}}(\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{n})
and
\varphi
form a commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(E,\phi)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{n},\rho)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(E^{\prime},\phi^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{n},\rho^{\prime}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi^{\prime}}

Thus, the left-to-right-to-left composition of the functors defined above is naturally equivalent to the identity functor.
∎

6.2
Decorated Representation Variety

Whereas the set of groupoid homomorphisms
\operatorname{\mathsf{Hom}}\big(\Pi_{1}(\Sigma),G\big)
is an infinite-dimensional complex manifold (it is a mapping space), its ‘discrete’ counterpart
\operatorname{\mathsf{Hom}}\big(\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P),G\big)
is a finite-dimensional algebraic space.
More precisely, we have the following fact that follows immediately from
Lemma 2.8
.

Lemma 6.2
.

For any surface with marked boundary
(\Sigma,P)
of genus
g
with
s\geq 1
holes, the set of groupoid homomorphisms
\operatorname{\mathsf{Hom}}\big(\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P),G\big)
is a smooth affine algebraic variety of dimension
n^{2}(2g+s+|P|-2)
with a non-canonical isomorphism

\operatorname{\mathsf{Hom}}\big(\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P),G\big)\cong G^{2g+s+|P|-2}.

Any such isomorphism is determined by a choice of free presentation of the discrete fundamental groupoid
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)
.

Now we introduce the relevant subset of groupoid homomorphisms.

Definition 6.3
.

Let
(\Sigma,P)
be a surface with marked boundary.
Then the
decorated representation variety
of
(\Sigma,P)
is the subset
R_{G}(\Sigma,P)
of
\operatorname{\mathsf{Hom}}\big(\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P),G\big)
consisting of all groupoid homomorphisms
\rho:\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)\to G
which are upper-triangular on the boundary loop at each secondary marked point:

R_{G}(\Sigma,P)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\left\{\rho~\Big|~\rho_{\delta_{p}}\in B\hskip 10.00002pt\forall p\in P_{\mathrm{II}}\right\}\subset\operatorname{\mathsf{Hom}}\big(\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P),G\big),

where
\delta_{p}\in\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p)
denotes the boundary loop based at the marked point
p\in P_{\mathrm{II}}
.

Evidently, in the absence of secondary marked points (
P_{\mathrm{II}}=\emptyset
), the defining constraint in this definition becomes vacuous, so we get a simplification:

R_{G}(\Sigma,P_{{\mathrm{I}}})=\operatorname{\mathsf{Hom}}\big(\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P_{{\mathrm{I}}}),G\big).

Proposition 6.4
.

Let
(\Sigma,P)
be any surface with marked boundary of genus
g
with
s\geq 1
holes,
r
secondary marked points, and
m
primary marked points.
Then the decorated representation variety
R_{G}(\Sigma,P)
is a smooth, closed, affine algebraic subvariety of
\operatorname{\mathsf{Hom}}\big(\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P),G\big)
, and there is a non-canonical isomorphism

R_{G}(\Sigma,P)\cong G^{2g+s+m-2}\times B^{r}.

Consequently,
R_{G}(\Sigma,P)
has dimension
n^{2}(2g+s+m-2)+\tfrac{1}{2}r(n^{2}+n)
.
Any such isomorphism is realised by choosing a finite and free presentation of the discrete fundamental groupoid
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)
.

We will prove this proposition by exploiting the fact that decorated representation varieties can be described completely explicitly using the convenient finite presentations of the discrete fundamental groupoid
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)
constructed in
subsection 2.1
.

Let
(\Sigma,P)
be any surface with marked boundary of genus
g
, with
\bm{s}=(l,r,d)
holes and
\bm{m}=(m_{1},\ldots,m_{d})
primary marked points.
Pick any basepoint
p_{0}\in P
, and choose a finite presentation of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)
as described in
Lemma 2.9
:

\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)\cong\left\langle\alpha_{i},\beta_{i},\gamma_{i},\delta_{i},\delta_{i,j},c_{i},d_{i}~\Big|~\text{ relation \eqref{251109164128}}\right\rangle.

(6.6)

Recall also that
c_{1}=1
if
p_{0}\in P_{\mathrm{I}}
and
d_{1}=1
if
p_{0}\in P_{\mathrm{II}}
.
Then any homomorphism
\rho:\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)\to G
is completely determined by its values on these generators, which we denote as follows:

\rho:~\alpha_{i}\mapsto\mathnormal{A}_{i}~,~~\beta_{i}\mapsto\mathnormal{B}_{i}~,~~\gamma_{i}\mapsto\mathnormal{M}_{i}~,~~\delta_{i}\mapsto\mathnormal{N}_{i}~,~~\delta_{i,j}\mapsto\mathnormal{T}_{ij}~,~~c_{i}\mapsto\mathnormal{C}_{i},~~~d_{i}\mapsto\mathnormal{D}_{i}~.

(6.7)

All these matrices are elements of
G
, and
\rho
belongs to the decorated representation variety
R_{G}(\Sigma,P)
if and only if each
\mathnormal{N}_{i}\in B
.
Furthermore, we automatically have
\mathnormal{C}_{1}=\mathds{1}
if
p_{0}\in P_{\mathrm{I}}
and
\mathnormal{D}_{1}=\mathds{1}
if
p_{0}\in P_{\mathrm{II}}
.
Let us organise this collection of matrices into the following notation:

\begin{gathered}\bm{\mathnormal{A}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\left\{\mathnormal{A}_{1},\ldots,\mathnormal{A}_{g}\right\}\subset G~,\qquad\bm{\mathnormal{B}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\left\{\mathnormal{B}_{1},\ldots,\mathnormal{B}_{g}\right\}\subset G~,\\
\bm{\mathnormal{M}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\left\{\mathnormal{M}_{1},\ldots,\mathnormal{M}_{l}\right\}\subset G~,\qquad\bm{\mathnormal{N}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\left\{\mathnormal{N}_{1},\ldots,\mathnormal{N}_{r}\right\}\subset B~,\\
\bm{\mathnormal{C}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\left\{\mathnormal{C}_{1}\ldots,\mathnormal{C}_{d}\right\}\subset G~,\qquad\bm{\mathnormal{D}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\left\{\mathnormal{D}_{1}\ldots,\mathnormal{D}_{r}\right\}\subset G\\
\bm{\mathnormal{T}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\left\{\mathnormal{T}_{ij}~\big|~1\leq i\leq d,~1\leq j\leq m_{i}\right\}\subset G.\end{gathered}

(6.8)

These collections of matrices are allowed to be empty; e.g.,
\bm{\mathnormal{A}},\bm{\mathnormal{B}}=\emptyset
if
g=0
.
These matrices satisfy the following relation implied by the relation (
2.2
) on the corresponding generators of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)
:

\prod_{i=1}^{\begin{subarray}{c}g\\
\longleftarrow\end{subarray}}[\mathnormal{A}_{i},\mathnormal{B}_{i}]\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{\begin{subarray}{c}l\\
\longleftarrow\end{subarray}}\mathnormal{M}_{i}\cdot\prod_{i={1}}^{\begin{subarray}{c}{r}\\
\longleftarrow\end{subarray}}\mathnormal{D}_{i}^{-1}\mathnormal{N}_{i}\mathnormal{D}_{i}\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{\begin{subarray}{c}d\\
\longleftarrow\end{subarray}}\mathnormal{C}_{i}^{-1}\left(\prod_{j=1}^{\begin{subarray}{c}m_{i}\\
\longleftarrow\end{subarray}}\mathnormal{T}_{ij}\right)\mathnormal{C}_{i}=\mathds{1}.

(6.9)

It follows that the decorated representation variety
R_{G}(\Sigma,P)
is in bijection with the set that parameterises the collection of all such matrices satisfying this relation.
To state this more accurately, we first introduce the following definition.

Definition 6.5
.

Fix any nonnegative integer
g
, any vector
\bm{s}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=(l,r,d)
of nonnegative integers, and any vector
\bm{m}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=(m_{1},\ldots,m_{d})
of strictly positive integers.
Put
s\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=l+d+r
and
m\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=m_{1}+\ldots+m_{d}
.
Then we denote the set of all matrices (
6.8
) satisfying the relation (
6.9
) by

\hat{R}_{G}(g,\bm{s},\bm{m})\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\left\{(\bm{\mathnormal{A}},\bm{\mathnormal{B}},\bm{\mathnormal{M}},\bm{\mathnormal{T}},\bm{\mathnormal{C}},\bm{\mathnormal{D}};\bm{\mathnormal{N}})\in G^{2g+s+m}{\times}B^{r}~\Big|~\textup{ relation \eqref{251107125603} }\big.\right\}.

We also single out the following subset

R_{G}(g,\bm{s},\bm{m})\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\left\{(\bm{\mathnormal{A}},\bm{\mathnormal{B}},\bm{\mathnormal{M}},\bm{\mathnormal{T}},\bm{\mathnormal{C}},\bm{\mathnormal{D}};\bm{\mathnormal{N}})~\Big|~\begin{subarray}{c}\textup{ $\mathnormal{C}_{1}=\mathds{1}$ if $d\geq 1\phantom{,r\geq 1}$ }\\
\textup{ $\mathnormal{D}_{1}=\mathds{1}$ if $d=0,r\geq 1$ }\end{subarray}\big.\right\}\subset\hat{R}_{G}(g,\bm{s},\bm{m}).

Observe that the relation (
6.9
) can be solved for any
\mathnormal{M}_{i},\mathnormal{N}_{i},\mathnormal{T}_{ij}
, but not for any of
\mathnormal{A}_{i},\mathnormal{B}_{i},\mathnormal{C}_{i}
.
Thus, eliminating any one of
\mathnormal{M}_{i},\mathnormal{N}_{i},\mathnormal{T}_{ij}
from the list destroys the relation.
But beware that using this identity to express any
\mathnormal{N}_{i}
in terms of all the other matrices in the list does not guarantee that
\mathnormal{N}_{i}
is upper-triangular.
On the other hand, the matrix
\mathnormal{M}_{i}
or
\mathnormal{T}_{ij}
(corresponding to one of the loops
\gamma_{i}
or boundary segments
\delta_{i,j}
, but not a boundary loop
\delta_{i}
) is completely determined by the relation (
6.9
).
It follows that, if
s\geq 1
, then the sets
R_{G}(g,\bm{s},\bm{m})\subset\hat{R}_{G}(g,\bm{s},\bm{m})
are smooth, closed, affine algebraic subvarieties of
G^{2g+s+m+r}
isomorphic to
G^{2g+s+m-2}\times B^{r}
and
G^{2g+s+m-1}\times B^{r}
, respectively.
Finally, recall that
\dim G=n^{2}
and
\dim B=\tfrac{1}{2}(n^{2}+n)
.
Therefore, we obtain the following result.

Lemma 6.6
.

The sets
\hat{R}_{G}(g,\bm{s},\bm{m})
and
R_{G}(g,\bm{s},\bm{m})
defined above are smooth, closed, affine algebraic subvarieties of
G^{2g+s+m}\times B^{r}
of dimensions
n^{2}(2g+s+m-1)+\tfrac{1}{2}r(n^{2}+n)
and
n^{2}(2g+s+m-2)+\tfrac{1}{2}r(n^{2}+n)
, respectively.
Moreover, if
l+d\geq 1
, then eliminating any one of the matrices
\mathnormal{M}_{i}
or
\mathnormal{T}_{ij}
(i.e., projecting this component away) determines isomorphisms

\hat{R}_{G}(g,\bm{s},\bm{m})\overset{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.35623pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}{\longrightarrow}G^{2g+s+m-1}{\times}B^{r}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002ptR_{G}(g,\bm{s},\bm{m})\overset{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.35623pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}{\longrightarrow}G^{2g+s+m-2}{\times}B^{r}.

Finally, the following lemma summarises the discussion that led to
Definition 6.5
.

Lemma 6.7
.

Let
(\Sigma,P)
be any surface with marked boundary of genus
g
, with
\bm{s}=(l,r,d)
holes and
\bm{m}=(m_{1},\ldots,m_{d})
primary marked points.
Pick any basepoint
p_{0}\in P
.
Choose a finite presentation of the discrete fundamental groupoid
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)
of the form (
6.6
).
Then the correspondence (
6.7
) defines a bijection

R_{G}(\Sigma,P)\overset{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.35623pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}{\longrightarrow}R_{G}(g,\bm{s},\bm{m}).

(6.10)

Proof of
Proposition 6.4
.

This follows from
Lemma 6.6
and
Lemma 6.7
.
We endow
\hat{R}_{G}(\Sigma,P)
with the unique algebraic structure for which the bijective map (
6.10
) is algebraic.
Then the subset
R_{G}(\Sigma,P)
inherits the same algebraic structure.
One only has to check that if we change the finite presentation of the discrete fundamental groupoid, this algebraic structure does not change.
But this is clear because any other set of generators of the discrete fundamental groupoid are expressible in terms of the old generators as finite concatenations.
This means any two bijections obtained this way are related by an algebraic automorphism of
\hat{R}_{G}(g,\bm{s},\bm{m})
.
∎

6.3
Filtered, Framed and Projectively Framed Character Varieties

We are now ready to define decorated character varieties.
To motivate their definition, let us briefly return to the discussion we started in
subsection 6.1
and examine the groupoid of
filtered
representations
\textup{Rep}_{G}^{\mathrm{Fi}}\big(\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)\big)
.

Suppose
(E,F,\phi)
is a filtered representation of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)
.
It is always possible to choose an isomorphism
\chi_{p}:E_{p}\overset{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.35623pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{C}^{n}
for every
p\in P
which sends the flag
F_{p}
to the standard flag
\mathbb{C}^{\bullet}
; i.e., a filtered isomorphism
\chi_{p}:(E_{p},F_{p})\overset{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.35623pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}{\longrightarrow}(\mathbb{C}^{n},\mathbb{C}^{\bullet})
.
This defines a global trivialisation
\chi\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\left\{\chi_{p}:p\in P\right\}:E\overset{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.35623pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}{\longrightarrow}\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
which therefore sends the flag in each fibre to the standard flag; i.e., a filtered isomorphism
\chi:(E,F)\overset{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.35623pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}{\longrightarrow}(\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{n},\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{\bullet})
.
Any other such filtered isomorphism
\tilde{\chi}:(E,F)\overset{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.35623pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}{\longrightarrow}(\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{n},\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{\bullet})
differs from
\chi
by an automorphism that preserves the standard flag in each fibre of
\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
; i.e., by
g=\tilde{\chi}\circ\chi^{-1}\in\operatorname{\mathsf{Aut}}(\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{n},\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{\bullet})
.
So each component of
g=\left\{g_{p}:p\in P\right\}
is just an upper-triangular matrix,
g_{p}\in B
.

Similarly, suppose
\varphi:(E,F,\phi)\to(E^{\prime},F^{\prime},\phi^{\prime})
is a filtered isomorphism of representations and we choose trivialisations
\chi,\chi^{\prime}
of the filtered bundles
(E,F),(E^{\prime},F^{\prime})
.
By definition,
\varphi
sends each flag
F_{p}
to
F^{\prime}_{p}
in each fibre.
But this means that its matrix representation must preserve the standard flag; i.e.,
g=\chi^{\prime}\circ\varphi\circ\chi^{-1}\in\operatorname{\mathsf{Aut}}(\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{n},\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{\bullet})
.
So, again, we find that
g_{p}\in B
for every
p\in P
.

Motivated by this, we introduce the following three affine algebraic subgroups of
G_{P}=\operatorname{\mathsf{Aut}}(\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{n})
:

\displaystyle B_{P}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\prod_{p\in P}B=\operatorname{\mathsf{Aut}}(\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{n},\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{\bullet}),

(6.11)

\displaystyle U_{P}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\prod_{p\in P}U=\mathop{\mathsf{UAut}}\nolimits(\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{n},\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{\bullet}),

\displaystyle U^{\times}_{P}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=(\mathbb{C}^{\times})^{|P|}\times U_{P},

where
\mathop{\mathsf{UAut}}\nolimits(\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{n},\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{\bullet})
is the set of unipotent automorphisms of
\mathbb{C}^{n}
that preserve the standard flag.

These groups act on the decorated representation variety
R_{G}(\Sigma,P)
by mixed conjugation.
So we get three action groupoids on affine algebraic varieties:

\displaystyle B_{P}\ltimes R_{G}(\Sigma,P),

\displaystyle U_{P}\ltimes R_{G}(\Sigma,P),\hskip 20.00003pt\text{with}\hskip 20.00003ptg.\rho\mathrel{\mathop{:}}={\mathrm{t}}^{\ast}g\cdot\rho\cdot{\mathrm{s}}^{\ast}g^{-1}.

\displaystyle U^{\times}_{P}\ltimes R_{G}(\Sigma,P)

Their moduli spaces are the primary subject matter of this paper.

Definition 6.8
.

The
filtered character stack
, the
framed character stack
and the
projectively framed character stack
of any surface with marked boundary
(\Sigma,P)
with nonempty
P
are respectively defined as the following affine algebraic quotient stacks:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{X}^{\mathrm{Fi}}_{G}(\Sigma,P)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=R_{G}(\Sigma,P)/B_{P},

\displaystyle\mathfrak{X}^{\mathrm{Fr}}_{G}(\Sigma,P)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=R_{G}(\Sigma,P)/U_{P},

\displaystyle\mathfrak{X}^{\mathrm{PFr}}_{G}(\Sigma,P)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=R_{G}(\Sigma,P)/U_{P}^{\times}.

Collectively, we refer to all these moduli spaces as
decorated character stacks
.

The following lemma is a straightforward consequence of the definition of
\mathbb{P}GL_{n}
and of the action of
U_{P}^{\times}
, however it is important because it allows to use the Fock-Goncharov-Shen coordinates and their complexification to describe
\mathfrak{X}^{\mathrm{PFr}}_{G}(\Sigma,P)
:

Lemma 6.9
.

Let

R_{\mathbb{P}G}(\Sigma,P)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\left\{\rho~\Big|~\rho_{\delta_{p}}\in\mathbb{P}B\hskip 10.00002pt\forall p\in P_{\mathrm{II}}\right\}\subset\operatorname{\mathsf{Hom}}\big(\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P),\mathbb{P}GL_{n}\big),

then we have the following canonical bijection:

\mathfrak{X}^{\mathrm{PFr}}_{G}(\Sigma,P)\cong R_{\mathbb{P}G}(\Sigma,P)/\mathbb{P}U_{P}.

Proof.

This is a straightforward consequence of the definition of
\mathbb{P}GL_{n}
and of the action of
U_{P}^{\times}
.
∎

Remark 6.10
.

When
P=P_{{\mathrm{I}}}
, namely there are no
secondary marked points, we can write the decorated character varieties of
(\Sigma,P_{{\mathrm{I}}})
more explicitly:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{X}^{\mathrm{Fi}}_{G}(\Sigma,P_{{\mathrm{I}}})

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\mathsf{Hom}}\big(\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P_{{\mathrm{I}}}),G\big)\big/B_{P_{{\mathrm{I}}}},

\displaystyle\mathfrak{X}^{\mathrm{Fr}}_{G}(\Sigma,P_{{\mathrm{I}}})

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\mathsf{Hom}}\big(\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P_{{\mathrm{I}}}),G\big)\big/U_{P_{{\mathrm{I}}}},

\displaystyle\mathfrak{X}^{\mathrm{PFr}}_{G}(\Sigma,P_{{\mathrm{I}}})

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\mathsf{Hom}}\big(\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P_{{\mathrm{I}}}),G\big)\big/U^{\times}_{P_{{\mathrm{I}}}}.

The framed character variety of the form

\mathfrak{X}^{\mathrm{Fr}}_{SL_{n}(\mathbb{C})}(\Sigma,P_{{\mathrm{I}}})=\operatorname{\mathsf{Hom}}\big(\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P_{{\mathrm{I}}}),SL_{n}(\mathbb{C})\big)/U_{P_{{\mathrm{I}}}}

was introduced by Chekhov-Mazzocco-Rubtsov in
[
11
, §4]
(who called it the
decorated character variety
) based on the work of Li–Bland-Ševera in
[
25
, §6]
.
Thanks to the fact that

\operatorname{\mathsf{Hom}}\big(\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P_{{\mathrm{I}}}),SL_{n}(\mathbb{C})\big)/U_{P_{{\mathrm{I}}}}\to\operatorname{\mathsf{Hom}}\big(\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P_{{\mathrm{I}}}),\mathbb{P}G\big)/\mathbb{P}U_{P_{{\mathrm{I}}}}=\mathfrak{X}^{\mathrm{PFr}}_{G}(\Sigma,P_{{\mathrm{I}}}),

is a
n:1
covering,
Lemma
6.9
combined with the forgetful functor of Section
5
, implies that the complexified Fock-Goncharov-Shen variables coordinatize the Chekhov-Mazzocco-Rubtsov decorated character variety.

Lemma 6.11
.

For any surface with marked boundary
(\Sigma,P)
, there are canonical equivalences of categories between the groupoid of decorated representations of the discrete fundamental groupoid
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)
and a suitable mixed conjugation action groupoid of the decorated representation variety of
(\Sigma,P)
:

\displaystyle\textup{Rep}_{G}^{\mathrm{Fi}}\big(\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)\big)\cong B_{P}\ltimes R_{G}(\Sigma,P),

\displaystyle\textup{Rep}_{G}^{\mathrm{Fr}}\big(\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)\big)\cong U_{P}\ltimes R_{G}(\Sigma,P),

\displaystyle\textup{Rep}_{G}^{\mathrm{PFr}}\big(\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)\big)\cong U^{\times}_{P}\ltimes R_{G}(\Sigma,P)

Proof.

Explicitly, these equivalences are given by the inclusion functors from right to left. Namely,
the equivalence
B_{P}\ltimes R_{G}(\Sigma,P)\overset{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.35623pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}{\longrightarrow}\textup{Rep}_{G}^{\mathrm{Fi}}\big(\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)\big)

sends any homomorphism
\rho\in R_{G}(\Sigma,P)
to the representation
(E,F,\Phi)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=(\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{n},\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{\bullet},\rho)
.
Analogously, the equivalence
U_{P}\ltimes R_{G}(\Sigma,P)\overset{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.35623pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}{\longrightarrow}\textup{Rep}_{G}^{\mathrm{Fr}}\big(\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)\big)

sends any homomorphism
\rho\in R_{G}(\Sigma,P)
to the representation

(E,F,\Phi,\beta)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=(\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{n},\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{\bullet},\rho,\mathds{1})
, and similarly the equivalence
U^{\times}_{P}\ltimes R_{G}(\Sigma,P)\overset{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.35623pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}{\longrightarrow}\textup{Rep}_{G}^{\mathrm{Fr}}\big(\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)\big)

sends any homomorphism
\rho\in R_{G}(\Sigma,P)
to the representation

(E,F,\Phi,\bar{\beta})\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=(\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{n},\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{\bullet},\rho,[\mathds{1}])
.
In each case, an element
g=\left\{g_{p}\right\}\in B_{P}
,
U_{P}
or
U^{\times}_{P}
is viewed as a vector bundle automorphism
g\in\operatorname{\mathsf{Aut}}(\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{n},\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{\bullet})
,
\mathop{\mathsf{UAut}}\nolimits(\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{n},\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{\bullet})
or
\mathop{\mathsf{U^{\times}Aut}}\nolimits(\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{n},\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{\bullet})
.

Any inverse functor is given by matrix
representation which requires a choice of trivialisation

\chi:(E,F,\beta)\overset{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.35623pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}{\longrightarrow}(\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{n},\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{\bullet},\beta_{\mathrm{std}})
for every decorated vector bundle over
P
.
Once chosen, it is given for any object
(E,\phi,F,\beta)
and any morphism
\varphi
by

(E,\Phi,F,\beta)\mapsto\rho\mathrel{\mathop{:}}={\mathrm{t}}^{\ast}\chi\circ\phi\circ{\mathrm{s}}^{\ast}\chi^{-1}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{and}\hskip 20.00003pt\varphi\mapsto g\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\chi^{\prime}\circ\varphi\circ\chi^{-1}.

Just like in the proof of
Lemma 6.1
, we only have to check that the functor suggested above defines an inverse to the inclusion functor.
One direction is obvious:

\rho\mapsto(\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{n},\rho,\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{\bullet},\beta_{\mathrm{std}})\mapsto\rho

and similarly on morphisms.
For the other direction, suppose
(E,\phi,F,\beta)
is a decorated representation of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)
and let
\rho
be its matrix representation with respect to the chosen trivialisation
\chi
.
But then
\chi
itself defines the desired isomorphism
\chi:(E,\phi,F,\beta)\overset{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.35623pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}{\longrightarrow}(\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{n},\rho,\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{\bullet},\beta_{\mathrm{std}})
.
Lastly, an isomorphism of decorated representations
\varphi:(E,\phi,F,\beta)\to(E^{\prime},\phi^{\prime},F^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})
is in particular an isomorphism of representations
\varphi:(E,\phi)\to(E^{\prime},\phi^{\prime})
.
So its matrix representation defines an element
g=\left\{g_{p}:p\in P\right\}\in G_{P}
by
g_{p}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\chi^{\prime}_{p}\circ\varphi_{p}\circ\chi_{p}^{-1}\in G
which necessarily belongs to
B_{P}
,
U_{P}
or
U^{\times}_{P}
because
\varphi
respects decorations.
∎

Combined with
Proposition 4.19
, this lemma implies the following theorem which is one of our main results.

Theorem 6.12
.

For any surface with marked boundary
(\Sigma,P)
, there are canonical equivalences of categories between the groupoids of decorated local systems, decorated representations of the fundamental groupoid
\Pi_{1}(\Sigma)
, decorated representations of the discrete fundamental groupoid
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)
, and suitable mixed conjugation action groupoids over the decorated representation variety
R_{G}(\Sigma,P)
:

\textup{Loc}_{G}^{\square}(\Sigma,P)\cong\textup{Rep}_{G}^{\square}\big(\Pi_{1}(\Sigma),P\big)\cong\textup{Rep}_{G}^{\square}\big(\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)\big)\cong K_{P}^{\square}\ltimes R_{G}(\Sigma,P),

for any
\square\in\left\{{\mathrm{Fi}},{\mathrm{Fr}},{\mathrm{PFr}}\right\}
where
K^{\mathrm{Fi}}_{P}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=B_{P}
,
K^{\mathrm{Fr}}_{P}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=U_{P}
and
K^{\mathrm{PFr}}_{P}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=U^{\times}_{P}
.

Corollary 6.13
.

For any surface with marked boundary
(\Sigma,P)
with nonempty
P
, there are canonical bijections between the moduli spaces of decorated local systems, decorated representations of the fundamental groupoid
\Pi_{1}(\Sigma)
, decorated representations of the discrete fundamental groupoid
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)
, and the decorated character stacks of
(\Sigma,P)
:

\mathfrak{Loc}_{G}^{\square}(\Sigma,P)\cong\mathfrak{Rep}_{G}^{\square}\big(\Pi_{1}(\Sigma),P\big)\cong\mathfrak{Rep}_{G}^{\square}\big(\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)\big)\cong\mathfrak{X}_{G}^{\square}(\Sigma,P),

for any
\square\in\left\{{\mathrm{Fi}},{\mathrm{Fr}},{\mathrm{PFr}}\right\}
.
Consequently, all these moduli spaces have a natural structure of an affine algebraic quotient stack.

Collecting the facts from previous sections, we recall that the first of these bijections is given by the holonomy representation functor, the second one is given by the restriction functor, and the third one is given by choosing trivialisations of all vector bundles over
P
.

Recall the ramified covering of moduli spaces of filtered local systems induced by the forgetful functor that drops the flags at secondary marked points, as described in
section 5
.
The analogue for filtered character varieties reads as follows.

Corollary 6.14
.

Let
(\Sigma,P)
be any decorated surface.
Then the surjective map

\mathfrak{X}_{G}^{\mathrm{Fi}}(\Sigma,P)\longrightarrow\mathfrak{X}_{G}^{\mathrm{Fi}}(\Sigma,P_{\mathrm{I}})

induced by the forgetful restriction functor is a ramified covering of degree
(n!)^{r}
where
r
is the number of secondary marked points; i.e.,
r=|P_{\mathrm{II}}|
.
For any groupoid homomorphism
\rho:\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P_{\mathrm{I}})\to G
, the fibre above its equivalence class
[\rho]\in\mathfrak{X}_{G}^{\mathrm{Fi}}(\Sigma,P_{\mathrm{I}})
is canonically in bijection with the set
J_{W}(\rho_{\delta_{1}})\times\cdots\times J_{W}(\rho_{\delta_{r}})
of
r
-tuples of shuffled Jordan matrices of the same Jordan type as the value of
\rho
on the boundary loops
\delta_{1},\ldots,\delta_{r}
based at the secondary marked points
P_{\mathrm{II}}=\left\{p_{1},\ldots,p_{r}\right\}
.
In particular, if
P
consists of secondary marked points only (
P=P_{\mathrm{II}}
), then the filtered character variety is a ramified covering over the usual character variety:

\mathfrak{X}_{G}^{\mathrm{Fi}}(\Sigma,P)\longrightarrow\mathfrak{X}_{G}(\Sigma).

6.4
Finite-Dimensional Presentation

In this subsection, we give an explicit finite-dimensional presentation of decorated character varieties and hence the moduli spaces of decorated local systems and representations encountered in our paper.
The key is
Lemma 6.6
and
Lemma 6.7
which give an explicit finite-dimensional presentation of the decorated representation variety.

Thus, recall the algebraic variety
R_{G}(g,\bm{s},\bm{m})
from
Definition 6.5
.
We define a left group action on it by the group
G^{m+r}
as follows.
Write any element
x\in G^{m+r}
as
x=(x^{\prime},x^{\prime\prime})\in G^{m}{\times}G^{r}
where

x^{\prime}=\big(x_{ij}:1\leq i\leq d,~1\leq j\leq m_{i}\big)\in G^{m}~,\hskip 10.00002ptx^{\prime\prime}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{r})\in G^{r}.

Then the action of
x
on any element
(\bm{\mathnormal{A}},\bm{\mathnormal{B}},\bm{\mathnormal{M}},\bm{\mathnormal{T}},\bm{\mathnormal{C}},\bm{\mathnormal{D}};\bm{\mathnormal{N}})
of
\hat{R}_{G}(g,\bm{s},\bm{m})
is defined by mixed conjugation as follows:

\begin{gathered}x.\mathnormal{A}_{i}=x_{0}\mathnormal{A}_{i}x_{0}^{-1}~,\quad x.\mathnormal{B}_{i}=x_{0}\mathnormal{B}_{i}x_{0}^{-1}~,\\
x.\mathnormal{M}_{i}=x_{0}\mathnormal{M}_{i}x_{0}^{-1}~,\quad x.\mathnormal{N}_{i}=x_{i}\mathnormal{N}_{i}x_{i}^{-1}~,\\
x.\mathnormal{C}_{i}=x_{i1}\mathnormal{C}_{i}x_{0}^{-1}~,\quad x.\mathnormal{D}_{i}=x_{i}\mathnormal{D}_{i}x_{0}^{-1}~,\\
x.\mathnormal{T}_{ij}=x_{i,j+1}\mathnormal{T}_{ij}x_{ij}^{-1}~,\end{gathered}

(6.12)

where
j
is understood mod
m_{i}+1
.
The subgroups
U^{m+r}\subset B^{m+r}\subset G^{m+r+1}
inherit the same action, yielding two action groupoids

B^{m+r}\ltimes R_{G}(g,\bm{s},\bm{m})\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002ptU^{m+r}\ltimes R_{G}(g,\bm{s},\bm{m}).

Denote by
R_{G}(g,\bm{s},\bm{m})
the set

R_{G}(g,\bm{s},\bm{m})\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\left\{(\bm{\mathnormal{A}},\bm{\mathnormal{B}},\bm{\mathnormal{M}},\bm{\mathnormal{T}},\bm{\mathnormal{C}},\bm{\mathnormal{D}};\bm{\mathnormal{N}})~\Big|~\begin{subarray}{c}\textup{ $\mathnormal{C}_{1}=\mathds{1}$ if $d\geq 1\phantom{,r\geq 1}$ }\\
\textup{ $\mathnormal{D}_{1}=\mathds{1}$ if $d=0,r\geq 1$ }\end{subarray}\big.\right\}\subset\hat{R}_{G}(g,\bm{s},\bm{m}).

where
(\bm{\mathnormal{A}},\bm{\mathnormal{B}},\bm{\mathnormal{M}},\bm{\mathnormal{T}},\bm{\mathnormal{C}},\bm{\mathnormal{D}};\bm{\mathnormal{N}})
means that we eliminate one generator
M_{ij}
or
T_{i}
by using relation
6.9
.

Proposition 6.15
.

Let
(\Sigma,P)
be any surface with marked boundary with nonempty
P
of genus
g
with
\bm{s}=(l,r,d)
holes and
\bm{m}=(m_{1},\ldots,m_{d})
primary marked points.
Let
s\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=l+r+d
and
m\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=m_{1}+\ldots+m_{d}
.
Choose any finite presentation of the discrete fundamental groupoid
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,P)
of the form (
6.6
) and distinguish one of the generators
\gamma_{i}
or
\delta_{i,j}
.
Then there are canonical equivalences of categories

\textup{Loc}_{G}^{\square}(\Sigma,P)\cong K^{m+r}_{\square}\ltimes R_{G}(g,\bm{s},\bm{m})\cong K^{m+r}_{\square}\ltimes(G^{2g+s+m-2}{\times}B^{r})

and therefore canonical bijections

\mathfrak{Loc}_{G}^{\square}(\Sigma,P)\cong\mathfrak{X}_{G}^{\square}(\Sigma,P)\cong R_{G}(g,\bm{s},\bm{m})/K^{m+r}_{\square}\cong(G^{2g+s+m-2}{\times}B^{r})/K^{m+r}_{\square},

for any
\square\in\left\{{\mathrm{Fi}},{\mathrm{Fr}},{\mathrm{PFr}}\right\}
where
K_{\mathrm{Fi}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=B
,
K_{\mathrm{Fr}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=U
and
K_{\mathrm{PFr}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=U^{\times}
.

Thus, in particular, if
P
contains no secondary marked points (
r=0
), then

\displaystyle\mathfrak{X}_{G}^{{\mathrm{Fi}}}(\Sigma,P)

\displaystyle\cong G^{2g+s+m-2}/B^{m},

\displaystyle\mathfrak{X}_{G}^{{\mathrm{Fr}}}(\Sigma,P)

\displaystyle\cong G^{2g+s+m-2}/U^{m},

\displaystyle\mathfrak{X}_{G}^{{\mathrm{Fr}}}(\Sigma,P)

\displaystyle\cong G^{2g+s+m-2}/\big(U^{\times}\big)^{m}.

Corollary 6.16
.

Suppose
(\Sigma,P)
is a surface with marked boundary with nonempty
P
of genus
g
with
s
holes,
m
primary marked points, and
r
secondary marked points.
Then the moduli spaces of decorated local systems
\mathfrak{Loc}_{G}^{\square}(\Sigma,P)
are algebraic stacks of dimension

\displaystyle\dim\mathfrak{Loc}_{G}^{\mathrm{Fi}}(\Sigma,P)

\displaystyle=(2g+s-2)n^{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}m(n^{2}-n),

\displaystyle\dim\mathfrak{Loc}_{G}^{\mathrm{Fr}}(\Sigma,P)

\displaystyle=(2g+s-2)n^{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}m(n^{2}+n)-rn,

\displaystyle\dim\mathfrak{Loc}_{G}^{\mathrm{PFr}}(\Sigma,P)

\displaystyle=(2g+s-2)n^{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}m(n^{2}+n-2)-r(n-1).

Proof.

The dimension is calculated as follows:

\displaystyle\dim\mathfrak{Loc}_{G}^{\square}(\Sigma,P)

\displaystyle=(2g+s+m-2)\dim G+r\dim B-(m+r)\dim K_{\square}

\displaystyle=(2g+s-2)\dim G+m(\dim G-\dim K_{\square})+r(\dim B-\dim K_{\square})

\displaystyle=(2g+s-2)n^{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}m(n^{2}-n+2\nu_{\square})-r\nu_{\square},

where
\nu_{\mathrm{Fi}}=0
,
\nu_{\mathrm{Fr}}=n
and
\nu_{\mathrm{PFr}}=n-1
.
∎

Thus, recall the algebraic variety
\hat{R}_{G}(g,\bm{s},\bm{m})
from
Definition 6.5
.
We define a left group action on it by the group
G^{m+r+1}
as follows.
Write any element
x\in G^{m+r+1}
as
x=(x^{\prime},x^{\prime\prime},x_{0})\in G^{m}{\times}G^{r}{\times}G
where

x^{\prime}=\big(x_{ij}:1\leq i\leq d,~1\leq j\leq m_{i}\big)\in G^{m}~,\hskip 10.00002ptx^{\prime\prime}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{r})\in G^{r}~,\hskip 10.00002ptx_{0}\in G.

Then the action of
x
on any element
(\bm{\mathnormal{A}},\bm{\mathnormal{B}},\bm{\mathnormal{M}},\bm{\mathnormal{T}},\bm{\mathnormal{C}},\bm{\mathnormal{D}};\bm{\mathnormal{N}})
of
\hat{R}_{G}(g,\bm{s},\bm{m})
is defined by mixed conjugation as follows:

\begin{gathered}x.\mathnormal{A}_{i}=x_{0}\mathnormal{A}_{i}x_{0}^{-1}~,\quad x.\mathnormal{B}_{i}=x_{0}\mathnormal{B}_{i}x_{0}^{-1}~,\\
x.\mathnormal{M}_{i}=x_{0}\mathnormal{M}_{i}x_{0}^{-1}~,\quad x.\mathnormal{N}_{i}=x_{i}\mathnormal{N}_{i}x_{i}^{-1}~,\\
x.\mathnormal{C}_{i}=x_{i1}\mathnormal{C}_{i}x_{0}^{-1}~,\quad x.\mathnormal{D}_{i}=x_{i}\mathnormal{D}_{i}x_{0}^{-1}~,\\
x.\mathnormal{T}_{ij}=x_{i,j+1}\mathnormal{T}_{ij}x_{ij}^{-1}~,\end{gathered}

(6.13)

where
j
is understood mod
m_{i}+1
.
The subgroups
U^{m+r}{\times}G\subset B^{m+r}{\times}G\subset G^{m+r+1}
inherit the same action, yielding two action groupoids

(B^{m+r}{\times}G)\ltimes\hat{R}_{G}(g,\bm{s},\bm{m})\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pt(U^{m+r}{\times}G)\ltimes\hat{R}_{G}(g,\bm{s},\bm{m}).

Appendix A
Proof of
Proposition 1.4

The proof is broken down into several parts.

Extension of
\operatorname{Hol}
and
\operatorname{Mon}_{p}
to morphisms.

The correspondence
\operatorname{Hol}
defined above on objects extends to a functor because any morphism of sheaves
\varphi:{\mathcal{E}}\to{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime}
extends to an
{\mathcal{O}}_{\Sigma}
-linear morphism of sheaves
\varphi:{\mathcal{E}}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{\Sigma}\to{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{\Sigma}
(and hence a continuous vector bundle map
\varphi:\mathnormal{E}\to\mathnormal{E}^{\prime}
) by acting on the
{\mathcal{O}}_{\Sigma}
component by the identity.
Since the fibres of
\mathnormal{E}
are canonically isomorphic to the corresponding stalks of
{\mathcal{E}}
, the fact that the induced bundle map
\varphi:\mathnormal{E}\to\mathnormal{E}^{\prime}
intertwines the holonomy representations (and therefore defines a morphism of representations) is the content of the commutative diagram (
1.3
).
Similarly, the correspondence
\operatorname{Mon}_{p}
defined on objects above extends to a functor because any map of sheaves
\varphi:{\mathcal{E}}\to{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime}
restricts to a linear map on stalks
\varphi_{p}:{\mathcal{E}}_{p}\to{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime}_{p}
which intertwines the monodromy representations thanks again to the commutative diagram (
1.3
).

We need to check that these functors are fully faithful and essentially surjective.

Functors
\operatorname{Hol}
and
\operatorname{Mon}_{p}
are fully faithful.

The fully faithful nature of
\operatorname{Hol}
follows from the fact that the fibres of
\mathnormal{E}
are canonically isomorphic to the corresponding stalks of
{\mathcal{E}}
, and maps of sheaves are equal if they are equal stalk-by-stalk.
For faithfulness: if
\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}:{\mathcal{E}}\to{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime}
are two maps of sheaves which the holonomy functor sends to the continuous bundle maps
\tilde{\varphi}_{1},\tilde{\varphi}_{2}:\mathnormal{E}\to\mathnormal{E}^{\prime}
such that
\tilde{\varphi}_{1}=\tilde{\varphi}_{2}
, then
\tilde{\varphi}_{1},\tilde{\varphi}_{2}
are in particular equal fibre-by-fibre which means
\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}
are equal stalk-by-stalk.
For fullness: any continuous bundle map
\varphi:\mathnormal{E}={\mathcal{E}}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{\Sigma}\to\mathnormal{E}^{\prime}={\mathcal{E}}^{\prime}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{\Sigma}
determines linear maps
\varphi_{p}:\mathnormal{E}_{p}\to\mathnormal{E}^{\prime}_{p}
fibre-by-fibre and hence linear maps
\tilde{\varphi}_{p}:{\mathcal{E}}_{p}\to{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime}_{p}
stalk-by-stalk.
If
\varphi
intertwines the corresponding holonomy representations, then these maps glue into a map of sheaves
\tilde{\varphi}:{\mathcal{E}}\to{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime}
.
Similarly,
\operatorname{Mon}_{p}
is fully faithful because the vector space
{\mathcal{E}}_{p}
is the stalk of
{\mathcal{E}}
at
p
.

Functor
\operatorname{Mon}_{p}
is essentially surjective.

For the essential surjectivity, consider first the monodromy representation functor
\operatorname{Mon}_{p}
.
Given a representation
(E,\phi)
of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p)
, a local system
{\mathcal{E}}
with monodromy representation isomorphic to
(E,\phi)
may be constructed as follows.
Begin by noting that the set
\operatorname{\mathsf{GL}}(\mathbb{C}^{n},E)
of all linear isomorphisms
\mathbb{C}^{n}\overset{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.35623pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}{\longrightarrow}E
has a natural free and transitive right
G
-action by pre-composition:
f.g=f\circ g
for any
f\in GL(\mathbb{C}^{n},E)
and
g\in G=GL(\mathbb{C}^{n})
.
It also has a natural free and transitive left
\operatorname{\mathsf{GL}}(E)
-action by post-composition:
h.f=h\circ f
for any
h\in GL(E)
.
Then the representation
\phi:\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p)\to GL(E)
induces a left action of the fundamental group
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p)
on
\operatorname{\mathsf{GL}}(\mathbb{C}^{n},E)
by post-composition:
\gamma.f=\phi_{\gamma}\circ f
for any
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p)
.

At the same time,
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p)
acts on the universal cover
\pi:\tilde{\Sigma}\to\Sigma
based at
p
by deck transformations.
This action restricts to a free and transitive action on the fibre
\pi^{-1}(q)\subset\tilde{\Sigma}
of any point
q\in\Sigma
, and therefore the quotient

\hat{{\mathcal{E}}}_{q}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\big(\pi^{-1}(q)\times GL(\mathbb{C}^{n},E)\big)\big/\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p)

by the diagonal action of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p)
is a set with a free and transitive right
G
-action; i.e.,
\hat{{\mathcal{E}}}_{q}
is a
G
-torsor.
By the same token,
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p)
acts on the preimage
\pi^{-1}(U)\subset\tilde{\Sigma}
of any open subset
U\subset\Sigma
though not necessarily freely and transitively, so the quotient of
\pi^{-1}(U)\times GL(\mathbb{C}^{n},E)
by the diagonal action of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p)
is also a set with a right
G
-action though not necessarily a
G
-torsor.
Nonetheless, if we let
\hat{{\mathcal{E}}}
be the sheaf associated with the presheaf

U\mapsto\big(\pi^{-1}(U)\times GL(\mathbb{C}^{n},E)\big)\big/\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p),

(A.1)

then
\hat{{\mathcal{E}}}
is a locally constant sheaf of sets equipped with a right
G
-action, and its stalk at any
q\in\Sigma
is nothing but the
G
-torsor
\hat{{\mathcal{E}}}_{q}
.
In other words,
\hat{{\mathcal{E}}}
is a
G
-local system and it gives rise to the linear local system
{\mathcal{E}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\hat{{\mathcal{E}}}\times_{G}\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{n}_{\Sigma}
as explained in
Lemma 1.1
.
For the benefit of the reader, we remark that, by abuse of notation, the sheaves
\hat{{\mathcal{E}}}
and
{\mathcal{E}}
are sometimes denoted in the literature by

\hat{{\mathcal{E}}}=\mathrel{\mathop{:}}\big(\tilde{\Sigma}\times GL(\mathbb{C}^{n},E)\big)/\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p)\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pt{\mathcal{E}}=\mathrel{\mathop{:}}\big(\tilde{\Sigma}\times E\big)/\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p).

The reason this notation makes sense is that the universal cover
\tilde{\Sigma}
can itself be thought of as a sheaf of sets on
\Sigma
(in fact, a
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p)
-local system on
\Sigma
).

For future use, the stalks of
{\mathcal{E}}
can be described more explicitly.
Using the canonical isomorphism
GL(\mathbb{C}^{n},E)\times_{G}\mathbb{C}^{n}\overset{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.35623pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}{\longrightarrow}E
given by
(f,v)\mapsto f(v)
, we have that the stalk of
{\mathcal{E}}
at any point
q\in\Sigma
is the quotient by the diagonal action of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p)
of
\pi^{-1}(q)\times E
which is the disjoint infinite union of copies of the vector space
E
enumerated by the preimages of
q
on the universal cover
\tilde{\Sigma}
:

{\mathcal{E}}_{q}=\big(\pi^{-1}(q)\times E\big)\big/\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p).

In other words, a vector
e\in{\mathcal{E}}_{q}
is the same thing as a collection
e=\big\{e_{\tilde{q}}\in E:\tilde{q}\in\pi^{-1}(q)\big\}
of infinitely many vectors in
E
, one for each preimage of
q
in the universal cover
\tilde{\Sigma}
, whose components are related by the action of the fundamental group
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p)
through the representation
\phi:\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p)\to GL(E)
:

{\mathcal{E}}_{q}=\left\{e\in\pi^{-1}(q)\times E~\Big|~e_{\gamma.\tilde{q}}=\phi_{\gamma}(e_{\tilde{q}})\hskip 10.00002pt\text{for all $\tilde{q}\in\tilde{U}$ and $\gamma\in\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p)$ }\right\},

(A.2)

where
\gamma.\tilde{q}
denotes the action of the deck transformation determined by
\gamma
.
In particular, since
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p)
acts freely and transitively on
\pi^{-1}(q)
for any
q\in\Sigma
, the stalk
{\mathcal{E}}_{q}
is isomorphic to the vector space
E
but not canonically.
To fix such an isomorphism is to choose a point
\tilde{q}
in the fibre
\pi^{-1}(q)
, which is the same thing as choosing a path
\gamma
on
\Sigma
that starts at
p
and ends at
q
.
This path determines the desired isomorphism which is nothing but the holonomy isomorphism
\operatorname{hol}_{\gamma}:E\overset{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.35623pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}{\longrightarrow}{\mathcal{E}}_{q}
of
{\mathcal{E}}
along
\gamma
.
On the other hand, since the chosen universal cover
\tilde{\Sigma}
is based at
p
, the fibre
\pi^{-1}(p)
has a distinguished point corresponding to the constant path at
p
.
Therefore, the stalk
{\mathcal{E}}_{p}
is canonically isomorphic to
E
.

We claim now that the monodromy representation of
{\mathcal{E}}
is isomorphic to
(E,\phi)
.
Indeed, since the stalk of
{\mathcal{E}}
at
p
is canonically isomorphic to
E
, the monodromy of
{\mathcal{E}}
around a loop
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p)
is given by
\gamma.e=\phi_{\gamma}(e)
for any
e\in E
.
This is because under the isomorphism
E\cong GL(\mathbb{C}^{n},E)\times_{G}\mathbb{C}^{n}
, every vector
e\in E
is of the form
e=f(v)
for some
(f,v)\in GL(\mathbb{C}^{n},E)\times_{G}\mathbb{C}^{n}
.
And the holonomy of the local system
\hat{{\mathcal{E}}}
along
\gamma
is computed as
\gamma.f=\phi_{\gamma}\circ f
, which means
\gamma.(f,v)=(\gamma.f,v)=\big(\phi_{\gamma}\circ f,v\big)
.
Consequently, the functor
\operatorname{Mon}_{p}
is essentially surjective, and so the proof that
\operatorname{Mon}_{p}
is an equivalence of categories is complete.

Functor
\operatorname{Hol}
is essential surjective.

Now we turn our attention to the essential surjectivity of the holonomy representation functor
\operatorname{Hol}
.
Given a representation
(\mathnormal{E},\Phi)
of the fundamental groupoid
\Pi_{1}(\Sigma)
, let
(E,\phi)
be its restriction to a representation of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p)
, and then let
{\mathcal{E}}
be the linear local system with
\operatorname{Mon}_{p}({\mathcal{E}})\cong(E,\phi)
as constructed above.
We claim that the holonomy representation
(\mathnormal{E}^{\prime},\Phi^{\prime})\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\operatorname{Hol}({\mathcal{E}})=({\mathcal{E}}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{\Sigma},\operatorname{hol})
is isomorphic to the original representation
(\mathnormal{E},\Phi)
.

The first fact we need is that the representation
\Phi
determines canonical local trivialisations of the vector bundle
\mathnormal{E}
.
More precisely, for any point
q\in\Sigma
, let
\mathnormal{E}_{q}
be the fibre of
\mathnormal{E}
at
q
, and choose any simply connected neighbourhood
U\subset\Sigma
of
q
.
Then the homomorphism
\Phi
induces a vector bundle isomorphism
\psi_{q}:\mathnormal{E}|_{U}\overset{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.35623pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}{\longrightarrow}\mathnormal{E}_{q}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{U}
where
\mathnormal{E}_{q}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{U}
is the sheaf of continuous sections of the trivial vector bundle
\mathnormal{E}_{q}\times U\to U
.
Indeed, for every point
q^{\prime}\in U
, there is a path from
q
to
q^{\prime}
which is entirely contained in
U
.
Such paths are unique up to homotopy with fixed endpoints, so for every
q^{\prime}\in U
there is a unique element
\gamma\in\Pi_{1}(U)
which determines an isomorphism
\Phi_{\gamma}:\mathnormal{E}_{q}\overset{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.35623pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}{\longrightarrow}\mathnormal{E}_{q^{\prime}}
.
Since
\Phi:\Pi_{1}(U)\to GL(\mathnormal{E}|_{U})
is a continuous map, as
q^{\prime}
varies continuously in
U
, the unique element
\gamma
varies continuously in
\Pi_{1}(U)
, thereby giving a continuous identification of all fibres of
\mathnormal{E}
over
U
with the fibre
\mathnormal{E}_{q}
.
Furthermore, for any
q^{\prime}\in U
, the change of trivialisation from
\psi_{q}
to
\psi_{q^{\prime}}:\mathnormal{E}|_{U}\overset{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.35623pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}{\longrightarrow}\mathnormal{E}_{q^{\prime}}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{U}
is nothing but the isomorphism
\Phi_{\gamma}
itself, extended to act on the
{\mathcal{O}}_{U}
-component by the identity:

\psi_{q^{\prime}}\circ\psi_{q}^{-1}=\Phi_{\gamma}:\mathnormal{E}_{q}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{U}\overset{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.35623pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}{\longrightarrow}\mathnormal{E}_{q^{\prime}}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{U}.

(A.3)

Next, we observe that, for essentially the same reason, the vector bundle
\mathnormal{E}
is canonically the trivial bundle with fibre
E=\mathnormal{E}_{p}
once pulled back to the universal cover.
More precisely, let
\pi:\tilde{\Sigma}\to\Sigma
be the universal cover based at
p
.
Then
\Phi
induces a bundle isomorphism
\psi:\pi^{\ast}\mathnormal{E}\overset{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.35623pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}{\longrightarrow}E\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{\tilde{\Sigma}}
where
E\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{\tilde{\Sigma}}
is the sheaf of continuous sections of the trivial vector bundle
\tilde{\Sigma}\times E\to\tilde{\Sigma}
.
Indeed, any point
\tilde{q}\in\tilde{\Sigma}
represents a homotopy class
\gamma\in\Pi_{1}(\Sigma)
of paths with source
p
and target
q=\pi(\tilde{q})
.
Meanwhile, the fibre of
\pi^{\ast}\mathnormal{E}
at
\tilde{q}
is by definition equal to the fibre
\mathnormal{E}_{q}
, but now we have a canonical isomorphism
\Phi_{\gamma}:\mathnormal{E}_{p}\overset{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.35623pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}{\longrightarrow}\mathnormal{E}_{q}
.
Since
\tilde{\Sigma}
is simply connected, this defines a global trivialisation of
\pi^{\ast}\mathnormal{E}
.

The third and final observation is that local sections of
\mathnormal{E}
are precisely the
\phi
-equivariant local sections of the trivial bundle
\tilde{\Sigma}\times E\to\tilde{\Sigma}
.
More precisely, suppose
\tilde{q}_{1},\tilde{q}_{2}\in\tilde{\Sigma}
are points in the same fibre over
q\in\Sigma
.
They represent paths
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}\in\Pi_{1}(\Sigma)
with source
p
and target
q
which together form a loop
\gamma_{2}^{-1}\circ\gamma_{1}\in\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p)
.
Now, under the global trivialisation
\psi
, two vectors
e_{1}\in E\cong\pi^{\ast}\mathnormal{E}_{\tilde{q}_{1}}
and
e_{2}\in E\cong\pi^{\ast}\mathnormal{E}_{\tilde{q}_{2}}
determine the same vector in the fibre
\mathnormal{E}_{q}
if and only if
\Phi_{\gamma_{1}}(e_{1})=\Phi_{\gamma_{2}}(e_{2})
i.e.
e_{2}=\phi_{\gamma}(e_{1})
.
Since
\pi^{\ast}\mathnormal{E}|_{\pi^{-1}(q)}\cong\pi^{-1}(q)\times E
, a vector
e
in
\mathnormal{E}_{q}
may be regarded equivalently as a collection
e=\big\{e_{\tilde{q}}\in E:\tilde{q}\in\pi^{-1}(q)\big\}
of infinitely many vectors in
E
enumerated by the fibre
\pi^{-1}(q)
whose components are related by the same action of the fundamental group
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p)
as in (
A.2
) through the restriction
\phi:\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p)\to GL(E)
of the given representation
\Phi
.
In other words, for every
q
, there is a canonical isomorphism between the fibre
\mathnormal{E}_{q}
and the stalk
{\mathcal{E}}_{q}
:

\eta_{q}:\mathnormal{E}_{q}\overset{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.35623pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}{\longrightarrow}{\mathcal{E}}_{q}=\big(\pi^{-1}(q)\times E\big)\big/\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p).

(A.4)

We also note that under this isomorphism, the representation
\Phi
becomes the holonomy of the local system
{\mathcal{E}}
; i.e., for every
\gamma\in\Pi_{1}(\Sigma)
with source
q
and target
q^{\prime}
, there is a commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathnormal{E}_{q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}}_{q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathnormal{E}_{q^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}}_{q^{\prime}}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{q}}

\scriptstyle\sim

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi_{\gamma}}

\scriptstyle\sim

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{hol}_{\gamma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{q^{\prime}}}

(A.5)

Combining the above three observations, we conclude that, for every
q\in\Sigma
and every simply connected neighbourhood
U\subset\Sigma
of
q
, there is a canonical isomorphism

\mathnormal{E}|_{U}\underset{\psi_{q}}{\overset{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.35623pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}{\longrightarrow}}\mathnormal{E}_{q}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{U}\underset{\eta_{q}}{\overset{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.35623pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}{\longrightarrow}}{\mathcal{E}}_{q}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{U}.

(A.6)

At the same time, since
U
is simply connected, the restriction of the local system
{\mathcal{E}}
to
U
is a constant sheaf isomorphic to the constant sheaf with value
{\mathcal{E}}_{q}
.
In particular, it gives a canonical trivialisation of the bundle
\mathnormal{E}^{\prime}
over
U
:

\nu_{q}:{\mathcal{E}}_{q}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{U}\overset{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.35623pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}{\longrightarrow}({\mathcal{E}}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{\Sigma})|_{U}=\mathnormal{E}^{\prime}|_{U}.

Altogether, we have constructed an isomorphism
\mu_{q}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\nu_{q}\circ\eta_{q}\circ\psi_{q}:\mathnormal{E}|_{U}\overset{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.35623pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}{\longrightarrow}\mathnormal{E}^{\prime}|_{U}
.

We can now choose any open covering of
\Sigma
by simply connected subsets and glue these local isomorphisms
\mu_{q}:\mathnormal{E}|_{U}\overset{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.35623pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}{\longrightarrow}\mathnormal{E}^{\prime}|_{U}
into a global isomorphism
\mu:\mathnormal{E}\overset{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.35623pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}{\longrightarrow}\mathnormal{E}^{\prime}
, provided they agree on double overlaps.
To see this, it is enough to choose two points
q,q^{\prime}\in U
in an arbitrary simply connected subset
U\subset\Sigma
and demonstrate that the two isomorphisms
\mu_{q},\mu_{q^{\prime}}:\mathnormal{E}|_{U}\overset{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.35623pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}{\longrightarrow}\mathnormal{E}^{\prime}|_{U}
agree.
Let
\gamma\in\Pi_{1}(U)
be the unique-up-to-homotopy path from
q
to
q^{\prime}
.
Recall from (
A.3
) that the trivialisations
\psi_{q}
and
\psi_{q^{\prime}}
of
\mathnormal{E}|_{U}
are related by the representation
\Phi_{\gamma}
.
Similarly, the trivialisations
\nu_{q}
and
\nu_{q^{\prime}}
of
\mathnormal{E}^{\prime}|_{U}
are related by the holonomy
\operatorname{hol}_{\gamma}
of the local system
{\mathcal{E}}
along
\gamma
.
Thus, unravelling their definition, the isomorphisms
\mu_{q}
and
\mu_{q^{\prime}}
agree by virtue of the commutativity of the following diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathnormal{E}|_{U}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathnormal{E}_{q}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{U}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}}_{q}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{U}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathnormal{E}^{\prime}|_{U}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathnormal{E}|_{U}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathnormal{E}_{q^{\prime}}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{U}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}}_{q^{\prime}}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{U}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathnormal{E}^{\prime}|_{U}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{q}}

\scriptstyle\sim

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi_{\gamma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu_{q}}

\scriptstyle\sim

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{hol}_{\gamma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{q^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{q^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu_{q^{\prime}}}

This diagram also demonstrates the fact that the isomorphism
\mu:\mathnormal{E}\overset{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.35623pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}{\longrightarrow}\mathnormal{E}^{\prime}
intertwines the representations
\Phi
and
\Phi^{\prime}=\operatorname{hol}
.
This is because they are both groupoid homomorphisms and any path on
\Sigma
is the concatenation of finitely many ‘short’ paths contained entirely in simply connected subsets where the above diagram is valid.
This completes the proof that
\operatorname{Hol}
is an equivalence of categories.

Functor
\mathnormal{R}
is an equivalence.

Finally, we show that the restriction functor
\mathnormal{R}
is also an equivalence of categories.
Recall that the monodromy representation functor
\operatorname{Mon}_{p}
is the composition of the holonomy representation functor
\operatorname{Hol}
and the restriction functor.
The essential surjectivity of the restriction functor follows from that of
\operatorname{Mon}_{p}
and
\operatorname{Hol}
.
For if
(E,\phi)
is any representation of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p)
, let
{\mathcal{E}}
be a local system such that
\operatorname{Mon}_{p}({\mathcal{E}})\cong(E,\phi)
, and take
(\mathnormal{E},\Phi)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\operatorname{Hol}({\mathcal{E}})
.
Then the restriction of
(\mathnormal{E},\Phi)
to
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p)
is literally the monodromy representation of
{\mathcal{E}}
, i.e.
\mathnormal{R}(\mathnormal{E},\Phi)=\operatorname{Mon}_{p}({\mathcal{E}})
, which we already know is isomorphic to
(E,\phi)
.

The restriction functor is obviously full but it is less clear why it is faithful.
That is to say, if
(\mathnormal{E},\Phi),(\mathnormal{E}^{\prime},\Phi^{\prime})
is a pair of representations of
\Pi_{1}(\Sigma)
, and
(E,\phi),(E^{\prime},\phi^{\prime})
are their restrictions to representations of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma,p)
, why does the restriction functor induce a bijection between
\mathnormal{R}:\mathop{\mathsf{Iso}}\nolimits\big((\mathnormal{E},\Phi),(\mathnormal{E}^{\prime},\Phi^{\prime})\big)\overset{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.35623pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}{\longrightarrow}\mathop{\mathsf{Iso}}\nolimits\big((E,\phi),(E^{\prime},\phi^{\prime})\big)
?
This can be argued as follows.
Using the essential surjectivity of
\operatorname{Hol}
and
\operatorname{Mon}_{p}
, let
{\mathcal{E}}
be a local system such that
\operatorname{Hol}({\mathcal{E}})=\mathrel{\mathop{:}}(\tilde{\mathnormal{E}},\tilde{\Phi})\cong(\mathnormal{E},\Phi)
and
\operatorname{Mon}_{p}({\mathcal{E}})=\mathrel{\mathop{:}}(\tilde{E},\tilde{\phi})\cong(E,\phi)
.
Same for
{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime}
.
By virtue of these isomorphisms, there are bijections
\mathop{\mathsf{Iso}}\nolimits\big(({\mathnormal{E}},{\Phi}),({\mathnormal{E}}^{\prime},{\Phi}^{\prime})\big)\cong\mathop{\mathsf{Iso}}\nolimits\big((\tilde{\mathnormal{E}},\tilde{\Phi}),(\tilde{\mathnormal{E}}^{\prime},\tilde{\Phi}^{\prime})\big)
and
\mathop{\mathsf{Iso}}\nolimits\big(({E},{\phi}),({E}^{\prime},{\phi}^{\prime})\big)\cong\mathop{\mathsf{Iso}}\nolimits\big((\tilde{E},\tilde{\phi}),(\tilde{E}^{\prime},\tilde{\phi}^{\prime})\big)
.
At the same time, we also have bijections
\operatorname{Hol}:\mathop{\mathsf{Iso}}\nolimits({\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime})\overset{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.35623pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}{\longrightarrow}\big((\tilde{\mathnormal{E}},\tilde{\Phi}),(\tilde{\mathnormal{E}}^{\prime},\tilde{\Phi}^{\prime})\big)
and
\operatorname{Mon}_{p}:\mathop{\mathsf{Iso}}\nolimits({\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime})\overset{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.35623pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}{\longrightarrow}\big((\tilde{E},\tilde{\phi}),(\tilde{E}^{\prime},\tilde{\phi}^{\prime})\big)
.

Appendix B
Invariant Flags and Shuffled Jordan Types

Suppose
(E,\phi)
is an
n
-dimensional complex vector space equipped with an endomorphism
\phi\in\operatorname{\mathsf{End}}(E)
.
Such a pair is sometimes called a
linear system
.
In this subsection, we ask the following question:
What are the possible ways to equip the vector space
E
with a complete
\phi
-invariant flag
F
up to an automorphism that commutes with
\phi
?
In other words, we want to understand the moduli set

\sf{Fi}_{\phi}(E)\big/{\sim}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{where}\hskip 10.00002pt\sf{Fi}_{\phi}(E)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\left\{F\in\sf{Fi}(E)~\Big|~\phi(F_{i})\subset F_{i}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{for all $i$ }\right\}

and
F\sim F^{\prime}
if there is a filtered isomorphism
\psi:(E,F)\overset{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.35623pt}{$\scriptstyle\sim$}}\,}{\longrightarrow}(E,F^{\prime})
such that
\phi\psi=\psi\phi
.
We remark that for
E=\mathbb{C}^{n}
and
\phi
nilpotent, the set
\sf{Fi}_{\phi}(\mathbb{C}^{n})
is called the
Springer fibre
over
\phi
; see, e.g.,
[
34
]
.
We will prove the following fact which, as far we can tell, has not yet appeared in the literature.

Proposition B.1
.

For any
(E,\phi)
, the moduli set
\sf{Fi}_{\phi}(E)/{\sim}
is finite and its size can be read off from the Jordan type of
\phi
.
In particular, if
\phi
has all-distinct eigenvalues, then
\sf{Fi}_{\phi}(E)/{\sim}
has
n!
points which are in one-to-one correspondence with total orders on the set of
n
eigenvalues of
\phi
.

In the case of all-distinct eigenvalues, this proposition follows from
Lemma B.5
below.
The general assertion will follow as a consequence of
Proposition B.11
below which uses the complete invariant of
\phi
-invariant flags on
E
called
shuffled Jordan type
, introduced in
Definition B.7
below.
A shuffled Jordan type is essentially a total order on the boxes of the Young tableau of
\phi
.
Thus, any equivalence class in
\sf{Fi}_{\phi}(E)
can be described as the set of all complete
\phi
-invariant flags with a fixed shuffled Jordan type.
Said differently, the moduli set
\sf{Fi}_{\phi}(E)/{\sim}
is in bijection with the set of all possible shuffled Jordan types on
(E,\phi)
, and the size of this set can be deduced from the Jordan type of
\phi
.
Let us begin by examining a few illustrative examples.

Example B.2
.

If
E=\mathbb{C}^{2}
and
\phi=\operatorname{diag}(1,2)
, then the only two possible
\phi
-invariant flags on
E
are the standard flag
\mathbb{C}^{\bullet}=\big(\left\langle e_{1}\right\rangle\subset\mathbb{C}^{2}\big)
and its opposite
\bar{\mathbb{C}^{\bullet}}=\big(\left\langle e_{2}\right\rangle\subset\mathbb{C}^{2}\big)
.
Any automorphism of
\mathbb{C}^{2}
that commutes with
\phi
must be a diagonal matrix, but a diagonal matrix cannot move the standard flag to the opposite flag.
Therefore, the moduli set
\sf{Fi}_{\phi}(\mathbb{C}^{2})/{\sim}
for
\phi=\operatorname{diag}(1,2)
has only two elements because the set
\sf{Fi}_{\phi}(\mathbb{C}^{2})
itself is finite and consists of just two inequivalent flags
\mathbb{C}^{\bullet}
and
\bar{\mathbb{C}^{\bullet}}
.
This is not generally the case: if
\phi=\operatorname{diag}(1,1)
, then any flag
F
is
\phi
-invariant, so
\sf{Fi}_{\phi}(\mathbb{C}^{2})=\sf{Fi}(\mathbb{C}^{2})
which is far from a finite set.
However, all these flags are equivalent because any automorphism of
\mathbb{C}^{2}
commutes with
\phi
, so
\sf{Fi}_{\phi}(\mathbb{C}^{2})/{\sim}
is a single point.

Example B.3
.

Next, consider the automorphism of
\mathbb{C}^{3}
given by a single Jordan block:

\phi=\begin{bmatrix}1&1&0\\
0&1&1\\
0&0&1\end{bmatrix}.

Then the only
\phi
-invariant flag is the standard flag
\mathbb{C}^{\bullet}
, meaning
\sf{Fi}_{\phi}(\mathbb{C}^{3})
is a single point.
Indeed, if
F
is a
\phi
-invariant flag, its first step
F_{1}
is a one-dimensional
\phi
-invariant subspace that must therefore equal the unique one-dimensional
\phi
-invariant subspace, namely
\left\langle e_{1}\right\rangle
.
Towards a contradiction, suppose
F_{2}
is not the standard flag, so that
e_{2}\not\in F_{2}
and we must have
ae_{2}+e_{3}\in F_{2}
for some
a\in\mathbb{C}
.
Since
F_{2}
is a
\phi
-invariant subspace, it follows that
\phi(ae_{2}+e_{3})=ae_{1}+(a+1)e_{2}+e_{3}
also belongs to
F_{2}
.
But this means that both
ae_{2}+e_{3}
and
(a+1)e_{2}+e_{3}
must also belong to
F_{2}
, so it follows that
e_{2}\in F_{2}
, a contradiction.

Example B.4
.

Finally, consider the automorphism of
\mathbb{C}^{3}
with triple eigenvalue
1
having two Jordan blocks of size
1
and
2
:

\phi=\begin{bmatrix}1&0&0\\
0&1&1\\
0&0&1\end{bmatrix}.

Then the set
\sf{Fi}_{\phi}(\mathbb{C}^{3})
has infinitely-many flags which we organise into three separate groups including a continuous one-parameter family of flags
\left\{F^{c}\right\}_{c\in\mathbb{C}}
and two other flags
F^{\prime}
and
F^{\prime\prime}
defined as follows:

\displaystyle F^{c}

\displaystyle=\big(\left\langle e_{1}+ce_{2}\right\rangle\subset\left\langle e_{1},e_{2}\right\rangle\subset\mathbb{C}^{3}\big),

\displaystyle F^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\big(\left\langle e_{2}\right\rangle\subset\left\langle e_{1},e_{2}\right\rangle\subset\mathbb{C}^{3}\big),

\displaystyle F^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle=\big(\left\langle e_{2}\right\rangle\subset\left\langle e_{2},e_{3}\right\rangle\subset\mathbb{C}^{3}\big).

Indeed,
\phi
has two eigenvectors
e_{1},e_{2}
so
F_{1}
of any
\phi
-invariant flag
F
must be generated by some linear combination
ae_{1}+be_{2}
.
If
F_{2}
contains the generalised eigenvector
e_{3}
, then by the same argument as in
Example B.3
it follows that
F_{1}
must be generated by
e_{2}
, yielding
F_{2}=\left\langle e_{2},e_{3}\right\rangle
.
Otherwise,
F_{2}=\left\langle e_{1},e_{2}\right\rangle
.

Any invertible matrix
\psi\in GL_{3}
that satisfies
\psi\phi=\phi\psi
must be of the following form:

\psi=\begin{bmatrix}x&0&y\\
z&u&v\\
0&0&u\end{bmatrix}

for some
x,y,z,u,v\in\mathbb{C}
with
x,u\neq 0
.
First, we observe that all flags
F^{c}
are equivalent to each other because the matrix
\psi
with
y,v=0
,
u,x=1
, and
z=c

commutes with
\phi
and moves the flag
F^{0}=\big(\left\langle e_{1}\right\rangle\subset\left\langle e_{1},e_{2}\right\rangle\subset\mathbb{C}^{3}\big)
to the flag
F^{c}
.
On the other hand, no
\psi
that commutes with
\phi
can give an equivalence between any two of
F^{0},F^{\prime},F^{\prime\prime}
.
Consequently, the moduli set
\sf{Fi}_{\phi}(\mathbb{C}^{3})/{\sim}
consists of three points.

B.1
Review of Linear Algebra and Jordan Types

To state and prove
Proposition B.11
, we first recall some elementary facts from linear algebra.
Suppose
F
is any complete
\phi
-invariant flag on
E
.
If
\phi
has all-distinct eigenvalues (so that
E
decomposes as a direct sum of one-dimensional eigenspaces), then in particular
F_{1}
must be an eigenspace.
But then
F_{2}
must contain an eigenvector of
\phi
not in
F_{1}
, so it decomposes into a direct sum of
F_{1}
and another eigenspace.
And so on.
We summarise this as follows.

Lemma B.5
.

For any
n
-dimensional linear system
(E,\phi)
with all-distinct eigenvalues, any
\phi
-invariant flag
F
is necessarily of the form

F_{i}=E_{\lambda_{1}}\oplus\cdots\oplus E_{\lambda_{i}}

where
\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{n}
are the eigenvalues of
\phi
and
E_{\lambda_{i}}
is the
\lambda_{i}
-eigenspace.
Thus, in this case,
\sf{Fi}_{\phi}(E)/{\sim}=\sf{Fi}_{\phi}(E)
is in bijection with the finite set of size
n!
of all possible orders
\tau=(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{n})
of the set of eigenvalues of
\phi
.

More generally, for any eigenvalue
\lambda
with multiplicity
m
, we consider the generalised
\lambda
-eigenspace

E_{\lambda}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\ker\big((\phi-\lambda\mathrm{id})^{m}\big)\subset E\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and let}\hskip 10.00002pt\nu_{\lambda}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=(\phi-\lambda\mathrm{id})\big|_{E_{\lambda}}:E_{\lambda}\to E_{\lambda}.

Recall that
E_{\lambda}
is an
m
-dimensional vector space equipped with a canonical
m
-step filtration by kernels of powers of the nilpotent part
\nu_{\lambda}
:

0\subset\ker(\nu_{\lambda})\subset\ker(\nu_{\lambda}^{2})\subset\cdots\subset\ker(\nu_{\lambda}^{m})=E_{\lambda}.

This is not a flag in general because this sequence typically stabilises to
E_{\lambda}
sooner than step
m
.
However, we can always reduce this filtration in a unique way to a partial flag of some length
l\leq m
by deleting all duplicate subspaces:

E_{\lambda}^{\bullet}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\Big(0\subset E_{\lambda}^{1}\subset\cdots\subset E_{\lambda}^{l}=E_{\lambda}\Big)\hskip 10.00002pt\text{where}\hskip 10.00002ptE_{\lambda}^{k}=\ker(\nu_{\lambda}^{k}).

(B.1)

Now, suppose
e_{0}\in E_{\lambda}^{k}\smallsetminus E_{\lambda}^{k-1}
is a generalised eigenvector of
\phi
of some rank
k\geq 1
.
Recall that this means
\nu_{\lambda}^{k}(e_{0})=0
but
e_{j}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\nu^{j}_{\lambda}(e_{0})\neq 0
for all
j=0,\ldots,k-1
.
These vectors form a
Jordan chain

\left\{e_{k-1},\ldots,e_{1},e_{0}\right\}\subset E_{\lambda}
of length
k
; i.e., an ordered set of linearly independent vectors, spanning a
k
-dimensional
\phi
-invariant subspace
E_{\lambda,0}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\left\langle e_{k-1},\ldots,e_{1},e_{0}\right\rangle\subset E_{\lambda}
.
This Jordan chain is called
maximal
if
e_{0}\not\in\sf{im}(\nu_{\lambda})
; i.e., there does not exist
e^{\prime}_{0}\in E_{\lambda}
such that
e_{0}=\nu_{\lambda}(e^{\prime}_{0})
.
The subspace
E_{\lambda,0}
spanned by a maximal Jordan chain is sometimes called a
Jordan block
.

Any two Jordan blocks are either equal or disjoint, so every generalised eigenspace decomposes into a direct sum of Jordan blocks
E_{\lambda}=\bigoplus_{b}E_{\lambda,b}
.
But beware that this decomposition is not unique, for if
e_{0}
and
e^{\prime}_{0}
are two linearly independent generalised eigenvectors of rank
k
, then
e_{0}+e^{\prime}_{0}
and
e_{0}-e^{\prime}_{0}
are also generalised eigenvectors of rank
k
.
The vectors
e_{0}
and
e^{\prime}_{0}
generate two linearly independent Jordan chains spanning a pair of Jordan blocks
E_{\lambda,b}
and
E_{\lambda,b^{\prime}}
.
So do the vectors
e_{0}+e^{\prime}_{0}
and
e_{0}-e^{\prime}_{0}
thus giving another pair of disjoint Jordan blocks
E^{\prime}_{\lambda,b}
and
E^{\prime}_{\lambda,b^{\prime}}
.
But
E_{\lambda,b}\oplus E_{\lambda,b^{\prime}}=E^{\prime}_{\lambda,b}\oplus E^{\prime}_{\lambda,b^{\prime}}
.

The length
l
in (
B.1
) is the size of the largest Jordan block in
E_{\lambda}
, whilst the dimension of each
E^{k}_{\lambda}
is the number of Jordan blocks of size
\geq k
.
Thus, the dimension of
E
is partitioned by the dimensions of its generalised eigenspaces, and the dimension of each generalised eigenspace is further partitioned by the sizes of the Jordan blocks in that generalised eigenspace.
Together, these partitions are known as the
Jordan type
of
\phi
.
More specifically, let
\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{d}
be the distinct eigenvalues of
\phi
with respective multiplicities
m_{1},\ldots,m_{d}
.
For each
i=1,\ldots,d
, let
n_{i}
be the total number of Jordan blocks in the generalised
\lambda_{i}
-eigenspace and let
r_{i1}\geq r_{i2}\geq\cdots\geq r_{in_{i}}
be their lengths.
They define a partition
\bm{m}_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=(m_{i}=r_{i1}+\ldots+r_{in_{i}})
.
Then the
Jordan type
of
\phi
is the unordered collection of these partitions labelled by the distinct eigenvalues of
\phi
:

\operatorname{Jt}(\phi)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\left\{(\lambda_{1},\bm{m}_{1}),\ldots,(\lambda_{d},\bm{m}_{d})\right\}.

Suppose
E_{\lambda,0}
and
E^{\prime}_{\lambda,0}
are two Jordan blocks of the same dimension in the same generalised eigenspace, spanned by Jordan chains
\{e_{k-1},\ldots,e_{1},e_{0}\}
and
\{e^{\prime}_{k-1},\ldots,e^{\prime}_{1},e^{\prime}_{0}\}
, respectively.
Let
\psi\in\operatorname{\mathsf{Aut}}(E)
be the automorphism that sends
e_{i}\mapsto e^{\prime}_{i}
and vice versa for all
i=0,\ldots,k-1
and leaves all other Jordan blocks untouched.
Then
\psi
commutes with
\phi
because
\psi\phi(e_{i})=\psi(\lambda e_{i}+e_{i+1})=\lambda e^{\prime}_{i}+e^{\prime}_{i+1}=\phi(e^{\prime}_{i})=\phi\psi(e_{i})
for all
i=0,\ldots,k-1
where we have set
e_{k}=e^{\prime}_{k}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=0
for convenience.

Every Jordan block has its own canonical complete flag given by any Jordan chain that spans it:

E_{\lambda,0}^{\bullet}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\Big(0\subset E_{\lambda,0}^{1}\subset\cdots\subset E_{\lambda,0}^{k}=E_{\lambda,0}\Big)\hskip 10.00002pt\text{where}\hskip 10.00002ptE_{\lambda,0}^{j}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\left\langle e_{k-1},\ldots,e_{k-j}\right\rangle.

Meanwhile, the complete flag
F
also restricts to a complete flag on any Jordan block
E_{\lambda,0}
given by intersecting the subspaces of
F
with
E_{\lambda,0}
and deleting all duplicate subspaces.
The point is that the relation
\nu_{\lambda}e_{j}=e_{j+1}
implies
\phi e_{j}=\lambda e_{j}+e_{j+1}
, and this means if
e_{j}\in F_{i}
for some
i
, then we must have
e_{k-1},\ldots,e_{j+1},e_{j}\subset F_{i}
because
F_{i}
is a
\phi
-invariant subspace.
In fact, by linear independence, all the vectors in the Jordan chain generated by
e_{0}
necessarily land in lower and lower levels of the flag
F
; i.e., if
e_{j}\in F_{i}\smallsetminus F_{i-1}
, then we must have
e_{j+1}\in F_{i^{\prime}}\smallsetminus F_{i^{\prime}-1}
for some
i^{\prime}<i
, and
e_{j+2}\in F_{i^{\prime\prime}}\smallsetminus F_{i^{\prime\prime}-1}
for some
i^{\prime\prime}<i^{\prime}
, and so on.
These observations can be summarised as follows.

Lemma B.6
.

For any linear system
(E,\phi)
, the restriction of any
\phi
-invariant flag
F
to any Jordan block
E_{\lambda,0}
must coincide with the canonical flag
E_{\lambda,0}^{\bullet}
.
Moreover, any automorphism
\psi\in\operatorname{\mathsf{Aut}}(E)
that swaps two Jordan chains of the same length within the same generalised eigenspace must necessarily commute with
\phi
.
More precisely, suppose
E_{\lambda,0}
and
E^{\prime}_{\lambda,0}
are any two Jordan blocks of the same dimension spanned by Jordan chains
\{e_{k-1},\ldots,e_{1},e_{0}\}
and
\{e^{\prime}_{k-1},\ldots,e^{\prime}_{1},e^{\prime}_{0}\}
, respectively.
Let
\psi\in\operatorname{\mathsf{Aut}}(E)
be the automorphism that sends
e_{i}\mapsto e^{\prime}_{i}
and vice versa for all
i=0,\ldots,k-1
and is the identity on all other Jordan blocks.
Then
\psi\phi=\phi\psi
.

B.2
Shuffled Jordan Types

To design the complete invariant parameterising the isomorphism classes of
\sf{Fi}_{\phi}(E)
, we review the Young tableau representing the Jordan type of an endomorphism.
Jordan blocks in a generalised eigenspace
E_{\lambda}
are often organised into a
Young tableau
(using the English convention) such as

or

\scriptsize\lambda

\scriptsize\lambda

\scriptsize\lambda

\scriptstyle{11}

\scriptstyle{13}

\scriptstyle{21}

\scriptstyle{22}

\scriptstyle{31}

\scriptstyle{33}

\scriptstyle{41}

where each row represents a Jordan block whose size equals the number of boxes in that row.
Each column represents a subspace
E^{k}_{\lambda}
and the number of boxes in that column equals the dimension of
E^{k}_{\lambda}
.
Every box is labelled by
ij
where
i
is the row number and
j
is the column number.
There is one tableau for every distinct eigenvalue of
\phi
so every box is also labelled by an eigenvalue
\lambda
.
Let us denote the Young tableau of
\phi
by
\operatorname{Yt}(\phi)
.
Thus,
\operatorname{Yt}(\phi)
is a collection of boxes labelled by
(\lambda,ij)
.

For example, if
\phi
has two distinct eigenvalues
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}
with multiplicities
8
and
3
respectively, four Jordan blocks of sizes
3,2,2,1
in
E_{\lambda_{1}}
, and two Jordan blocks of sizes
2,1
in
E_{\lambda_{2}}
, then the Young tableau
\operatorname{Yt}(\phi)=\operatorname{Yt}_{1}(\phi)\sqcup\operatorname{Yt}_{2}(\phi)
of
\phi
has two subtableaux labelled by the eigenvalues
\lambda_{1}
and
\lambda_{2}
which are as follows.

\scriptstyle{\lambda_{1}}

\scriptstyle{\lambda_{1}}

\scriptstyle{\lambda_{1}}

\scriptstyle{11}

\scriptstyle{13}

\scriptstyle{21}

\scriptstyle{22}

\scriptstyle{31}

\scriptstyle{33}

\scriptstyle{41}

\scriptstyle{\lambda_{2}}

\scriptstyle{\lambda_{2}}

\scriptstyle{11}

\scriptstyle{12}

\scriptstyle{21}

When the total number of rows in the tableau is not very large, it is convenient to omit the row-number from the label
ij
and use distinct colours for all the rows.
For example, the above Young tableau can be alternatively displayed as follows.

\scriptstyle{\lambda_{1}}

\scriptstyle{\lambda_{1}}

\scriptstyle{\lambda_{1}}

\scriptstyle{1}

\scriptstyle{3}

\scriptstyle{1}

\scriptstyle{2}

\scriptstyle{1}

\scriptstyle{2}

\scriptstyle{1}

\scriptstyle{\lambda_{2}}

\scriptstyle{\lambda_{2}}

\scriptstyle{1}

\scriptstyle{2}

\scriptstyle{1}

Now we are ready to define the complete invariant parameterising the isomorphism classes of
\sf{Fi}_{\phi}(E)
.
We being with a less formal but more intuitive definition.
First, to
shuffle
the rows of a Young tableau is to combine all boxes into a single row in a way that preserves the order of the boxes that came from the same row.
When all eigenvalues are distinct, this is the same thing as ordering the eigenvalues.
More generally, a shuffle can arrange the indistinct eigenvalues out of order as long as the order coming from each row is maintained.
In addition, we consider two shuffles to be
equivalent
if they only differ by swapping pairs of rows of the same length in the same subtableau, and we call an equivalence class a
shuffled Jordan type
.

For example, some of the many possible ways to shuffle the six rows of the above Young tableau with eleven boxes are as follows.

\scriptstyle{\lambda_{2}}

\scriptstyle{\lambda_{2}}

Notice that the eigenvalue labels
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}
and the colours may occur in any order, but the order of the boxes within each colour is respected.
In contrast, any combination of boxes that contains, say,

\scriptstyle\cdots

\scriptstyle\cdots

\scriptstyle 1

is disallowed.
The top-left and middle-left shuffles are equivalent because they can be obtained from one another by swapping the second and third rows in the
\lambda_{1}
subtableau.
Similarly, the top-right and bottom-right shuffles are equivalent.
In contrast, the top-left and bottom-left shuffles are not equivalent because we disallow swapping rows from the
\lambda_{1}
-subtableau with rows from the
\lambda_{2}
-subtableau.
Obviously, there are too many possible shuffled Jordan types in this example to list them all (in fact,
415\,800
; see
Lemma B.8
below).
For a much smaller example, consider
\phi
in

with just one eigenvalue and two Jordan chains of lengths

and

.

Even more concretely, in

in

with just one Jordan block of size

.
In this case, the Young tableau consists of just one row

.

Notice that, up to relabelling using a different set of eigenvalues, the set of all shuffled Jordan types depends only on the Jordan type of
\phi
.
That is to say, if
\phi
and

have different eigenvalues but the same Jordan type, there is a one-to-one correspondence between the shuffles for
\phi
and the shuffles for
\phi^{\prime}
.

More formally, given
(E,\phi)
, for every distinct eigenvalue
\lambda
, let
R_{\lambda,i}
denote the ordered collection of boxes in row
i
of the
\lambda
-subtableau
\operatorname{Yt}_{\lambda}(\phi)\subset\operatorname{Yt}(\phi)
, let
r_{\lambda,i}
be the number of boxes in
R_{\lambda,i}
, and let
R
be the total union of all the boxes:

R_{\lambda,i}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\Big(\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\hbox{\raise 13.49979pt\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt}\crcr}}\kern 345.4pt}}}\Big)_{j=1}^{r}=\Big(\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\hbox{\raise 13.49979pt\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern
