to8.4pt{\vss\hbox to8.00006pt{\hss$\scriptstyle{i1}$\hss}\vss}\kern-8.4pt\vrule height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\vrule height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-8.4pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt}\crcr}}\kern 345.4pt}}}\,,\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\hbox{\raise 13.49979pt\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to8.4pt{\vss\hbox to8.00006pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\lambda$\hss}\vss}\kern-8.4pt\vrule width=0.0pt,height=8.4pt\kern 0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\vrule width=0.0pt,height=8.4pt\kern 0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-8.4pt\kern 0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\kern 0.39993pt}\nullfont \cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to8.4pt{\vss\hbox to8.00006pt{\hss$\scriptstyle{i2}$\hss}\vss}\kern-8.4pt\vrule height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\vrule height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-8.4pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt}\crcr}}\kern 345.4pt}}}\,,\ldots,\raisebox{-3.0pt}{\hbox{\raise 13.49979pt\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to8.4pt{\vss\hbox to8.00006pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\lambda$\hss}\vss}\kern-8.4pt\vrule width=0.0pt,height=8.4pt\kern 0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\vrule width=0.0pt,height=8.4pt\kern 0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-8.4pt\kern 0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\kern 0.39993pt}\nullfont \cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to8.4pt{\vss\hbox to8.00006pt{\hss$\scriptstyle{ir}$\hss}\vss}\kern-8.4pt\vrule height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\vrule height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-8.4pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt}\crcr}}\kern 345.4pt}}}\Big)\hskip 10.00002pt\text{where}\hskip 10.00002ptr=r_{\lambda,i}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{and}\hskip 20.00003ptR\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\bigcup_{\lambda,i}R_{\lambda,i}.

Definition B.7
.

A
shuffle
of the Young tableau
\operatorname{Yt}(\phi)
is a choice of total order on the set
R
whose restriction to each
R_{\lambda,i}\subset R
coincides with the natural order.
We call two shuffles
equivalent
if one can be obtained from the other by a permutation of two sets
R_{\lambda,i}
and
R_{\lambda,j}
of the same length, or by a sequence of such permutations.
A
shuffled Jordan type
on
(E,\phi)
is an equivalence class
\tau
of shuffles.
Note that if
\phi
has all-distinct eigenvalues, a shuffled Jordan type is the same thing as a total order on the eigenvalues.

We denote the set of shuffles and shuffled Jordan types on
(E,\phi)
by

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathsf{Sh}}\nolimits\big(\operatorname{Yt}(\phi)\big)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\left\{\text{shuffles on $(E,\phi)$}\Big.\right\},

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathsf{ShJt}}\nolimits(\phi)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\left\{\text{shuffled Jordan types on $(E,\phi)$}\Big.\right\}=\mathop{\mathsf{Sh}}\nolimits\big(\operatorname{Yt}(\phi)\big)\big/{\sim}.

To build a shuffled Jordan type for
\phi
, start by drawing
n=\dim E
empty boxes in a row.

\scriptstyle{\lambda_{1}}

\scriptstyle{\lambda_{1}}

\scriptstyle{\lambda_{1}}

\scriptstyle{1}

\scriptstyle{3}

\scriptstyle{1}

\scriptstyle{2}

\scriptstyle{1}

\scriptstyle{2}

\scriptstyle{1}

\scriptstyle{\lambda_{2}}

\scriptstyle{\lambda_{2}}

\scriptstyle{1}

\scriptstyle{2}

\scriptstyle{1}

Pick a row from the Young tableau of
(E,\phi)
, let
r_{1}
be the number of boxes in it, and fill in any
r_{1}
of the
n
empty boxes.

\scriptstyle{\lambda_{1}}

\scriptstyle{\lambda_{1}}

\scriptstyle{\lambda_{1}}

\scriptstyle{1}

\scriptstyle{3}

\scriptstyle{1}

\scriptstyle{2}

\scriptstyle{1}

\scriptstyle{2}

\scriptstyle{1}

\scriptstyle{\lambda_{2}}

\scriptstyle{\lambda_{2}}

\scriptstyle{1}

\scriptstyle{2}

\scriptstyle{1}

\scriptstyle{\lambda_{1}}

\scriptstyle{\lambda_{1}}

\scriptstyle{\lambda_{1}}

Then pick a different row from the Young tableau, let
r_{2}
be the number of boxes in it, and fill in any
r_{2}
of the remaining
n-r_{1}
empty boxes.

\scriptstyle{\lambda_{1}}

\scriptstyle{\lambda_{1}}

\scriptstyle{\lambda_{1}}

\scriptstyle{1}

\scriptstyle{3}

\scriptstyle{1}

\scriptstyle{2}

\scriptstyle{1}

\scriptstyle{2}

\scriptstyle{1}

\scriptstyle{\lambda_{2}}

\scriptstyle{\lambda_{2}}

\scriptstyle{1}

\scriptstyle{2}

\scriptstyle{1}

\scriptstyle{\lambda_{1}}

\scriptstyle{\lambda_{2}}

\scriptstyle{\lambda_{1}}

\scriptstyle{\lambda_{1}}

\scriptstyle{\lambda_{2}}

And so on until we run out of rows in the Young tableau.

\scriptstyle{\lambda_{1}}

\scriptstyle{\lambda_{1}}

\scriptstyle{\lambda_{1}}

\scriptstyle{1}

\scriptstyle{3}

\scriptstyle{1}

\scriptstyle{2}

\scriptstyle{1}

\scriptstyle{2}

\scriptstyle{1}

\scriptstyle{\lambda_{2}}

\scriptstyle{\lambda_{2}}

\scriptstyle{1}

\scriptstyle{2}

\scriptstyle{1}

\scriptstyle{\lambda_{1}}

\scriptstyle{\lambda_{1}}

\scriptstyle{\lambda_{1}}

\scriptstyle{\lambda_{2}}

\scriptstyle{\lambda_{1}}

\scriptstyle{\lambda_{1}}

\scriptstyle{\lambda_{1}}

\scriptstyle{\lambda_{2}}

\scriptstyle{\lambda_{1}}

\scriptstyle{\lambda_{2}}

\scriptstyle{\lambda_{1}}

\scriptstyle{1}

\scriptstyle{2}

We see that the total number of shuffles is nothing but a multinomial coefficient

\big|\mathop{\mathsf{Sh}}\nolimits\big(\operatorname{Yt}(\phi)\big)\big|={n\choose r_{1},r_{2},\ldots,r_{m}}=\frac{n!}{r_{1}!r_{2}!\cdots r_{m}!},

where
m
is the total number of rows in the Young tableau.
To take equivalence of shuffles into account, we must divide the above multinomial coefficient by the total number of allowed permutations of rows within each subtableau.
We summarise this as follows.

Lemma B.8
.

Let
(E,\phi)
be an
n
-dimensional linear system, and suppose that the Young tableau of
\phi
has
m
rows with lengths
r_{1},\ldots,r_{m}
.
For every distinct eigenvalue
\lambda
and every positive integer
r
, let
c_{\lambda,r}
be the number of rows of length
r
in the
\lambda
-subtableau.
Let
\mathnormal{C}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\prod c_{\lambda,r}!
where the product is taken over all distinct eigenvalues
\lambda
and all integers
r\geq 1
.
Then the total number of possible shuffled Jordan types on
(E,\phi)
is

\big|\mathop{\mathsf{Sh}}\nolimits\big(\operatorname{Yt}(\phi)\big)/{\sim}\big|=\frac{1}{\mathnormal{C}}{n\choose r_{1},r_{2},\ldots,r_{m}}.

In the above example with six rows and eleven boxes, the total number of shuffles is
11!/(3!2!2!1!2!1!)=831\,600
.
There is just one pair of rows of length
2
in the first subtableau, so
c_{\lambda_{1},2}=2
and all other numbers
c_{\lambda,r}
are either
1
or
0
.
So
\mathnormal{C}=2!
and therefore the total number of shuffled Jordan types is
415\,800
.

B.3
Shuffled Jordan types from Jordan Basis

The main use of a Young tableau in linear algebra is of course to construct a Jordan basis; i.e., a basis of generalised eigenvectors with respect to which
\phi
becomes a Jordan normal form.
Generalised eigenvectors in a Jordan basis are labelled by boxes of the Young tableau, and so any shuffle determines a total order on a Jordan basis.
Conversely, any permutation of a Jordan basis determines an shuffle so long as it does not scramble amongst themselves the vectors within each Jordan chain.
This leads to the following observation.

Lemma B.9
.

Let
(E,\phi)
be a linear system.
Given a Jordan basis
\beta
for
\phi
, the set of permutations of
\beta
that preserve the order within each Jordan chain are in one-to-one correspondence with the set of shuffles of the Young tableau of
\phi
.

More concretely, given a linear system
(E,\phi)
, choose any Jordan basis
\beta
for
\phi
.
Write it as a union of Jordan bases
\beta_{i}
for each generalised eigenspace
E_{i}
, and furthermore write each
\beta_{i}
as a union of maximal Jordan chains
\beta_{ij}
in
E_{i}
:

\beta=\beta_{1}\cup\cdots\cup\beta_{d}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{where}\hskip 20.00003pt\beta_{i}=\beta_{i1}\cup\cdots\cup\beta_{in_{i}}.

Here,
d
is the number of distinct eigenvalues of
\phi
and
n_{i}
is the total number of Jordan blocks in the generalised
\lambda_{i}
-eigenspace.
Thus,
\beta
determines a decomposition of
E
into Jordan blocks:

E=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{d}E_{i}=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{d}\bigoplus_{j=1}^{n_{i}}E_{ij}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{where}\hskip 10.00002ptE_{ij}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\left\langle\beta_{ij}\right\rangle.

Let
\operatorname{\mathsf{Sym}}(\beta)\cong W\subset G
be the symmetric group on the Jordan basis
\beta
, canonically isomorphic to the subgroup of permutation matrices in
G
; i.e.,
\operatorname{\mathsf{Sym}}(\beta)
is the set of all permutations of the basis vectors in
\beta
.
Any permutation
\tau\in\operatorname{\mathsf{Sym}}(\beta)
gives a new basis
\tau(\beta)
of
E
but which may not be a Jordan basis.
Similarly,
\operatorname{\mathsf{Sym}}(\beta_{ij})
is the symmetric group on each maximal Jordan chain
\beta_{ij}
: any permutation of
\beta_{ij}
is still a basis of the Jordan block
E_{ij}
but it is no longer a Jordan chain if the permutation is nontrivial.
Any permutation
\tau\in\operatorname{\mathsf{Sym}}(\beta)
induces a permutation
\tau_{ij}\in\operatorname{\mathsf{Sym}}(\beta_{ij})
on each Jordan chain
\beta_{ij}
which records how
\tau
reorders the elements of the set
\beta_{ij}
amongst themselves.
Let
\mathop{\mathsf{Sh}}\nolimits(\beta)\subset\operatorname{\mathsf{Sym}}(\beta)
denote the subset of permutations of
\beta
that preserve the order within each Jordan chain:

\mathop{\mathsf{Sh}}\nolimits(\beta)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\left\{\tau\in\operatorname{\mathsf{Sym}}(\beta)~\Big|~\tau_{ij}=1\in\operatorname{\mathsf{Sym}}(\beta_{ij})\hskip 10.00002pt\text{for all $i,j$}\right\}.

Amongst all such permutations, let
\sf{sh}_{0}(\beta)\subset\mathop{\mathsf{Sh}}\nolimits(\beta)
be the subset of permutations which only swap a pair of Jordan chains in the same generalised eigenspace and leave the rest of the Jordan basis unchanged.
Let
\mathop{\mathsf{Sh}}\nolimits_{0}(\beta)\subset\mathop{\mathsf{Sh}}\nolimits(\beta)
be the subset consisting of all finite compositions of elements of
\sf{sh}_{0}(\beta)
.
In symbols,

\displaystyle\sf{sh}_{0}(\beta)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\left\{\tau\in\mathop{\mathsf{Sh}}\nolimits(\beta)~\Big|~\text{$\tau(\beta_{ij})=\beta_{ij^{\prime}}$ and $\tau=1$ on $\beta\smallsetminus(\beta_{ij}{\cup}\beta_{ij^{\prime}})$ for some $i,j,j^{\prime}$}\right\},

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathsf{Sh}}\nolimits_{0}(\beta)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\left\{\tau\in\mathop{\mathsf{Sh}}\nolimits(\beta)~\Big|~\tau=\tau_{k}\circ\cdots\circ\tau_{1}\text{ for some }\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{k}\in\sf{sh}_{0}(\beta)\right\}.

Note that if
\tau\in\sf{sh}_{0}(\beta)
and
\tau(\beta_{ij})=\beta_{ij^{\prime}}
, then
\beta_{ij}
and
\beta_{ij^{\prime}}
necessarily have the same length and
\tau
is an order-preserving bijection.
Finally, we use
\mathop{\mathsf{Sh}}\nolimits_{0}(\beta)
to introduce an equivalence relation on the set
\mathop{\mathsf{Sh}}\nolimits(\beta)
by declaring two permutations of
\beta
to be equivalent if they differ by an element of
\mathop{\mathsf{Sh}}\nolimits_{0}(\beta)
; i.e., for all
\tau,\tau^{\prime}\in\mathop{\mathsf{Sh}}\nolimits(\beta)
,

\tau\sim\tau^{\prime}\hskip 10.00002pt\mathrel{\mathop{:}}\Leftrightarrow\hskip 10.00002pt\tau^{\prime}\circ\tau^{-1}\in\mathop{\mathsf{Sh}}\nolimits_{0}(\beta).

Then we obtain the following sharper version of
Lemma B.9
.

Lemma B.10
.

Let
(E,\phi)
be a linear system, and choose any Jordan basis
\beta
for
\phi
.
Then the sets
\mathop{\mathsf{Sh}}\nolimits(\phi)
and
C(\phi)
of shuffles and shuffled Jordan types on
(E,\phi)
are in one-to-one correspondence with the set
\mathop{\mathsf{Sh}}\nolimits(\beta)
of all permutations of
\beta
that preserve the order within each Jordan chain and the set
\mathop{\mathsf{Sh}}\nolimits(\phi)/\mathop{\mathsf{Sh}}\nolimits_{0}(\phi)
, respectively:

\mathop{\mathsf{Sh}}\nolimits(\phi)\cong\mathop{\mathsf{Sh}}\nolimits(\beta)\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002ptC(\phi)\cong\mathop{\mathsf{Sh}}\nolimits(\beta)/\mathop{\mathsf{Sh}}\nolimits_{0}(\beta).

B.4
Invariant Flags as shuffled Jordan types

The main result of this subsection is the following proposition.

Proposition B.11
.

Let
(E,\phi)
be a linear system.
Any shuffled Jordan type determines a complete
\phi
-invariant flag uniquely up to a
\phi
-equivariant automorphism.
Conversely, any complete
\phi
-invariant flag determines a shuffled Jordan type.
In particular, if two complete
\phi
-invariant flags
F,F^{\prime}
are related by a
\phi
-equivariant automorphism
\psi\in\operatorname{\mathsf{Aut}}(E)
(i.e., such that
\psi(F_{i})=F^{\prime}_{i}
and
\psi\phi=\phi\psi
), then
F,F^{\prime}
determine the same shuffled Jordan type.
In other words, the set
\sf{Fi}_{\phi}(E)/{\sim}
is finite and in bijection with the set of shuffled Jordan types on
(E,\phi)
:

\sf{Fi}_{\phi}(E)/{\sim}\cong\mathop{\mathsf{Jt}}\nolimits_{W}(\phi).

Proof.

Given a shuffled Jordan type, choose any Jordan basis and order it according to any shuffle that represents the given shuffled Jordan type.
The resulting basis
(e_{1},\ldots,e_{n})
defines a
\phi
-invariant flag
F
by
F_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\left\langle e_{1},\ldots,e_{i}\right\rangle
.
Any other choice of Jordan basis and/or shuffle results in a potentially different basis
(e^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,e^{\prime}_{n})
and hence a different complete
\phi
-invariant flag
F^{\prime}
by
F^{\prime}_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\left\langle e^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,e^{\prime}_{i}\right\rangle
.
However, the automorphism
\psi\in\operatorname{\mathsf{Aut}}(E)
obtained as the change-of-basis matrix from
(e_{i})
to
(e^{\prime}_{i})
moves the flag
F
to
F^{\prime}
, and
\psi
is the product of several changes of basis corresponding to the individual swaps of pairs of rows of the same length within the same subtableau.
By
Lemma B.6
, any such change-of-basis matrix commutes with
\phi
, so
\psi\phi=\phi\psi
.

Conversely, suppose
F
is a complete
\phi
-invariant flag.
We will construct a basis of generalised eigenvectors adapted to
F
which is a permutation of a Jordan basis that respects the order within each Jordan chain.
Since
E
decomposes into a direct sum of generalised eigenspaces, it is enough to focus on each individual generalised eigenspace
E_{\lambda}
and construct an ordered basis of generalised eigenvectors adapted to the restriction of
F
.
So, without loss of generality, let us assume that
\phi
has only one eigenvalue
\lambda
so that the whole
n
-dimensional space
E
is the generalised
\lambda
-eigenspace.

Observe that whilst
\phi
satisfies
\phi(F_{i})=F_{i}
for each
i
, its nilpotent part
\nu\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\phi-\lambda\mathrm{id}
satisfies
\nu(F_{i})\subsetneq F_{i}
for each
i
.
Rephrased in more elementary terms, the matrix of
\phi
in any basis adapted to
F
is upper-triangular but the matrix of
\nu
is strictly-upper triangular.
Start by picking any vector
e_{n}\in F_{n}\smallsetminus F_{n-1}
.
It generates a Jordan chain
(\nu^{k-1}e_{n},\nu^{k-2}e_{n},\ldots,\nu e_{n},e_{n})
of some length
k
.
These are linearly independent vectors which must therefore belong to different levels of the flag
F
in the following sense.
For each
i=0,\ldots,k-1
, there is some
n_{i}
such that
e_{n_{i}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\nu^{i}e_{n}\in F_{n_{i}}
yet
e_{n_{i}}\notin F_{n_{i}-1}
.
Note that
0<n_{k-1}<n_{k-2}<\ldots<n_{1}<n_{0}=n
.
As a result, we have constructed generalised eigenvectors
e_{i}\in F_{i}\smallsetminus F_{i-1}
for all
i\in\left\{n_{k-1},n_{k-2},\ldots,n_{1},n_{0}\right\}\subset\left\{1,\ldots,n\right\}
.

Next, let
m<n
be the largest integer not equal to any
n_{i}
.
We repeat the above argument by picking a vector
e_{m}\in F_{m}\smallsetminus F_{m-1}
and using it to generate another Jordan chain
(\nu^{l-1}e_{m},\ldots,\nu e_{m},e_{m})
of some length
l
.
Define
e_{m_{i}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\nu^{i}e_{m}
where
m_{i}
is such that
e_{m_{i}}\in F_{m_{i}}\smallsetminus F_{m_{i}-1}
for all
i=0,\ldots,l-1
.
Again, we have
m_{l-1}<\ldots<m_{1}<m_{0}=m

Since Jordan chains are linearly independent, it follows that all vectors
e_{m_{i}},e_{n_{j}}
are linearly independent, and so none of the
m_{i}
and
n_{j}
are equal.
We have therefore constructed vectors
e_{i}\in F_{i}\smallsetminus F_{i-1}
for all
i\in\left\{m_{l-1},\ldots,m_{0},n_{k-1},\ldots,n_{0}\right\}\subset\left\{1,\ldots,n\right\}
.

We continue this process until we exhaust the set
\left\{1,\ldots,n\right\}
.
This process stops after a finite number of steps, resulting in the desired basis
(e_{1},\ldots,e_{n})
of generalised eigenvectors adapted to
F
.
Furthermore, every vector
e_{i}
is a member of a Jordan chain of vectors belonging to the basis
(e_{1},\ldots,e_{n})
, and each Jordan chain respects the order of this basis.
By
Lemma B.9
, it determines a shuffle and hence a shuffled Jordan type.

Thus, we have a bijection between the set
\sf{Fi}_{\phi}(E)/{\sim}
and the set of shuffled Jordan types
\mathop{\mathsf{Jt}}\nolimits_{W}(\phi)
of
\phi
which is finite by
Lemma B.8
.
∎

B.5
Shuffled Jordan Matrices

A
Jordan matrix
is any block-diagonal matrix whose diagonal blocks are of the form
\lambda\mathds{1}+\mathnormal{N}
where
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}^{\ast}
and
\mathnormal{N}
is the nilpotent matrix that has
1
’s on the superdiagonal and
0
’s elsewhere.
Let
J\subset B
denote the subset of all invertible Jordan matrices.
If
\phi\in\operatorname{\mathsf{Aut}}(E)
is an automorphism of an
n
-dimensional vector space, let
J(\phi)\subset J
denote the subset of all Jordan matrices that have the same Jordan type as
\phi
:

J(\phi)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\left\{\mathnormal{J}\in J~\big|~\operatorname{Jt}(\mathnormal{J})=\operatorname{Jt}(\phi)\right\}.

Recall that
J(\phi)
is a finite set whose cardinality equals the number of all possible permutations of the distinct eigenvalues of
\phi
and of the Jordan blocks for the same eigenvalue.
In particular, if
\phi
has all-distinct eigenvalues, then
|J(\phi)|=n!
.

A
shuffled Jordan matrix
is any upper-triangular matrix of the form
\mathnormal{Z}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\tau\mathnormal{J}\tau^{-1}
where
\mathnormal{J}\in J
and
\tau\in W
.
Given a Jordan matrix
\mathnormal{J}\in J
, in general the conjugate
\mathnormal{Z}
remains upper-triangular only for a subset
W(\mathnormal{J})\subset W
of permutations
\tau
, which we call
(Jordan) shuffles
of
\mathnormal{J}
.
In particular, if
\mathnormal{J}
is diagonal, then
W(\mathnormal{J})=W
; otherwise,
W(\mathnormal{J})\subset W
is a proper subset.
We denote the subset of all shuffled Jordan matrices by
\mathop{\mathsf{Jt}}\nolimits_{W}\subset B
, and the subset of all shuffled Jordan matrices with the same Jordan type as
\phi
by
\mathop{\mathsf{Jt}}\nolimits_{W}(\phi)\subset\mathop{\mathsf{Jt}}\nolimits_{W}
.
In symbols,

\displaystyle W(\mathnormal{J})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\left\{\tau\in W~\Big|~\mathnormal{Z}=\tau\mathnormal{J}\tau^{-1}\in B\right\},

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathsf{Jt}}\nolimits_{W}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\left\{\mathnormal{Z}=\tau\mathnormal{J}\tau^{-1}\in B~\Big|~\mathnormal{J}\in J,~\tau\in W(\mathnormal{J})\right\},

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathsf{Jt}}\nolimits_{W}(\phi)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\left\{\mathnormal{Z}=\tau\mathnormal{J}\tau^{-1}\in B~\Big|~\mathnormal{J}\in J(\phi),~\tau\in W(\mathnormal{J})\right\}.

Equivalently,
\mathop{\mathsf{Jt}}\nolimits_{W}
is the set of all pairs
(\mathnormal{J},\tau)\in J\times W
such that
\mathnormal{J}_{\tau}
is upper-triangular, considered up to equivalence
(\mathnormal{J},\tau)\sim(\mathnormal{J}^{\prime},\tau^{\prime})~\mathrel{\mathop{:}}\Leftrightarrow~\tau\mathnormal{J}\tau^{-1}=\tau^{\prime}\mathnormal{J}^{\prime}\tau^{\prime-1}
:

\mathop{\mathsf{Jt}}\nolimits_{W}\cong\left\{(\mathnormal{J},\tau)\in J{\times}W~\Big|~\tau\mathnormal{J}\tau^{-1}\in B\right\}\big/{\sim}.

Similarly, we define a
generalised shuffled Jordan matrix
(resp.
special-generalised
) to be an upper-triangular matrix of the form
\mathnormal{J}_{\omega}=\omega\mathnormal{J}\omega^{-1}
for a Jordan matrix
\mathnormal{J}\in J
and a generalised permutation matrix
\omega\in N
(resp. special-generalised permutation matrix
\omega\in N_{0}
).
We denote their subsets similarly:

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathsf{Jt}}\nolimits_{N}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\left\{\mathnormal{Z}=\omega\mathnormal{J}\omega^{-1}\in B~\Big|~\mathnormal{J}\in J,~\omega\in N(\mathnormal{J})\right\}\cong\left\{(\mathnormal{J},\omega)\in J{\times}N~\Big|~\omega\mathnormal{J}\omega^{-1}\in B\right\}\big/{\sim},

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathsf{Jt}}\nolimits_{N_{0}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\left\{\mathnormal{Z}=\omega\mathnormal{J}\omega^{-1}\in B~\Big|~\mathnormal{J}\in J,~\omega\in N_{0}(\mathnormal{J})\right\}\cong\left\{(\mathnormal{J},\omega)\in J{\times}N_{0}~\Big|~\omega\mathnormal{J}\omega^{-1}\in B\right\}\big/{\sim}.

The natural surjective map
\operatorname{Jt}:\mathop{\mathsf{Jt}}\nolimits_{W}\to\mathop{\mathsf{Jt}}\nolimits
that extracts the Jordan type of a shuffled Jordan matrix has finite fibres.
In particular, observe that
\mathop{\mathsf{Jt}}\nolimits_{W}(\phi)
is a finite set for any
\phi
; in fact, fixing a Jordan matrix
\mathnormal{J}\in J(\phi)
determines a bijection
\mathop{\mathsf{Jt}}\nolimits_{W}(\phi)\cong W(\mathnormal{J})
.
The cardinality of the fibre is largest over any generic Jordan type (i.e., with all-distinct eigenvalues) where it equals to
n!
(the number of all possible permutations of
n
eigenvalues) and this fibre is (non-canonically) in bijection with
W
.
In contrast, the cardinality of the fibre is smallest over any Jordan type consisting of a single Jordan block of length
n
where it equals
1
.

There is also a well-defined surjective map
\operatorname{diag}:\mathop{\mathsf{Jt}}\nolimits_{W}\to T
that extracts the diagonal part
\operatorname{diag}(\mathnormal{J}_{\tau})\in T
of a shuffled Jordan matrix.
This map has finite fibres too, and in fact it is a bijection onto the dense open subset of
T
consisting of diagonal matrices with all-distinct diagonal entries.
On the other hand, restricting
\operatorname{diag}
over the lower- and lower-dimensional subvarieties of
T
with coincident diagonal entries, we find more and more connected components of
\mathop{\mathsf{Jt}}\nolimits_{W}
, enumerated by the possible Jordan types, and the restriction of
\operatorname{diag}
to each of these components is a bijection onto the given subvariety of
T
.
Consequently,
\mathop{\mathsf{Jt}}\nolimits_{W}
is a stratified smooth algebraic variety with the largest stratum of dimension
n
equal to the dimension of
T
.
This structure is easiest seen in low-dimensional examples.

Example B.12
.

If
n=2
, then the preimage
\mathop{\mathsf{Jt}}\nolimits_{W}^{0}=\operatorname{diag}^{-1}(T_{0})
of the dense open subset
T_{0}\subset T=\left\{\operatorname{diag}(t_{1},t_{2})\right\}\cong(\mathbb{C}^{\times})^{2}
given by
t_{1}\neq t_{2}
consists of the diagonal matrices
\operatorname{diag}(t_{1},t_{2})
with
t_{1}\neq t_{2}
.
The map
\operatorname{diag}
restricts to the obvious bijection
\mathop{\mathsf{Jt}}\nolimits_{W}^{0}\cong T_{0}
.
A cartoon of this situation is depicted in
2(a)
.
On the other hand, the preimage of the
1
-dimensional subvariety
T_{1}\subset T
given by
t_{1}=t_{2}=\mathrel{\mathop{:}}t
has two connected components
\mathop{\mathsf{Jt}}\nolimits_{W}^{10},\mathop{\mathsf{Jt}}\nolimits_{W}^{11}
consisting of all the matrices

\begin{bmatrix}t&0\\
0&t\end{bmatrix}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\begin{bmatrix}t&1\\
0&t\end{bmatrix}.

The map
\operatorname{diag}
restricts to give the obvious bijections
\mathop{\mathsf{Jt}}\nolimits_{W}^{10}\cong T_{1}
and
\mathop{\mathsf{Jt}}\nolimits_{W}^{11}\cong T_{1}
.
Furthermore, just as
T_{1}
is contained in the closure of
T_{0}
, the subset
\mathop{\mathsf{Jt}}\nolimits_{W}^{10}
(but not the subset
\mathop{\mathsf{Jt}}\nolimits_{W}^{11}
) is contained in the closure of
\mathop{\mathsf{Jt}}\nolimits_{W}^{0}
.
This gives a description of
\mathop{\mathsf{Jt}}\nolimits_{W}
as a stratified algebraic variety with the largest stratum
\mathop{\mathsf{Jt}}\nolimits_{W}^{0}
of dimension
2
isomorphic to
T_{0}
, and two
1
-dimensional strata
\mathop{\mathsf{Jt}}\nolimits_{W}^{10},\mathop{\mathsf{Jt}}\nolimits_{W}^{11}
, each isomorphic to
T_{1}
:

\mathop{\mathsf{Jt}}\nolimits_{W}=\mathop{\mathsf{Jt}}\nolimits_{W}^{0}~\amalg~\coprod_{i=0,1}\mathop{\mathsf{Jt}}\nolimits_{W}^{1i}.

(a)

(b)

Figure 2
:

Example B.13
.

If
n=3
, then
T=\left\{\operatorname{diag}(t_{1},t_{2},t_{3})\right\}\cong(\mathbb{C}^{\times})^{3}
has the following relevant subsets; see
2(b)
for a cartoon.
First, there is the dense open subset
T_{0}\cong\left\{t_{i}\neq t_{j}\right\}\subset T
.
Second, there are three
2
-dimensional subsets
T_{1}\cong\left\{t_{2}=t_{3}=\mathrel{\mathop{:}}t\neq t_{1}\right\}
,
T_{2}\cong\left\{t_{1}=t_{3}=\mathrel{\mathop{:}}t\neq t_{2}\right\}
, and
T_{3}\cong\left\{t_{1}=t_{2}=\mathrel{\mathop{:}}t\neq t_{3}\right\}
.
Finally, there is the
1
-dimensional subset
T_{4}\cong\left\{t_{1}=t_{2}=t_{3}\right\}
.
The preimage
\mathop{\mathsf{Jt}}\nolimits_{W}^{0}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\operatorname{diag}^{-1}(T_{0})
consists of the diagonal matrices
\operatorname{diag}(t_{1},t_{2},t_{3})
with
t_{i}\neq t_{j}
, hence
\mathop{\mathsf{Jt}}\nolimits_{W}^{0}\cong T_{0}
.
On the other hand, the preimages of
T_{1},T_{2},T_{3}
have two connected components each.
These preimages
\mathop{\mathsf{Jt}}\nolimits_{W}^{ij}=\operatorname{diag}^{-1}(T_{i})
with
j=0,1
consist respectively of all matrices of the form

\begin{bmatrix}t_{1}&\ast&0\\
0&t&0\\
0&0&t\end{bmatrix}~,\hskip 20.00003pt\begin{bmatrix}t&0&\ast\\
0&t_{2}&0\\
0&0&t\end{bmatrix}~,\hskip 20.00003pt\begin{bmatrix}t&0&0\\
0&t&\ast\\
0&0&t_{3}\end{bmatrix}~,

where
\ast
is
0
or
1
, and
t,t_{i}
have the same restrictions as those defining
T_{1},T_{2},T_{3}
in each case.
Thus, map
\operatorname{diag}
determines a bijection
\mathop{\mathsf{Jt}}\nolimits_{W}^{ij}\cong T_{i}
.
Finally, the preimage of
T_{4}
has five connected components
\mathop{\mathsf{Jt}}\nolimits_{W}^{4j},j=0,\ldots,4
, consisting of the matrices

\begin{bmatrix}t&0&0\\
0&t&0\\
0&0&t\end{bmatrix}~,\hskip 20.00003pt\begin{bmatrix}t&1&0\\
0&t&0\\
0&0&t\end{bmatrix}~,\hskip 20.00003pt\begin{bmatrix}t&0&1\\
0&t&0\\
0&0&t\end{bmatrix}~,\hskip 20.00003pt\begin{bmatrix}t&0&0\\
0&t&1\\
0&0&t\end{bmatrix}~,\hskip 20.00003pt\begin{bmatrix}t&1&0\\
0&t&1\\
0&0&t\end{bmatrix}.

Notice that the matrix with
1
in the
(1,3)
entry is not a Jordan matrix, but it is a permutation of a Jordan matrix with two Jordan blocks of size
1
and
2
.
Again, the map
\operatorname{diag}
restricts to give a bijection
\mathop{\mathsf{Jt}}\nolimits_{W}^{4j}\cong T_{4}
.
Consequently, we obtain a description of
\mathop{\mathsf{Jt}}\nolimits_{W}
as a stratified algebraic variety with the largest stratum
\mathop{\mathsf{Jt}}\nolimits_{W}^{0}
of dimension
3
isomorphic to
T_{0}
, and six
2
-dimensional strata
\mathop{\mathsf{Jt}}\nolimits_{W}^{ij}
pairwise isomorphic to
T_{1},T_{2},T_{3}
, and five
1
-dimensional strata each isomorphic to
T_{4}
:

\mathop{\mathsf{Jt}}\nolimits_{W}=\mathop{\mathsf{Jt}}\nolimits_{W}^{0}~\amalg~\coprod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=1,2,3\\
j=0,1\end{subarray}}\mathop{\mathsf{Jt}}\nolimits_{W}^{ij}~\amalg~\coprod_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=0,\ldots,4\end{subarray}}\mathop{\mathsf{Jt}}\nolimits_{W}^{4j}.

Consequently, we can form the fibre product

\tilde{G}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=G\underset{\hbox{\tiny$\operatorname{Jt}$}}{\times}J_{W}=\left\{(\mathnormal{M},\mathnormal{Z})\in G\times J_{W}~\Big|~\operatorname{Jt}(\mathnormal{M})=\operatorname{Jt}(\mathnormal{Z})\right\}

and conclude that the first projection
\tilde{G}\to G
is a finite ramified covering of degree
n!
.

B.6
B
-Conjugacy Classes in
B

The of
G
-conjugacy classes in
G
is in one-to-one correspondence with the set of Jordan types:
G/G\cong\mathop{\mathsf{Jt}}\nolimits
.
Similarly, we have the following result about conjugacy classes within the Borel subgroup
B\subset G
of upper-triangular matrices.

Proposition B.14
.

The set
B/B
of
B
-conjugacy classes in
B
is in one-to-one correspondence with the set of all shuffled Jordan types
\mathop{\mathsf{Jt}}\nolimits_{W}
in
G
.
Similarly, the sets
B/U
and
B/U
of
U^{\times}
- and
U^{\times}
-conjugacy classes in
B
are in one-to-one correspondence with the sets
\mathop{\mathsf{Jt}}\nolimits_{N}
and
\mathop{\mathsf{Jt}}\nolimits_{N_{0}}
, respectively.

B/B\cong\mathop{\mathsf{Jt}}\nolimits_{W}~,\hskip 20.00003ptB/U\cong\mathop{\mathsf{Jt}}\nolimits_{N}~,\hskip 20.00003ptB/U^{\times}\cong\mathop{\mathsf{Jt}}\nolimits_{N_{0}}.
